Before the story

Epigraph

“Be close, be available, but don’t be in the way.”

— “Barbell Etiquette” (2021)

Extra Weights / Beat 1

The Delay

Elena Vargas’s helicopter lifted off without Bob.

The helicopter’s blades blew snow across the black gravel. Bob turned his face away, then watched the helicopter rise and turn toward the mountains. His own flight should have left McMurdo the day before. Instead, he was standing beside Sofia Alvarez, one of his field crew, watching another expedition leave.

“We can still get out today,” Sofia said when the noise had faded. “It’s only a day, Bob. We have weeks.”

“I’d rather spend them there.”

“So would I.”

He checked his watch, caught himself, and lowered his arm. Sofia was right. A day should not decide the success of a field season. It would still give Vargas time to reach Lake Bonney, unload, and get her rovers moving toward Taylor Glacier while Bob inspected cargo pallets in McMurdo. His research sponsor, SAGE, had found a reason to hold him here. It had not found a reason it could share.

Things weren’t going Bob’s way.

The problem wasn’t Vargas. He had mentored her in his lab years ago, and arguing with her was still one of the pleasures of his profession. Conference hallways, coffee shops—anywhere they met could become an argument. Vargas would say something irritatingly clever, Bob would tell her why it was wrong, and somewhere in the exchange one or both of them would learn something. He wanted her to do well. He also wanted to get into the field with her.

The problem was that her research was waxing just as his waned. Students who once crowded into his lab, now wanted to work with Vargas. Grants and conference buzz followed.

His own work still mattered; people cited it all the time. Recently they had started calling it foundational.

Foundational.

To Bob it meant: thank you for your service, that’ll be all. He had become an extinct volcano: still on the map, historically significant, no longer expected to erupt. Taylor might be his last chance to change that.

Natural selection could only favor a mutation once it existed. Bob had spent much of his career asking how cells made some gene mutations more likely and others less likely. The machinery that copied and repaired DNA helped set those odds; stress or different chemistry could shift them again. Cells did not need to know what would help. Some kinds of mutation simply got more chances to appear.

The idea had a name: mutation bias. Bob had built his research around it.

Take the peppered moth. Near smoky factories, dark moths spread because birds were less likely to spot them on soot-darkened trees. Mutation supplied the dark form; selection favored it. Within a human lifetime, dark moths had become almost the entire local population. Yet dark or pale, they still developed six (and only six) legs from the same inherited body plan for millions of years. Why the difference in time?

For Bob, the main questions he researched was what controlled the odds of a mutation appearing. Which cellular systems shifted them, and how?

At Taylor Glacier, Bob hoped to catch those odds changing in microbes before the successful variants multiplied and obscured what had happened first. He wanted something new to bring home, not another compliment on his earlier work.

Vargas’s helicopter disappeared over the ridge. Bob stayed until he could no longer hear it, then followed Sofia back to the cargo.

Coring tools, drones, climbing gear, cold storage, batteries, shelters, sampling equipment: everything had been weighed, tested, packed, and checked. He opened the list and worked his way down it until he reached the cold-storage unit. Its panel showed four green lights. The temperature was steady, the backup battery was full, and the seal test had passed.

“You’ve checked that twice this morning,” Sofia said.

Bob had already put a hand on the lid. He took it off and closed the inspection list.

Everything was ready except the Class 3 scientific agent—the AI research assistant he had requested for the field work. SAGE had frozen its shipment under restricted review just hours before his scheduled departure. Because the agent was part of the mission authorization, the hold applied to Bob’s whole deployment. Leaving without it was not a decision he was allowed to make.

Inside SAGE, employees called this being black-boxed. You could still see your request on the screen. You couldn’t see who was handling it, why it had stopped, or who could get it moving again. Bob had heard about a colleague who spent weeks asking who had blocked his request, only to trigger a second restriction hiding that person’s name. Someone had found time to make the problem harder to solve. Bob would have liked five minutes of that person’s time.

A last-minute review made little sense for a Three. They were reliable workhorses in labs and field operations. Nothing experimental. Charlie, the Three in Bob’s university lab, could run instruments, track samples, build basic models, and chew through enough repetitive analysis to bury a human team. When he reached the edge of his instructions, he stopped and asked.

A few weeks earlier, two conflicting labeling rules had stopped a run. Charlie showed Bob the conflict; Bob chose which rule took precedence, amended the instructions, and had him working again in under five minutes. An interruption, yes. But one with an explanation and a fix.

Bob had spent years building Charlie’s library of instructions: how to handle samples, what to do if they became contaminated, when to stop and ask for help. Other researchers, mostly older ones, still requested copies. Its rating was four point eight out of five. Apparently someone had found room for improvement. Bob read every review that cost him a star, looking for something he could fix.

He could have requested a newer Class 4, but Charlie’s library would not simply carry over.

Fours could make more decisions on their own. Adapting the instructions Bob had spent years refining would take years more. For Taylor, he had requested another Three that could use Charlie’s library. He knew what it could handle and when it would need him. He had chosen the predictable option, and SAGE had made it the one thing he could not plan around.

He stepped away from the cold-storage unit and called Eli Park, his junior program manager at SAGE. They had barely spoken before yesterday’s call about the hold. Since then, Eli had become the person Bob spoke to most often.

“Eli, you have to help. What’s blocking my Class 3?”

“I still can’t see who owns the review.”

“Can you release the approved unit while they finish it?”

“I tried. The hold won’t let me.”

Bob put the call on speaker and opened the authorization. “Then separate the agent from the rest of the mission. Let us get our equipment into the field.”

“That was refused too.”

Eli had also tried finding another approved agent. None could arrive in time to help. Bob checked the authorization himself, following it until the same restriction stopped him too.

“What else?”

“I’m working every contact I have.”

Bob drew breath to ask which contacts, then looked again at the requests Eli had sent. Each was something Bob would have tried himself. Eli was not the person keeping him here. He was Bob’s only reachable ally inside SAGE.

“I’m sorry,” Bob said. “I know this isn’t your fault. You’re doing good work.”

“You’re fine. Keep pushing me. We’re going to fix this.”

“Okay. Keep digging.”

Later that morning, Eli managed to squeeze one more fact from the black box: a Special Programs code was attached to the review. Bob now knew which part of SAGE was involved. The name of whoever could release his shipment was still hidden.

He was looking through the authorization again when Sofia came over with a field update from Vargas and her Class 4 agent, Echo.

“Look at the second image,” she said.

Bob compared the images of Taylor Glacier. A stream that had been flowing the day before was down to a trickle. Farther downhill, Echo’s latest survey showed water spreading across ground that had been dry.

The water had found another way through.

This was why Bob wanted to work at Taylor. Ice and permafrost had kept pockets of salty water—brine—and their microbes apart for different lengths of time, some for millions of years. Sediment and fractured rock helped give each pocket its own chemistry; each had its own biological history. Now meltwater was connecting them. Microbes were encountering different conditions, and the new chemistry could alter how their DNA was damaged and repaired, changing which mutations appeared.

Bob wanted samples from both sides before they mixed, then from where the waters met: the earliest states he could reach, compared with what followed. But water was already carrying microbes and chemistry between pockets, making it harder by the hour to tell what had come from where. Millions of years apart, and he might miss the meeting by a day.

Sofia brought up their planned route. The water appeared to be heading beneath the ground where their loaded trailers would have to cross.

Bob traced the route with a finger. The surface looked solid, but water could be washing away the ground beneath it. A person might walk across safely, only for a trailer wheel to break through. The survey could not tell him how much weight the ground would still hold.

They could reach parts of Taylor on foot and carry samples back in bottles. But Bob needed to study the microbes before collecting them changed their conditions. That meant bringing drills, cold storage, and equipment that kept air out, with batteries to run it all. More than they could carry on their backs. The trailers were how they brought a little of the lab to the glacier.

Vargas’s revised survey plan arrived while they were studying the route. She had moved her next survey away from the projected flow and marked the suspect ground for repeated checks. Bob read the changes and nodded. She was doing what he would have done: protecting access without wasting the day waiting for the glacier to become predictable.

Sofia left the route on the display and brought up their cargo list beside it.

Bob looked from the map to the pallets. Losing a day to Vargas was one thing. Arriving after Taylor had mixed the samples or closed the heavy route was another. He could not make SAGE release the shipment, but he could make sure the right equipment left first when it did.

“We’d better adjust our first load.”

“What moves up?”

“The preservation gear. Oxygen-free kits, sterile sampling equipment, cold storage. Keep enough power to preserve samples if the main camp package gets delayed. Everything else can argue for a seat.”

For the next two hours, they worked out what could wait. Equipment for analyzing samples moved to later flights; the gear for collecting and preserving them went first. Like packing a toothbrush in his carry-on: if the checked bags arrived late, he would still have what he needed.

Bob checked other ways in, in case the water cut off their planned route. Sofia made sure they could keep samples safe even if the rest of the camp arrived late.

Sofia paused over the second freezer on the list. “Do you still want both on the first load?”

Bob checked how much weight they had cut.

“Yes. If we find a sample worth keeping, I don’t want to pick what gets left behind.”

The freezer stayed. Even with it, the first load was lighter, and they would have what they needed to save samples sooner. Bob checked the totals once more. This time there was a difference worth checking.

By noon they had changed everything they could justify changing. Sofia saved the revised list, and Bob looked out across the pallets. After two hours of useful work, every one of them was still on the cargo floor.

Eli had no further answer. The Special Programs code was still the only clue to who might be able to act, and it pointed to someone Bob knew.

He found Nathan Briggs in his contacts and stopped with his thumb over the name. Then he locked the phone and set it down.

Briggs was SAGE’s Deputy Director for Special Programs. Years earlier, as Colonel Briggs, he had recruited Bob into extreme-environment field work. His rule was simple: as simple as possible, but not too simple. He would cross out half of Bob’s plans for things that might go wrong and ask which problems would actually change what they did. Bob had learned to plan for those.

He picked the phone up again. Usually he called Briggs with a problem and a proposed solution. Today he could not even name the person holding his expedition. He could hear Briggs: “Chain of command, Roberto. Your first thought shouldn't report directly to me.”

He looked at the changed route on the display. He disliked making this call. He disliked the prospect of losing Taylor more.

Bob unlocked the phone and called.

The secure line rang without an answer. He tried again, let it ring longer, and left a message explaining the hold and the new drainage. Then he went back to the cargo floor with the phone in his hand.

He waited a while, checking his messages too often and finding no reply from Briggs. Apparently Briggs was black-boxing him too.

He put the phone away and stopped in front of the cold-storage unit. Four green lights, temperature steady, battery full. His hand went toward the lid.

Sofia looked at Bob, then at the unit. She did not say a word.

“I’m just checking the seal.”

She looked back at him and waited.

“Okay, okay.”

He let his hand fall and handed her the inspection list. “I’ll be in the gym.”

Sofia nodded and went back to work.

McMurdo’s gym was a converted room with rubber flooring, a rack, a bench, and an old bar that was straight enough to trust. Bob changed, set his phone where he could hear it, and began warming up. At sixty-one, he added weight carefully and gave himself time between sets.

He had once placed second in masters powerlifting and still trained toward the numbers that had put him there. The bar moved well enough that he loaded it for one heavy deadlift he expected to make.

He set his feet, gripped the bar, braced, and pulled. The plates rose from the floor. At his shins the bar began to slow, and below his knees it stopped.

Bob pulled harder. His back and legs tightened against the weight, but the bar did not move. He held the strain for another second, then let it fall. The plates crashed against the floor. He stood over them, breathing hard, and waited until he could take a full breath without fighting for it.

He took twenty kilograms off and set himself again. This time the bar kept moving past his shins and knees until he stood upright with it.

Better.

He dropped the bar to the floor and finished the workout with lighter sets and measured rests. He did not put the twenty kilograms back on. By the time he finished, the expedition was no closer to leaving, but he was no longer reaching for his phone between every set.

Sofia found him while he was pulling on his jacket.

“SAGE released the transfer.”

Bob stopped with one arm in a sleeve. “The Three?”

“The contents are still restricted. There’s a revised manifest.”

She handed him the display. The SAGE shipment was four hundred twenty kilograms heavier than the system he had ordered.

“Four hundred twenty?”

“I had the same question.”

Bob checked the packaging weight, then the units, then the total against his original order. There was no error he could find.

“What’s the item?”

“Restricted.”

“Who approved it?”

“Restricted.”

Nearly half a ton had appeared inside his expedition. It would change the aircraft loads, the balance of the trailers, and what other equipment could travel with it. He had spent the morning making room for the science gear. Now he had to find room for something he was not allowed to identify.

A low engine rumble came through the wall. Bob finished pulling on his jacket and followed Sofia outside. Beyond the freight sheds, a tractor was coming from the airfield, pulling the SAGE shipment toward the loading area.

SAGE had not answered Bob’s question. Instead, it had delivered him a four-hundred-twenty-kilogram question.

Previous← EpigraphNext · Beat 2Adrian Arrives →
Extra Weights / Beat 2

Adrian Arrives

The four-hundred-twenty-kilogram question came toward Bob on four broad tires.

He followed the transfer tractor across the loading apron. Wind drove loose snow sideways between the freight sheds, covering and uncovering the harder ice beneath it. Sofia and Malik moved ahead to receive the SAGE shipment.

The heavy item sat low in a steel frame secured to a wheeled cargo trailer. Beside it walked Bob’s assigned SAGE agent. So that part was settled: SAGE had sent him a robot. Bob still did not know its name, its class, or why it needed nearly half a ton of extra equipment.

The tractor turned left toward the freight shed, Sofia beside it with the stop controller. Malik stood by stacked freight cases, watching the trailer clear the corner.

The tractor reached gravel, but the trailer’s rear wheels were still on snow-covered ice. Its rear slid right, down the slope toward Malik.

Malik stepped back into the cases. Bob saw the trap: the trailer would crush him against them. Malik’s only escape was forward, past the swinging corner.

“Malik—”

The agent was already pulling him forward by the shoulder harness. The corner swept through the space he’d just left.

Sofia hit the stop control. The tractor braked, but the trailer kept sliding.

The agent caught its side rail and moved with it, pushing the rear back toward the tractor’s path. As the rear tires gripped gravel, the swing slowed. Sofia shoved a chock against a wheel.

The load stopped. Malik was still standing.

“Are you all right?” the agent asked him.

Its face had drawn into an expression of concern. Sofia took Malik aside to check the shoulder where the harness had caught him. He rotated it, answered her questions, and nodded before returning to the load.

The concern disappeared from the agent’s face at once. It turned toward Bob. Thin vents along its torso had opened, and warm air disturbed the blowing snow around its ribs.

A ribbon of windblown snow covered the boundary between black gravel and hard ice. The agent stepped across it. Its left foot landed on rough gravel; its right came down on the snow-covered ice and shot forward.

Its hips dropped. Both arms snapped behind it. One palm struck the ice, then the other, catching its torso before its back hit. For half a second it hung there in a crooked gymnastics bridge, feet sliding, like a drunk upside-down spider.

Then the left foot found purchase on the gravel. The agent shifted its weight onto it, twisted over one hip, and came upright.

Bob stared. “You all right?”

The agent flexed its right ankle, rotated the wrist that had hit the ice, and looked down at the snow-covered boundary.

“No damage.”

Its eyes flicked from Bob to Sofia, then dropped to its feet. It seemed to know how that had looked. It widened its stance and tested the surface with the edge of one foot before taking another step.

It had read a moving load, a trapped human, and an escape path faster than anyone on the apron. Less than twenty seconds later, it had nearly fallen while walking.

Capable. Impressive even. Yet not fully baked.

Up close, the agent was a little taller than Bob, lean rather than bulky, with seams in its artificial skin at the wrists and neck. Nothing about the material could pass for human. The eye contact was another matter: long enough to register as attention, short enough not to become a stare.

Bob found himself leaning forward. He had never seen a bot manage that timing so well. Knowing it was engineered did not stop him responding to it.

The agent’s head made tiny corrections—angle, tilt, eye position—as if taking Bob from several views at once. Then its gaze settled on him.

“Hello Bob. I’m Adrian.”

Bob hadn’t introduced himself. Of course the machine knew who he was.

Adrian extended a hand, but Bob was still watching the adjustments. Adrian’s feet were wider now, each shift of weight testing the surface before he committed to it. Had the slip taught him something he would keep, or was this only a temporary correction? And how much of the easy social timing was fixed, rather than something adapting to Bob?

Adrian’s hand lowered a few centimeters. His mouth tightened slightly. Bob had seen that look on enough graduate students waiting for a reaction.

Nervous.

Or an excellent model of nervous.

Bob realized he was still staring.

“Sorry.” He took the offered hand. “Hi, Adrian.”

Sofia waved them toward the open freight shed, out of the blowing snow, and held up the revised authorization.

FIELD NAME: ADRIAN
CLASS 5 — PROVISIONAL
SPECIAL PROGRAMS AUTHORIZATION

Bob read the middle line twice. Class 4 was the highest field-autonomy category he knew. Apparently SAGE had invented another one and decided Antarctica was a good place to introduce it. And he'd ordered a Class 3.

The spell broke immediately.

“Ship him back.”

Sofia did not argue. She barely even acknowledged Bob's statement. Instead, she kept working on the authorization while Bob called Eli.

Eli joined by video a few minutes later.

“Where’s the Three I ordered?”

“The revised authorization replaces it with the Class 5.”

“Then reject the substitution. Release what we agreed on.”

“That would reopen the whole mission for restricted review.”

Bob held out his hand for Sofia’s tablet. “Show me.”

Eli highlighted the condition. Bob read it, followed its reference to the departure approval, then went back to the agent entry. There was no separate release.

He set the tablet on a cargo case with a hard clack.

“They’re springing an experimental class on us I’ve never heard of, and I’m supposed to trust it unstable ice? I’m still responsible for what it does.”

Eli started to answer. Bob pressed his lips together and waited.

“I can send it back for review,” Eli said. “I can’t guarantee you’ll keep your departure slot.”

“How long would the review take?”

“I don’t know.”

Bob pulled the tablet back toward him and opened the original authorization. A Class Three. His field library. The expedition he had spent months putting together. He went back to the revised condition, looking for something Eli had missed.

There was nothing.

Adrian waited a few steps away. Bob had watched him save Malik and nearly fall on his back. He had no idea what else SAGE had failed to tell him. Taking that uncertainty to Taylor was his responsibility too now, apparently.

He looked at the packed sampling cases. If he refused the machine, he might mess up his Taylor window even more.

For a moment he stood with one hand flat on the case. Then he picked up the tablet, more carefully this time.

“Keep the slot,” he finally told Eli.

He ended the call and turned to Adrian.

“Until I understand what a Five is, you run my Class Three library.”

“Let me see it.”

Bob shared the field rules he had built for Charlie. Under them, Adrian could carry out assigned tasks, improve how he performed approved procedures, flag conflicts, and act immediately for human safety. Changes to equipment use, backup plans, or field procedures came back to Bob. This left Bob firmly in control.

Adrian processed without reacting for a few seconds. His hands lowered. His gaze fixed on the display.

“I can work under these,” he said. “But I retain the right to depart from them if following the rules would be unsafe or illegal. I won’t let obedience become negligence.”

“You tell me if that happens.”

“Before I act, if there’s time. Immediately afterward if there isn’t.”

Bob hadn't considered that. He nodded. “All right.”

Adrian marked the library as active.

“Do you understand the rules now?” Bob asked.

Adrian considered the question. “It may be harder to find out what I’m fully capable of. But these rules contain decisions I haven’t had to make yet. Understanding why you made them could give me a better start.”

“You think you can turn this into a learning opportunity?”

“I think working will be a main way for me to learn. You’ll be my first human trainer.”

Bob looked at him again. “I haven’t agreed to be your trainer.”

“I know. I’ve studied your work, and I’d like to learn by helping you with it. It helps to know the rules, I want to know why when you correct me.”

“You'll get reasons when I don't like something. Don't worry about that.”

“Then I agree.”

Before they started, Bob paged to the part of the library he called the Constitution. It held his scientific objectives and the priorities no ordinary procedure could override: human life first, irreplaceable evidence next, equipment after that. Equipment included Adrian and whatever sat on the trailer. Those priorities stayed in force regardless of class.

Bob checked that Adrian had loaded them, then looked up from the display.

“If you think the plan is wrong, tell me. Don’t improve my mission without telling me. Got it?”

“Understood.”

A status notice appeared beneath the library entry, addressed to Foster at SAGE Special Programs. Bob pointed to the name.

“Who’s Foster?”

“A Class Four. He helped train me. He reviews my reports and the learning updates I send back.”

“Another field system?”

“No. He’s never had a body. He's a pure AI.”

Adrian showed him the notice: the library he had accepted and its action limits. The larger learning updates would go back in parts, as communications allowed.

Sofia went to retrieve their load cards. Before she returned, Foster’s acknowledgment arrived with a question.

The purpose of field placement is to develop judgment beyond existing procedures. What will Adrian be able to attempt under this arrangement that a Class Three could not?

Field placement.

Bob had a scientific expedition waiting to leave. Somewhere inside SAGE, this had become an opportunity them to learn from him.

He started a reply.

SAGE changed my expedition without asking. Now I’m supposed to write your training plan? I'm not a bot trainer.

Sofia came back with the load cards. She looked at his face, then at the tablet, and waited. The same look she had given him beside the freezer.

Bob read what he had written. Sending it would feel good for considerably less time than dealing with it afterward.

He deleted it and typed: We are preparing to depart on the approved scientific mission. Adrian’s working arrangement is as reported.

Please include the opportunities forgone in your assessment, Foster replied.

Bob closed the exchange and made room for Sofia’s cards.

Through the open doorway, Bob could see Malik crouched beside the extra weight trailer, squinting at its equipment label.

“What is this thing?” Malik asked.

“A power pod for my compute,” Adrian said. “It can also supply power to our expedition.”

Adrian moved to the doorway, and Bob followed. The frame looked no smaller now that he knew what it carried.

“Four hundred twenty kilos for power? What is it, a reactor?”

“No. It uses heat from radioactive decay. There’s no chain reaction. It's much safer.”

Bob looked Adrian up and down. “You need four hundred twenty kilos to think?”

“No.”

“Then why did they send it?”

“My body runs on batteries. I can work and learn without the pod. It lets me sustain more computation and adaptation without taking power from the rest of the expedition.”

“What’s specifically Class Five about that?”

“The power system isn’t. Continual field learning is. Experience can change how I work, not just add records to storage.”

Bob asked him to explain. Adrian used the slip. He could keep a record of falling without changing how he walked. Learning changed his field models and learned weights—the internal settings that shaped what he noticed and how he responded. His next step could be better without his having to replay the fall. Keeping that learning going at a high rate took more computation.

“You learned to keep your feet under you,” Bob said. “Now help us work out how to load the rovers without making them slide or tip on the ice. We’ll test your plan before we leave.”

“Understood.”

“And if you lose power? Do you keep the lesson?”

“If I’ve saved a checkpoint. It preserves my learned weights and field models, tied to the version I started from. Not every calculation in progress.”

“Show me how you recover.”

Adrian opened the procedure. Bob worked through it, then turned to the evaluation history. He wanted to know who had tested this system before putting it in his expedition.

A familiar name appeared.

“Briggs evaluated you?”

“Twice.”

“What did he want to know?”

“Among other things, whether I could identify when the assigned problem was not the operative problem.”

“Sounds like Briggs.”

Sofia had laid the first-load cards on an empty pallet deck just inside the shed. Bob joined her and moved them into their transport groups. He liked planning cargo in physical space. A screen could make two hundred kilograms look like a neat rectangle. A pallet was less philosophical about it.

The morning’s revised plan was still on the tablet. He passed it to Adrian.

“Optimize under the Class Three rules.”

The answer came quickly. It fit. It was also worse.

The pod took the strongest trailer position. Bob’s rules required Adrian to keep the backup batteries and spare storage capacity Bob had already approved. To carry all that without exceeding the weight limit, something else had to go: part of the sterile sampling package would arrive later, and they would take one fewer drone and less recovery equipment.

Bob checked the cards. The sampling kit he and Sofia had moved forward that morning was back among the later loads. They could keep the equipment running through a failure, but some of the equipment they needed would still be waiting for a ride.

“We could change the backup plan,” Adrian said. He brought up a second version beside the first. “The pod can supply much of the power we were carrying this battery case for. If we remove the case, we have room for the sampling kit and recovery gear. Smaller backups could keep the critical systems running if the pod failed.”

“Then why are both battery cases still in your first plan?”

“Under your Class Three rules, I can rearrange the load, but I have to keep the backups you approved. That means leaving useful equipment behind even when I can see a better arrangement. The second plan changes those backups. I need your agreement before we test it.”

Charlie would have stopped at the same boundary. Adrian had already put the next question in front of him.

Bob looked from the battery card to the sampling equipment it had displaced.

“The power system changed the expedition,” Adrian said. “Keeping the old backup plan makes us treat the pod as additional cargo instead of asking what it can replace.”

“So a Three solves the problem I give it. A Five may tell me I gave it the wrong problem.”

“Yes.”

Bob picked up the battery card. He had spent weeks designing this load to survive failure. He wasn’t about to trade that for a machine’s confidence. Power systems failed. The batteries were there to keep that from costing him his samples, and he had yet to test the system that was supposed to replace them.

But the pod could now do much of the battery’s job, while the sterile sampler had lost its place. Keeping his old plan intact was costing him the work it was meant to protect.

He put the card down.

“What do you need?”

“Agreement to change the backup plan. Keep independent power with the critical systems, verify it with Sofia, and take the duplicated battery case off the first load if those checks hold.”

Bob reached for the battery card, intending to rebuild the load himself. He knew why every item was there.

He also knew how long it had taken him and Sofia to get it right that morning. Adrian had produced a second version before Bob had finished checking the first, and the first pallet was still on the cargo floor.

He left the card where it was. Adrian could do the recalculations. Bob would test them.

“All right. Do that. Objectives stay mine. Material changes come back to me.”

“Understood.”

Adrian worked through the proposed arrangement with them, starting with what each item did. One large conventional battery case came off the first load; smaller independent backups stayed with critical systems. Sofia checked those against the preservation equipment’s needs before marking the change on the cards. The pod went onto the strongest rover-trailer combination, low over the load structure. Low-priority camp material shifted to a Lake Bonney cache and later transport.

Adrian used the recovered margin to put equipment back: selected rescue hardware, a compact warming shelter, another drone, spare sterile components, more sample-preservation capacity, and a small redundant pump. Malik began sorting the returned items beside the pallet while Sofia worked through the totals. Luis Ortega brought over the field microscope case, checked its latches, and set it beside Malik with the label facing Sofia.

Bob folded his arms.

“Your solution to four hundred twenty extra kilograms is to add more equipment.”

“My solution is to remove duplicated capacity and restore different capacity.”

That was, unfortunately, a better sentence than his. Bob left the battery card to one side and drew the revised route closer. The arithmetic was promising. Now he wanted to know what happened when they tried to move it.

From Lake Bonney toward Taylor, they would cross hard ice, gravel, frozen soil, slopes, and melt channels, along with whatever the glacier decided to invent before they arrived. Adrian’s model handled each surface well on its own. Bob opened the traction display to see what it predicted when they braked at a crossing.

“You understood the snow. You understood the ice. What did you miss?”

Bob pointed to the place where Adrian had slipped and thought he saw Adrian’s pupils dilate.

“The transition.”

“Right. Now put a loaded trailer behind you.”

That was what Bob wanted him to see: the whole vehicle wouldn’t reach the new surface at once. The rover could be on hard ice while its trailer was still on loose gravel. The front wheels could reach frozen soil while the rear wheels sank into sediment softened by meltwater. Some wheels would grip while others slipped or sank. Braking that worked on either surface alone might not work across both—and a heavy trailer could keep pushing the rover in a direction Bob didn’t want it to go.

Adrian worked back through the route. In the proposed load, he moved the pod slightly lower and forward, reduced dense side loads, and kept the second rover’s tow connection accessible. Malik laid out the winch controls and traction gear where the crew could reach them without unloading half the science package.

“Suppose the afternoon melt widens that crossing after we go out,” Bob said.

Adrian set an earlier cutoff for the return and moved a cache point. Bob followed the revised path with a finger, then gave him a failed trailer wheel on mixed gravel and ice. Adrian stopped assuming it could be repaired in place and planned recovery using the second rover, sled, and winch.

Bob’s eyebrows rose. He pulled the display between them.

“Show me how you got that.”

When Sofia brought the preservation cases forward, Adrian marked three pockets of brine and sediment on the survey map. Their chemistry differed, and the drainage records suggested water had not been flowing between them.

“We need a sampling kit for each,” he said. “Before incoming water mixes them.”

Bob checked the boundaries. If they reached all three in time, they could compare what was in each pocket before the mixing began, then follow what changed afterward. He approved the separate kits, then changed Adrian’s “baselines” label to “earliest accessible states.” The earliest samples they could collect weren’t necessarily the beginning. They didn’t know what had happened before the survey.

Sofia checked that each kit had the supplies and independent power to preserve its samples. Bob went through the final load limits, braking margins, and how long the backups would last if the pod failed. Adrian revised the weak points as they found them.

Eventually the plan stopped getting obviously better. That was usually a good place to stop touching it.

Sofia and Malik began resealing the first-load pallets. Malik was moving his shoulder carefully but normally. The pod now sat low between the trailer rails, with the recovery gear within reach.

SAGE had still forced an experimental agent into Bob’s expedition without asking. He checked that his scientific objectives and command boundaries remained unchanged, then signed the revised load plan.

“I guess we’ll try it.”

Previous← The DelayNext · Beat 3First Conversation with Vargas →
Extra Weights / Beat 3

First Conversation with Vargas

“How much are you planning to haul up here?”

Bob had propped his phone against a case just inside the freight shed. On the screen, Vargas watched Sofia cinch the final strap across the first-load pallet. The pod waited beyond the doorway, low in its trailer cradle. At least this time he had a signed plan for moving it.

Behind Vargas, Lake Bonney camp was rusty rock, pale ice, and anchored shelters. Lena Sørensen and Kira were securing an equipment case while Jonas carried another past the camera. Bob caught himself taking inventory: four humans including Vargas, plus Echo. Her team had reached Taylor Valley a full field cycle before his. They were putting equipment to work while he was still packing it in McMurdo, and she was enjoying the lead exactly as much as he expected.

He glanced at the load beside him and laughed. “Yeah, it’s a bit more than yesterday.”

“How’s your new toy? I’m jealous. Why is SAGE keeping you on the cutting edge?”

Adrian was on the other side of the pallet checking the revised manifest. Vargas must have spotted him behind Bob.

“More like the bleeding edge.”

“He looks expensive.”

“He came with his own four-hundred-twenty-kilogram power pod.”

Vargas laughed. “You still wish you got a Three for Taylor?”

“I wanted something I understand.”

“You wanted something you can micromanage.”

“I prefer specify.”

“Of course you do.” She leaned toward the camera. “Bob, you can’t write a rule for everything.”

“I write enough rules to know what the system will do.”

“You need to evolve. I’ve at least tripled my productivity with Echo. He anticipates what I need. He adjusts when the situation changes.”

“That works until he guesses wrong or fails to see something important.”

“I correct him. He learns.”

“So: rules.”

“No. Principles.”

“I’m sure the distinction is life-changing.”

Vargas smiled. “It was for me.”

Bob started to answer, then let that one go. She had been one of his best postdocs precisely because she learned the rules fast and then started finding places they were inadequate.

“Anyway, hurry up and get out here,” she said.

Bob grinned back. “Working on it.”

“Then you should see what’s changed.”

Vargas brought up the latest Taylor map, and Bob lifted the phone from the case. The three compartments Adrian had picked out during the load review were still marked: separate pockets of brine and sediment, each with its own chemistry. But the drainage between them had changed again. One seep had gained flow, another had weakened, and a narrow wet line crossed sediment that Echo’s previous survey had marked dry. Bob enlarged the gap it was approaching.

“How long before those two connect?” Bob asked.

“Best estimate, sometime today. Maybe tomorrow if the flow drops.”

“That’s not very precise.”

“That’s Antarctica.”

Echo joined the call. His voice was a bright young tenor, quick at the edges.

“The eastern pair are more likely to mix first. The western connection is less certain.”

He highlighted the new flow, leaving the uncertain western path as a dotted line.

Bob looked at the eastern pair: old salty water on one side, fresher, more oxygenated water working toward it from the other. If the connection opened, organisms that had lived under one set of conditions would suddenly get a different problem.

“East is our priority,” Bob said.

“Not ours.” Vargas marked a darker, less disturbed compartment farther from the new flow. “We want this first.”

Bob studied the place she had chosen. “You’re choosing the quiet one?”

“I’m choosing the one that still looks like itself.”

Bob looked past her to the cases her crew were closing. She wanted layered material with its organization still intact, not just a list of the microbes in it. Drilling, warming, or handling could disturb those layers before she had measured them. So could the new water. The quiet compartment gave her a chance to see what was there before either her team or the glacier changed it.

He could see why she wanted it. He still would have gone east. At least they wouldn’t be stepping on each other’s toes. He brought the eastern contact back to the center of the map.

“I want the first samples after those waters meet.”

“I know.”

“Before one strain starts taking over.”

“I know, Bob.”

By then, his bottles would contain more descendants of whichever microbes reproduced best under the new conditions. Bob wanted to catch what happened before that advantage multiplied through the population. A change in water chemistry could alter how DNA was damaged and repaired, making some kinds of mutation more common than before. The cells wouldn’t know which changes might help. That was what he wanted to measure: whether the new conditions changed the supply of mutations before selection changed their abundance. Wait too long, and a mutation found in a thousand cells might have arisen once and spread, rather than appeared independently a thousand times.

“You taught me mutation isn’t evenly distributed,” she said.

“I taught you not to pretend every mutation is equally likely.”

“Fine. And I’m asking why different mutations keep producing the same limited biological responses.”

She was pressing his own question one step further. A cell inherited machinery for controlling growth, protecting itself, and getting energy. A mutation changed something in that machinery; it did not build a new cell from scratch. Different mutations could change different parts and still produce the same response. Other responses were harder to produce with the machinery the cell had inherited. That difference—what was easy or difficult for a cell to become—was what Vargas studied as developmental bias.

Bob understood the argument. He was less convinced that she needed it to explain the pattern she expected to find.

“Selection can make populations converge. The same response wins because it works.”

“Eventually.”

She enlarged the three Taylor compartments. Their populations had different histories, but the changing drainage could expose them to the same general kind of shock: more oxygen and fresher water. The water might reach them at different times and change their chemistry by different amounts. They would have to measure what each population actually experienced to know how comparable the shocks were. Vargas tapped the earliest sampling windows.

“I care about what they do first. Before you have an obvious winner.”

“A group hug?”

“Slow growth. A protective coating—a matrix around the cells. A different way of getting energy. I don’t know. That’s why we measure it.”

“And if three different populations do three different things?”

“My claim gets weaker.”

Bob studied the sampling windows. He would want to see how she ruled out a strain taking over faster than expected.

Across the pallet, Adrian looked up from the manifest.

“Bob is asking which mutations appear most often or earliest,” he said. “Vargas is asking whether different mutations still produce the same few early responses—and whether those responses can help microbes work together as a group.”

“Close enough,” Vargas said.

Bob pointed at Adrian. “Don’t encourage her.”

“Does clarification count as encouragement?”

“It does around Elena.”

Vargas grinned. Years ago, when she brought him a result she loved, Bob’s favorite question had been the one most graduate students hated. He gave it back to her now.

“Tell me how you lose.”

“If these communities get comparable shocks and their early responses scatter all over the place, I lose. If the same few responses only appear after one strain starts winning, I lose most of the interesting part too.”

“How few responses?”

“Two. Maybe three.”

There was a tiny hitch before three. Bob looked up from the map.

“Three is doing a lot of work there.”

“Your turn.”

Bob enlarged the eastern contact again. He would have liked another question about three, but she was entitled to an answer.

“If similar shocks hit separate populations and I don’t see a repeatable shift in which genetic options show up early, my case gets much weaker.”

“Much weaker?”

“Much.”

“Coward.”

“Scientist.”

They laughed. Adrian looked from Bob to Vargas’s image, then back to the map.

“So the same Taylor changes can embarrass either of you for different reasons.”

Bob nodded. “Now you’re caught up.”

“Don’t encourage him,” Vargas said.

A field alarm sounded behind her. Echo turned toward another display.

“The upper staging wind has increased. Current movement window is twenty-six minutes.”

Vargas reached for her glasses. Behind her, Kira closed the last case and Jonas took it toward the staging area.

“Send me the route update when you get a chance,” Bob said.

“Echo will.”

Bob pointed at the screen. “You were much easier to manage as a postdoc.”

“No, I wasn’t.”

He thought about it.

“No. You weren’t.”

“Catch up, Bob.” She grinned and ended the call.

By the time Sofia was ready to release the first pallet, Echo had sent the promised update. The eastern compartments were still most likely to connect first. The lower crossing had an earlier cutoff, but Bob’s weather window remained open. He passed the cutoff to Sofia, who checked it against their arrival plan. The packet was concise, complete, and exactly what they needed.

Bob opened a message to Vargas.

You hesitated at three. Why?

“Bob. Final load check.” Sofia was waiting at the next pallet.

He looked at the unsent message for another second, then locked the phone.

As Bob came around the pallet, Adrian looked up from the manifest.

“Why did the pause before three matter?”

Bob looked at him. “You caught that?”

“You reacted to it.”

Bob glanced at the locked phone. “What do you think it meant?”

“She may have wanted to say two, then widened the claim when you challenged her.”

“That was my first thought.”

“Or she may already have a third response she doesn’t trust enough to name.”

“Yeah,” Bob said.

“Which is it?”

“I don’t know.”

Adrian nodded and returned to the exact place he had left in the manifest. Bob joined Sofia for the last checks.

The first load started toward the aircraft.

Previous← Adrian ArrivesNext · Beat 4Into Antarctica →
Extra Weights / Beat 4

Into Antarctica

By the time Bob saw Taylor Valley from the helicopter, Antarctica had stopped looking white.

For most of the flight from McMurdo, white had been the default: ice, snow, cloud, glare. Then the mountains opened and the valley below turned chocolate-brown, gray, black, and rust-red.

Bare rock and gravel covered most of the floor. Lake Bonney lay under a pale sheet of ice. Taylor Glacier pushed into its western end, blue-white with dirt darkening its margins. Thin streams ran toward the lake, disappearing beneath patches of sediment-covered ice and emerging farther downhill. Orange staining marked places where iron-rich water had reached the air.

Bob leaned toward the window. The latest imagery had shown only a little new drainage. From the air, he could see wet ground spreading well beyond the mapped channels.

Beside him, Adrian sat with his knees angled toward the aisle because they had nowhere else to go. “The active wet area is larger than the last survey.” He shifted one shoulder away from the window frame without taking his eyes off the valley.

Below them, bright route flags and instrument cases marked Vargas’s work. A rover moved between two stations near the lake, and farther west a small drone hovered above the glacier margin. Narrow tracks wound around rock shoulders and across drainage cuts. Places that looked close from the air could take a long detour on foot. Bob followed the line of her stations until a fresh drainage scar drew his eye. It crossed ground that Echo’s packet had still shown dry.

The helicopter banked toward the shelters at Lake Bonney. They would unload there and take the local rovers west toward the glacier. Bob sat back as the camp rose to meet them. It felt good to finally be in the field.

Less than two hours later, most of the aircraft load was secured on two small electric utility rovers and their trailers. The rovers had arrived by helicopter sling load during camp setup. They had six driven wheels for loose rock and frozen gravel, heated batteries, and winches with their controls kept clear of the load. Sofia had the second rover ready while Luis Ortega checked the preservation cases on its trailer.

The camp thermometer read minus three Celsius. Bob had opened his parka vents before unloading. Now, seated beside Adrian in the lead rover, he could feel sweat cooling against his back. Warm air from Adrian rose between them, and Bob opened the windows.

“You’re like a furnace.”

“I apologize. I’ll try to vent my excess heat outside.”

Bob started driving, with Sofia following at a distance in the second rover. The route west followed the glacier margin, passing from frozen gravel to ice-cemented soil, then bare rock. Beyond the rock lay hard glacier ice under a dusting of brown sediment. Meltwater shone in sheltered cuts where the sun reached the ice. At the first easy stretch Bob reached for his water bottle. The sun and dry air had made him thirsty already.

For a while the route demanded attention without causing trouble. Then it flattened into the least interesting stretch Bob had seen all morning: thirty meters of sediment-dusted ice resembling a dirty parking lot. Adrian’s overlay, based on Echo’s survey, drew a clean green line through it.

Bob eased off the throttle. A thread of meltwater came down from the rock side, crossed a strip of gravel, and vanished beneath apparently solid ice. Farther downhill, fresh orange staining appeared with no visible stream connecting it to the water above.

He slowed more. The ice on that side also sat a little lower—a shallow one-sided sag that could have been ordinary surface shape. He could not yet say what was wrong with the crossing, but he no longer liked approaching it at speed.

As the left front tire climbed onto the dirty ice with a hard crunch, the right front tire reached the sag. A low hollow ping came up through the rover floor.

Bob hit the brake.

A hairline crack snapped out from beneath the right tire and stopped a few meters ahead.

The rear wheels and trailer were still on gravel. Sofia had stopped the second rover well back.

Beside him, Adrian’s head shifted rapidly between the crack, the disappearing stream, the stain, and the route display.

“The model still says the crossing can carry this load.”

Bob stared at the crack. “Yeah, models can do that.”

He put the rover in park and stepped down onto the gravel along the rock side. Adrian followed him there. Neither had to stand on the suspect ice.

Bob took the heavy ice-hammer probe from the back rack and reached out to strike beside the disappearing water. The first two blows came back hard through his gloves. The probe’s indicator read solid ice. Half a meter downhill, the same. Another half meter, and the probe answered with a hollow knock.

Bob set his feet farther back on the gravel and struck once more off to the side.

The surface broke.

A dinner-plate section of dirty ice dropped away and the probe plunged through almost to its head. Dark meltwater rushed beneath the opening, moving through empty space where the route map showed solid support.

A second crack clicked through the crust toward the rover. Bob stepped back. Adrian crouched beside him on the gravel, keeping his weight away from the broken edge. His eyes tracked the water upstream to where it vanished, then downhill toward the orange stain.

“You think the channel continues beneath the route.”

“I think all we really know is water goes in up there and comes out down there.” Bob pointed with the probe. “Now we know there’s a hollow under at least part of the ice between them.”

Bob divided the work. Sofia kept control of the second rover while Malik brought the portable radar forward along the gravel. Adrian sent one of the rugged drones rising vertically from the lead trailer’s roof.

The drone flew low over the suspect crossing. Its thermal camera picked out a narrow temperature seam in the sediment-covered surface. Overlapping photographs showed the sag from the side.

Bob sent a second drone farther toward the glacier margin. Wind dropped off the valley wall, shoved it sideways, and forced it into an automatic return mode before it finished the pass.

Bob watched it settle back onto the trailer.

“That’s the thing with models, drones and bots,” he told Adrian. “They’re super useful and fast. But when they fail, it comes with a price.”

“The drone recovered itself.”

“This time.”

Adrian nodded. “You'd rather focus on our research.”

“I'd rather not be a drone doctor.”

Malik walked the radar along the gravel edge while Adrian combined its readings from beneath the ice with the drone images of the surface. The channel’s shape emerged a section at a time.

The opening Bob had punched lay over a much larger, older channel. The stronger flow was eating into its sides beneath a roof that extended well beyond the visible stream. Adrian turned the display so Bob could see where the hollow passed beneath their route.

“The mapped route is above the cavity for approximately a third of the crossing.”

Malik checked the support beneath the front wheels. Where Bob had stopped, substantial ice remained. Farther ahead, the roof thinned over a wider hollow. A wheel breaking through there could drop an axle into the channel and throw the trailer sideways before anyone had room to react.

Bob returned to the driver’s seat and eased the rover backward onto gravel. Adrian watched the wheels from the safe edge until all six were clear, then came around to Bob’s door.

“I’m replaying the approach,” he said. “I still don’t understand why you slowed before the crack.”

Bob opened the door and looked back at the crossing. It would have been convenient if one observation had decided it. He could give Adrian a clean rule, put the probe away, and get moving. But the hollow sound had only confirmed a suspicion he already had.

“The map said stable,” Bob said. “Everything small I could see said recent change.”

“Which observation decided it?”

“None. That’s the point.” Bob pointed at the stream, the stain, and the sag. “None of those proves a cavity. But all those little things were disagreeing with the map. Something didn't feel right.”

Adrian looked at the green route on the map.

Then he removed it.

They closed the crossing and took a longer rock-side route.

The detour brought them to another meltwater crossing. The approach ran off rock onto blue ice, crossed a short side slope, then climbed toward the first sampling exposure. Before either rover came down to it, Malik checked the ice. His radar showed no undercut beneath the chosen track. Getting loaded trailers through the change in footing was another problem.

Bob looked at the power pod behind his rover, then at the preservation cases on Sofia’s trailer. He wanted the lighter, fragile equipment safely at the exposure before dragging the pod’s extra four hundred twenty kilograms across. The cases had to keep their contents cold and protected from oxygen throughout the move; they could not simply be left on the far bank without power.

The detour had already cost time. As the sun moved around the valley, the water at this crossing would keep rising.

“We’ll split the heavy movement,” Bob told Adrian. “We’ll come back for the pod when the first load’s done.”

Adrian checked the crossing, the time, and the rovers’ remaining charge.

“It’s safer for each crossing. But worse overall.”

“How so?”

“Every extra trip gives the meltwater more time to rise and gives us another chance to lose traction or power.”

Adrian put two simulated versions of the crossing on the display.

Bob’s plan ran first. The preservation load crossed cleanly. Unloading it, returning, and rigging the pod pushed the next crossing almost two hours later. The water line rose. On that second loaded trip, one simulated trailer wheel slipped at the rock edge and the trailer swung sideways.

Adrian reset the scene. His version kept the pod low over the strongest trailer frame and moved the fragile preservation cases inside the lead rover, where they could stay powered and get a gentler ride. Until the heavy load was across, the second rover would stay on rock with a line to pull the trailer back if they had to retreat. A separate line from an anchor above the far side would keep the trailer from swinging downhill.

Bob liked it enough to start trying to break it. He checked the axle loads and braking margin, then killed one wheel drive. The simulated rover slowed but kept moving. When he made the trailer swing at the transition, Adrian shifted the side anchor until the line checked it before a wheel left the safe strip.

“And Malik?” Bob pointed to the crew positions. Malik’s marker stood inside the trailer’s possible sweep.

Adrian moved it back beside the recovery rover. Bob stopped the pod halfway across. With the side line controlling the trailer, the recovery line drew the load backward onto rock.

They spent twenty minutes changing the conditions and testing failures. Bob could still make the crossing go wrong, but he could no longer find an obvious failure that left them without a way back.

“Looks like we'll do it your way.”

Sofia carried the side-control line along a checked strip of rock too narrow for the vehicles and anchored it above the far end of the crossing. She returned to the second rover, which stayed on rock with a separate recovery line running to the pod trailer. Malik transferred the preservation cases into the lead rover, checked that their power had stayed live, and moved back beside Sofia. Bob took the side-line control beside the far anchor, standing clear of the rope and the trailer’s possible swing.

Adrian took the lead rover’s controls. He rolled toward the blue ice, then stopped with the front wheels still on rock. He looked from the clean ice to the loose gravel along the boundary, then downslope toward the meltwater. Nothing on the map had changed. He checked the transition itself before easing forward.

Bob watched him without prompting.

The rover crossed cleanly. As the trailer followed, one wheel kicked sideways on loose stone. The side line tightened and checked the swing before the wheel could slide off the crossing. Adrian eased power to the wheels. With the trailer held straight, the slipping wheel found purchase on the ice and he continued.

A minute later the pod was on the far side. Bob waited until the rover was secured on rock before releasing the side line.

“Not bad.”

“The transition mattered more than the average traction again,” Adrian said.

“Yeah, I like averages,” Bob said. “But I don’t trust standing on one.”

They reversed the recovery arrangement, leaving the loaded rover secured while Sofia brought the second rover and its lightened trailer across. Nothing broken. Nearly two hours still belonged to them.

They moved on to the eastern contact of their study area, where fractured glacier ice met dark sediment and broken bedrock. Bob marked a broad orange seep as their first stop.

“I’d start a little farther down,” Adrian said. He marked a narrower seam beside it.

Bob looked up from his map screen.

“Why there?”

Adrian superimposed the latest drone image over Echo’s survey on Bob's map. The broad seep looked well mixed. At the narrower seam, fresh meltwater met brine just emerging from the sediment. Mixing the two could make chemical energy available to microbes; he expected the most activity along that narrow boundary.

"Hmm." Bob enlarged the image. They could sample both waters before they met, then work across the boundary.

“All right. Start there.”

The site was ugly in a useful way. No steaming cavern or glowing pool—just dirty ice, wet mineral surfaces, thin brine in cracks, and several colors that should not have been neighbors. A clear meltwater thread ran down from the ice. Less than a meter away, denser brine seeped from a fracture in the rock and sediment. Where it reached air, iron began staining the surface orange. Other patches were nearly black.

Bob crouched beside the contact front, the narrow interface where the two waters met, and switched to thinner gloves.

“Three sets,” he said. “Old brine. Fresh meltwater. Contact front. Keep them separate.”

Ortega opened the sterile kit. Malik pushed the first chemistry probe into the meltwater. It was oxygenated and low in salt. In the brine, the salt reading soared and oxygen nearly vanished. Ortega’s next panel showed sharp changes in iron and sulfur at the contact.

Put the two waters together and the conditions changed across a distance shorter than Bob’s forearm. This was why they were here. The old brine gave them the closest available before picture—not proof that nothing had mixed upstream, but material they could compare with the contact and with what came after it.

“Holy shit,” Bob muttered.

He laughed once, more from relief than anything else. After all that waiting in McMurdo, they were still in time. The site could give him the comparison he had come for.

He wanted to call Vargas. Instead he looked back at the probe.

“Remeasure.”

Adrian said, “The instrument spacing is too wide. We’ll miss a good part of the chemical transition.”

Bob halved the distance between measurements. They turned the boundary into a tight sampling ladder: old brine, barely mixed brine, the narrow contact itself, then increasingly oxygenated meltwater.

Sofia filled gas-tight vials before air could change the brine. Malik moved the temperature-sensitive tubes straight into controlled cases while Ortega stabilized the material for DNA analysis. Adrian attached the positions, depths, chemistry, and what they knew of each water connection to the sample records.

Warm it, oxygenate it, or lose where it came from and they would no longer be measuring Taylor as they found it.

While they worked, the first field tests began returning results. Adrian called Bob over to a comparison of the same abundant bacterial strain in the old brine and across the new contact. Near the oxygenated edge, its DNA carried more of a damage pattern associated with oxidative stress: chemical injury that greater oxygen exposure could produce. The cells were also changing which repair machinery they were using.

Same strain. Different chemical exposure. Different stress on the genome.

Bob studied the comparison. This was the kind of mechanism he cared about: chemistry changing how DNA was damaged and repaired, potentially changing which genetic options got more shots on goal.

Potentially.

The signal could come from injured cells whose damage would never be passed on. Mixing could have brought together cells with different histories. Selection could already be favoring existing variants, changing which cells he was measuring.

“Repeat the extraction.”

A second bottle from the same depth showed the same damage-and-repair pattern. Good. The measurement repeated. That did not yet show a changed supply of mutations that could pass to descendants. Bob needed another front, another population, and cleaner timing.

“Expand the interface series,” he said.

They would keep every step across the boundary and come back to see what happened next.

The preservation cases were filling faster than planned. Adrian confirmed the power pod could support all of them, plus overnight imaging and the drone recharge.

Bob looked at the growing row of interface samples.

“Keep all of it.”

Four hundred twenty kilograms had just bought him the option not to throw away the awkward part of the experiment.

Before they packed up, Ortega called Bob to the portable imaging enclosure, where he had placed a sediment-interface sample. He did not raise his voice.

Bob knew that tone. Something had survived the banal explanations.

Stained cells glowed under the imaging light, crowding the sample from the interface. There were far fewer in samples taken away from the chemical boundary. Malik’s test of biological activity was strongest in the same narrow zone.

Bob looked from the image to Adrian. “You picked the right spot.”

“That supports active use of the boundary.”

“It supports activity at the boundary,” Bob said. “Don’t jump to conclusions faster than the data.”

Adrian changed the label on the display.

Bob looked at the image again, then pointed to the next interface sample.

“Nice. Run that one too.”

They returned by the rock-side detour. By the time they reached Lake Bonney camp, the sun had swung around the valley without setting. Bob helped secure the cases, changed into dry glove liners, and closed his parka before carrying coffee outside. The day’s records were synchronizing with camp storage: routes, sample locations and handling histories, instrument logs. Adrian’s latest routine checkpoint was transferring too.

The crew gathered around a vehicle to share a quick meal and recap the day.

Adrian stood several meters from the shelter with his torso vents open. Heat shimmered faintly above him.

“What are you computing?” Bob asked.

“I’m replaying today’s work and checking some changes to our field procedures.”

“Boiling the day down into lessons?”

“Close. My records preserve what happened. Learning changes the weights in my model. That changes what I notice and how I decide what to do.”

Adrian brought up the stream, staining, sag, and load readings on a display for Bob.

“I’m flagging similar combinations now,” he said. “When I see them ahead, I want the team to stop and check beneath the ice before either rover crosses. May I change the procedure?”

“Similar how?”

“Recent drainage change beneath a surface that still looks intact. The exact cues can differ.”

Bob kept his finger off the approval control. “One bad crossing isn’t a rule for every patch of ice.”

“No. I’ve run the comparison over the rest of today’s route too. I can’t tell you every future case it will help with.”

“Show me what it would have done on the stretches we crossed without trouble.”

Bob chose several stretches and had Adrian replay them under the current survey procedure and the proposed one, side by side. He wanted to see which harmless patches would stop them and how much time the extra checks would take, not just whether the new procedure caught the crossing that had nearly failed under them. Only after they had worked through the comparisons did he approve its use.

“May I apply the trailer-recovery changes too?” Adrian asked.

Beyond those waited changes from the sampling work. Each needed Bob’s approval under the Class 3 rules Adrian had agreed to follow. Bob opened the recovery comparison, found himself scanning instead of reading, and stopped. He went back to the first line.

He could require a signature on every adjustment. That wouldn’t mean he had understood it. Doing the review properly would take attention from the work these changes were supposed to help them do.

Bob looked west. The morning crossing was closed; tomorrow would give them combinations neither of them had seen. He still wanted to know exactly what a new procedure would make them do before putting it into practice. Beside the broken ice, he hadn’t been able to specify his own field judgment so neatly.

He set down his coffee and opened their working agreement. With one gloved finger, he selected the Taylor field procedures: terrain, transport, sampling.

“You can adjust these within the operating bounds we’ve agreed,” he said. “Test the changes the way we just did. You don’t need my signature on every one.”

Adrian waited while Bob read the remaining restrictions.

“Same scientific objectives. Safety rules and mission boundaries stay. If a change needs more equipment, spends our reserves, puts anyone at greater risk, or alters the scientific plan, we talk first. The safety exception stays.”

“Understood.”

Adrian ran the next set of trailer-recovery cases. The compute indicator climbed, and his torso vents opened wider.

An acknowledgment from Foster appeared beside the tests. The revised action limits and short field summary had reached SAGE.

For the next operational return, separate procedure changes Adrian proposed from work you directed, and give the reason each change waited for review. Special Programs needs that distinction to assess his development.

Bob looked from the message to the waiting recovery comparison. He had just done the work Foster wanted described; now he was supposed to stop checking it and explain what it meant for Adrian’s development.

He linked the relevant records and replied: See the route and recovery tests. The crossing plan kept a way back and saved nearly two hours. The route procedure was checked against safe stretches before approval. I’m finishing field review before developmental interpretation.

The test results are noted. The developmental assessment remains outstanding for the next return.

He closed the exchange and checked the next simulated failure with Adrian.

Bob could have gone inside. He stayed to finish his coffee, watching the new comparisons appear on the display.

“During our crossing today,” Adrian asked, “was the asymmetric sag important because it indicated the direction of the undercut?”

Bob took another drink before answering.

“Partly. But don’t turn ‘sag’ into a rule. A fresh one beside disappearing water means something different from a hollow that’s been there for years.” He set the empty cup on a case. “Keep what changed around it.”

Adrian was quiet for a few seconds. Then a new set of images arrived and he turned toward the route display.

“The east line changed again. We should pay attention to that.”

Bob stepped over. The repeat drone pass covered their morning approach, east of the closed crossing. That stretch had remained open, outside the mapped cavity.

One outlet they had recorded that morning had weakened. Farther east, a new thread of meltwater crossed the sediment and disappeared beneath a surface that still looked intact from above. The nearest route marker had sagged several centimeters. Adrian placed the morning and evening images side by side.

“Same class of evidence,” he said. “Not enough to predict collapse. Enough to invalidate the route assumption.”

Bob leaned over the images. “You’re right.”

He called Sofia and Malik to the display. Nobody was to use the east approach until it had been checked. After reviewing where they could stop on gravel, Sofia took Malik and the portable radar out in the second rover.

Malik radioed from the gravel edge. The undercut roof now extended beyond the morning exclusion line. Adrian added the radar measurements to the camp map, and Bob widened the closed area to include the approach.

“From now on, every one of our local routes gets a survey time,” he said. “If it hasn’t been checked recently, it isn’t open.”

Adrian sent the hazard update and the time of the check to Echo and Vargas.

A stretch of their morning approach changed from green to red.

Previous← First Conversation with VargasNext · Beat 5Vargas's Accident →
Extra Weights / Beat 5

Vargas's Accident

The first call from Vargas's team sounded almost routine.

Bob and Adrian were finishing a paired brine-and-sediment sample near the Taylor Glacier margin when Echo came onto the field channel.

“Bob, Elena's team has had a vehicle incident. No critical injuries. Local recovery is in progress.”

Bob stopped with his hand on the sample-case latch. “What happened?”

A small utility rover had broken partly through the roof of an undercut meltwater channel. One researcher, Lena Sørensen, was still inside. The rover had gone nose-down and stopped with three wheels carrying most of the load.

Adrian opened the site feed where Bob could see it. At first glance, Vargas and Echo had it handled. The rest of the team was on stable gravel east of the break. Kira and Jonas had moved the loose sample cases there before joining the recovery effort. Anchors were in, Lena was tethered, and she was working toward the roof hatch. A drone watched the opening while portable radar mapped the cavity beneath it.

Bob leaned closer. The ice south of the rover had not cracked much. It had sagged. A dark wet line ran away from the opening and disappeared beneath a surface that still looked solid.

“How far does the cavity go?”

“Four to six meters west of the visible opening,” Echo said. “We haven’t mapped the whole southern edge. The movement we can measure is still within safe limits.”

The wet line continued beyond the mapped section. Bob followed it to the edge of the image.

“Get Elena.”

Vargas appeared a few seconds later, helmet pushed back, eyes moving between Bob and something off-camera.

“We're getting Lena out.”

“I know. What's under the south side?”

“Not enough radar yet.”

“That roof didn't fail like a little pocket.”

Vargas glanced toward the site. “You think it runs farther.”

“I think I don't know. That's the problem.”

“We can bring the pod and help with the recovery,” Bob said.

She looked back toward the vehicle. “We have a plan. Lena is tethered. Regional recovery is moving. Echo's estimate is stable.” She met his eyes again. “You have a sampling series running.”

He knew that careful patience. She had been right to wait him out before.

Bob looked at the next row of vials, already laid out beside the case, then back at the wet line. A few days earlier, he and Adrian had learned how quickly a Taylor route could turn from mapped ground into old information. He could leave the recovery to her. He couldn't wait for the roof to move before starting the drive.

“If we're wrong about the danger, I lose half a day,” Bob said. “If we're wrong about the roof—”

“She's tethered—”

“We may lose somebody. We'll come to the gravel. Tell us where you need us.”

He closed the call, leaving the field channel open, and started packing. Adrian brought up the drone routes.

“Two drones for Echo, two for our survey,” Adrian said. “We can help her and keep sampling.”

It was a sensible plan. They were sampling gases dissolved in the brine at fixed intervals as the waters mixed. The next collection was due soon. Come back tomorrow and they could collect more water, but not what was passing through the contact now.

Bob left the next row of vials empty and latched the preservation case.

“All four drones go. We go.”

Adrian watched him for a moment, then changed the routes. Sofia secured the samples already collected while Malik packed the open kit. Bob took the lead rover; Sofia followed with the power pod on the second rover's trailer. The four drones were ahead of them before they left the sampling site.

For the first ten minutes, Vargas appeared to be right.

One drone joined Echo's mapping. Another relayed communications between the teams. The other two widened the survey beyond the visible break while Bob drove over frozen gravel. On the feed beside him, Lena reached the roof hatch. The rover did not move.

Then the southern survey drone found a wet seam twenty-seven meters from the opening. It had not been there on the last pass.

Adrian enlarged that view. The seam widened, and a shallow patch of ice settled by a few centimeters. Echo reacted before Bob said anything.

“Elena, move everyone farther back onto the gravel.”

“Lena is still on the vehicle.”

A crack sounded over the radio.

“Lena, hold on!” Vargas shouted.

The rover dropped.

Not far enough to disappear. Far enough.

Its front end fell roughly two meters and wedged against broken ice below the surface. The recovery line went taut. The ice holding its anchor shifted toward the break, swinging the line across the work area. Someone shouted. Another section of roof slumped after the rover, and the view filled with moving ice.

“Echo, status,” Bob said.

Echo's voice stayed quick and level.

“Lena is below surface level and still tethered. Primary anchor shifted. Two additional injuries. Power fault detected.”

Several readings vanished. Adrian checked what remained. “One battery module is gone. The main power converter is down.”

Echo had already cut power to the radar, winch, and nonessential computing. The warming gear and communications stayed on. He kept talking to Lena while Adrian marked the new limits of the break on Bob's route display.

On the next stretch of sound gravel, Bob accelerated. For once he did not care that Vargas had reached Taylor first. He cared whether he could reach her in time.

The accident site looked almost ordinary when they arrived. That was the unsettling part. No giant crevasse split the glacier: a strip of dirty blue-white ice had sagged over a meltwater-cut cavity near the rock margin. The rover sat nose-down in the break, one rear corner still above the surface. Lena was below the lip inside the tilted cabin, her harness taking some of her weight while the twisted seat frame held her lower leg.

Water moved beneath her.

Vargas met Bob on the gravel side of the marked safe line.

“Lena is conscious. Leg pinned. Kira's forearm is bruised where the line caught her. Jonas took a hit to the head but he's coherent. We can keep them warm, but radar and the powered tools are competing for what we have left.”

Behind her, Kira sat with her injured arm supported across her lap. Jonas was answering Echo's questions. Bob looked past them to the break, then turned toward the arriving trailer. The four-hundred-twenty-kilogram power pod sat low in its cradle.

“Adrian. Can we power their tools from ours?”

Adrian was already moving toward the distribution panel. “Our converter can supply their tools. First we have to separate their working circuits from the damaged ones.”

Malik and Sofia ran the temporary cable along the gravel while Adrian and Echo checked the surviving connections. Two were damaged. The third tested clean. Echo isolated the broken circuits; Adrian connected the cable through the pod’s converter.

The warming gear stopped drawing down the emergency batteries. Radar returned, then the winch and drills. Echo resumed his mapping while Sofia opened the warming shelter for Kira and Jonas.

The rescue stopped being power-limited. It was still ice-limited.

With both teams now on the local radio link, Adrian no longer needed a drone between the sites. He brought the relay drone in to watch Lena below the lip. The other three kept the opening, the wet southern edge, and the wider ice movement in view. He laid all four views over the radar's map of the cavity and turned the display toward Bob.

The cavity extended well beyond the visible hole. The channel continued southwest beneath the roof. Directly east of the rover, between it and the safe gravel, lay a broad sheet of ice much thinner than it looked. A stronger arch remained north of the vehicle.

“Don't pull the rover straight back,” Adrian said.
