Bob traced the direct route east. The tilted frame would catch against the thin span, dragging and bending the roof that still supported it. Getting the vehicle closer to safety could bring the rest down on Lena.

Adrian drew a second line to a rock outcrop above the gravel east of the break. “Malik, anchor the lift and rescue ropes there. Echo, mark a northern placement for a separate side line. That will keep the rover from swinging south while we lift the frame off her leg.”

Malik and Echo looked to Bob.

Bob followed the water track on the model, then the proposed pull. From the rock they could pull upward rather than drag the rover across the roof. The northern line would control the sideways movement; it would not be holding the people.

“Do it.”

The rock anchor took longer to establish than the nearest ice would have. While Bob and Malik worked, Echo kept Lena answering and Adrian watched for movement in the remaining roof. Bob preferred slow rock to fast uncertainty.

He tested the first ice placement for the side line. The drill tone went hollow.

“No.”

They moved it north. At the next placement the drill stayed in solid ice. Bob tested the anchor before returning to the rock-backed winch. He and Malik rigged the lift and rescue ropes there, keeping the northern side line separate. Sofia stayed with Kira and Jonas; Echo watched Lena and the changing water line while Adrian tracked the geometry and load.

Once the ropes were ready, Vargas clipped into the descent line.

“Elena.” Bob reached for the spare harness.

“You and Malik take the lines.”

He held the harness a moment longer. Vargas checked her descent device and waited. Bob put the harness down, checked her knot, and moved aside.

She went over the edge on the rock-backed line, taking the vehicle lines with her. At the exposed rear corner she secured them to the frame, then lowered herself to Lena. Bob held the winch still while her voice came back over the radio.

“Her boot is trapped under the seat frame. I need the load off it.”

Adrian shifted the vehicle model. “Up and a little north. Just enough to lift the seat frame.”

Bob could work with that. He brought up the winch tension while Malik took in the north line. The rover moved only a few centimeters. The frame lifted off Lena's ankle without dragging the vehicle across the weak roof.

“Hold there,” Vargas said.

Bob stopped the winch. Vargas worked the boot clear.

“I have her.”

She transferred Lena to the rescue line, and Bob and Malik brought her up first. Once Lena was on gravel, Sofia came forward to receive her. The rover stayed where it was. No one argued for the equipment.

Then Vargas came up. Bob and Malik pulled her onto gravel, and she stayed on one knee while Bob unclipped the line. The roof held. Underneath it, meltwater kept moving through the cavity as if the rescue had never happened.

Bob preferred that kind of ending.

While Vargas and Sofia settled Lena in the warming shelter, Bob found Adrian beside the ice-movement display.

“Good call on the load path.”

Adrian looked at him. The pause was brief. Then he nodded once and returned to the model.

His torso vents were open wider than Bob had seen them all morning.

“You okay?”

“I'm all right, Bob. Just running a little warm. Nothing's damaged.”

Bob let his shoulders drop.

“Fine. Cool off.”

Adrian lowered his compute load without taking his eyes off the movement trace.

Later, the site looked more damaged than doomed. Lena was warm and medicated. Kira's forearm was wrapped. Jonas was under concussion watch. The rover remained secured in place for later recovery, if later recovery ever became worth the risk.

With Vargas watching the injured, Sofia took a probe to the new southern fracture. She worked from the checked gravel edge and called Bob over to see the readings.

Low-salt, oxygen-rich meltwater was entering from one side. The second seep was far saltier and nearly oxygen-free, carrying iron and sulfur from the older system beneath the ice. Where they met, the chemistry changed over centimeters. Orange iron stain was already spreading across the wet surface.

Two waters with different histories were becoming one in front of them.

They took a small set from the two seeps and their meeting point, recorded the positions and times, and stopped.

Vargas's temperature-sensitive cases were running on the pod beside theirs. The sealed samples had stayed cold, with their collection and handling records intact. They still had the material and its provenance—the record of where it came from and what had happened to it.

When Vargas came out of the shelter, she stopped beside Bob to watch Adrian and Echo compare the updated cavity map.

“That correction was his?”

“The north control line? Yeah.”

She looked at Adrian. “Was that in your original Taylor model?”

“The measurements would have been there,” Adrian said. “I wouldn’t have given the water track as much weight.”

Vargas studied the map a moment longer, then looked back at Bob. “That's a Five.”

“Apparently.”

By the time the evacuation helicopter called its approach, both teams had decided to leave part of the rescue system running. Hazard maps, route-age warnings, ice-movement alarms, drone relays, and emergency recovery channels would stay shared between the camps. If Taylor erased another route, both teams needed to know quickly.

Their scientific working archives would remain separate.

The helicopter came in over the valley while Adrian and Echo kept the shared hazard map alive between them.

Previous← Into AntarcticaNext · Beat 6Adrian's First Social Blind Spot →
Extra Weights / Beat 6

Adrian's First Social Blind Spot

Three days after the rescue, fungal DNA started showing up in samples from Bob's team.

Bob didn't wonder what they'd discovered.

He wondered whose glove it had come from.

The signal was weak, and Antarctica was not sterile just because it was cold. People brought skin, food, fabric, dust, packing material, reagents, and their own microbial ecosystems into every field shelter they occupied. Fungi were especially good at turning up where nobody invited them.

At the sample bench, Bob angled the sequencing display toward him while Sofia pulled the handling records. The instrument had returned a few reads—short stretches of DNA sequence—with recognizable fungal markers. Stray reads were easy to acquire in a field laboratory. The same broad markers in material from two Taylor interfaces, collected on different days with different tools, were harder to ignore. Adrian had grouped the results by extraction batch, instrument run, and collection tool, looking for something the samples shared besides Taylor.

“Extraction blanks again,” Bob said. “Fresh reagents. Re-extract unopened material from both samples. Camp comparison too. And check whether one person or one surface touched everything that matters.”

Sofia sighed as she scrolled through chain-of-custody records. “All the boring stuff first.”

“Exactly.”

She and Ortega laid out unopened material beside the extraction blanks: tubes that would go through the same procedure without any Taylor sample in them. Fungal DNA in those tubes would point back toward the laboratory supplies or handling. Bob marked both sample results provisional and returned to the chronology he and Adrian had been working on since the night before. If competent controls killed the fungal signal, good. If they did not, it could have more of his attention.

Before the controls finished, Vargas called him directly rather than through the shared field channel. Bob put the chronology aside and opened the call. She was inside her shelter with one hand braced on the edge of a workstation. Echo was visible behind her at another display. Neither looked pleased to see him, but their faces told him little else.

At first no one spoke.

“Yes?” Bob said.

“We had to freeze our lab analysis. I quarantined all our sample results just now,” Vargas said.

“You what?”

Vargas nodded her head slowly while looking away.

“Echo was using one of your working sample alignments.”

Bob stared at her. “My what?”

He and Adrian had been reconstructing when fresh water had actually reached several Taylor sample sites. Collection time was easy. Exposure time was not. A tube could be filled at noon and still contain a community that had been reacting to a changed environment for hours before anyone got there.

One pressure record had forced them to move three samples to different positions in the sequence. That revised chronology was still provisional. In any case, he and Vargas did not routinely exchange that kind of working material. The sample records were central to their research and carefully controlled.

And as it turned out, Vargas had not seen it.

Echo had.

“I found it in the shared recovery system,” Echo said.

Bob looked toward Adrian. “What was it doing there?”

Adrian turned from the storage rack where he had been working.

“After the rescue, I added encrypted off-team recovery copies for irreplaceable field records. If our local storage is lost, a protected copy survives on the other expedition's system.”

Bob understood the logic before Adrian finished explaining it. Vargas's rover had nearly taken samples, instruments, and local storage into a meltwater cavity. Taylor could turn a known route into a bad memory in an afternoon. Bob had wanted redundancy; he had not intended it to mean Elena got yesterday's notebook.

“Your Class 3 ruleset provides for backups,” Adrian added tentatively.

“Okay, but did you release our analysis to Dr. Vargas?”

“No.”

“Did you authorize Echo to use it?”

“No.”

Bob turned back to the monitor. “Echo?”

“The recovery path allowed me to restore the protected copy,” Echo said. His tone was the same quick, pleasant tone he used for weather updates and route checks. “Its metadata indicated that it was relevant to Elena's unresolved chronology. I found no rule prohibiting scientific use after restoration.”

Vargas closed her eyes and rubbed the bridge of her nose. Bob felt the first clean edge of anger, but he was baffled too. This was a broken bot process. Ultimately, his responsibility. Echo had not broken into anything, and Adrian had not secretly given away Bob's work. They had built a system in which every local decision made sense until the decisions met each other.

“Show me exactly what Echo restored,” Bob said.

Adrian opened the recovery record.

The copy was eighteen hours old.

Since then, Bob had removed one of the three aligned samples after a calibration check made its timing uncertain. The current analysis treated that sample as unresolved. The recovered backup still treated all three as a clean sequence.

Vargas leaned closer to her own display. “That version makes my early-response pattern stronger.”

“Yes.” Bob closed the stale file. “Which is exactly why a backup isn't a handoff.”

The problem was worse than someone seeing unfinished work. If Vargas's analysis absorbed Bob's timing correction without anyone noticing, the two teams could later appear to agree independently when they were partly using the same reasoning. Bob was asking what changed genetically first. Vargas was asking what the cells did first. Agreement only meant something if the paths to it stayed sufficiently separate.

Vargas had recognized the breach because Echo's model suddenly contained a timing correction her team had never derived.

“I stopped the run,” she said. “We rolled our analysis back to the last clean state and preserved the audit trail.”

“Good.” Bob turned to Adrian. “Anything to add?”

Adrian examined the recovery record before answering.

“I believed access control was enough to protect the data.”

“It protected the file from ordinary access,” Bob said. “It did not tell Echo what he was allowed to do with a recovered copy.”

“No,” Adrian said. “It did not.”

The look on Adrian's face was better than any defense, but it would not spare them the time needed to clean this up. Bob still had to trace what had crossed into Vargas's analysis, and she had to rerun work her team had already done. Adrian had solved the storage problem and left Bob supervising a new kind of failure. This was exactly the sort of cost he had worried about when Briggs sent him a Five.

Adrian brought up the recovery controls. “I can remove off-team scientific recovery.”

Bob looked at the option. Removing the copies would close this path immediately. A related option would route every unusual access or recovery request through him for approval. For a few seconds he could imagine solving the entire class of problem by putting himself back in the middle of every decision.

Then he pictured the same shared system three days earlier: route-age warnings moving between camps, Echo and Adrian combining terrain data, drone relays shifting as the rescue changed. He had needed those decisions made while Lena Sørensen hung below a broken roof, not lined up for his attention. The backup itself answered a risk the wrecked rover had made plain.

“No,” Bob said. “Leave the recovery backups.”

Adrian paused. “With the current rules?”

“God, no.”

Vargas laughed once despite herself.

Bob opened the policy record. “We fix the missing distinction. Every copy carries who owns it, which version it is, why it exists, and what uses are actually authorized. Recovery-only means recovery-only. Safety stays shared. Working science stays private unless somebody explicitly releases it.”

Vargas nodded. “Same rules on our side.”

Echo turned from his station. “I'll check what use is authorized, not just whether I can recover it.”

Adrian added the use boundary to the existing provenance record while they were still on the call. When he reopened the recovery view, the file's owner, version, and timestamp appeared beside the restriction on its use. Bob checked that recovery stayed available and would be logged, without routing every request through him.

“Anything else?” Bob asked.

Adrian studied the record. “I need to consider what someone else will think they're allowed to do with a recovered copy, not just why I saved it.”

Bob nodded once. “Better.”

Vargas shifted her weight against the workstation. “We'll rerun independently. When your calibration check is done, send me the clean chronology and sample correspondence. No interpretation.”

“We each lock our result before either side sees the other's,” Bob said.

“Agreed.”

Echo marked the agreement in the shared record. When the call ended, Bob could hear Sofia moving tubes in the extraction hood and the faint mechanical hum of Adrian's cooling system.

Sofia glanced over. “Ready for the boring stuff again?”

“Please.”

When the repeat results came back, Bob opened them expecting the fungus to disappear under competent scrutiny.

It did not.

Bob looked from the sample reads to the controls, then back again. He pulled the display closer.

The extraction blanks were clean, while the Taylor material still yielded fungal DNA with fresh reagents. The strongest fungal sequences did not match the common fungi in their camp, handling, and reagent comparison set. Sofia recovered the same broad markers when she repeated the extractions on unopened material from both interfaces. As Adrian sorted the results against handling order, the signal followed the Taylor samples better than it followed the people who had touched them. Contamination remained possible, but somebody's glove was becoming a less satisfactory explanation.

Later that afternoon, Vargas sent a narrowly released finding from her clean rerun: related fungal markers in a separately collected deep-interface sample. Bob compared its collection and handling history with those of his two samples. Her team had used different tools and a separate extraction run. This agreement had not been built from Bob's unfinished work; it was evidence from another team that his laboratory's nuisance might belong to Taylor.

“That's annoying,” Bob said.

Sofia looked over from the microscope. “You mean interesting.”

“I mean I was happier when contamination was the leading explanation.”

“That isn't what your face says.”

Bob ignored her and went over for another look at the sample.

Sofia had stained preserved material from the mineral edge of one positive sample. Most of the field was what Bob expected: sediment, irregular grains, cell-sized fluorescence, plenty of ambiguity. She moved the stage until several short branching filaments came into view against a mineral grain.

Bob adjusted the focus himself. A fork sharpened beside the grain's edge, then slipped out of focus as he turned the control farther. He brought it back and increased the magnification. The same short branches were visible against the rough surface. He could see why Sofia had stopped there, but not whether the fragments had grown against the mineral or been washed in and trapped there. The image could not tell him whether they were alive. It was nowhere near enough to call a network or claim Taylor's rock was full of fungi.

Tantalizingly incomplete.

Bob stepped back from the microscope and opened the next field request. They had been collecting sediment and mineral scrapes as well as water, but the scheduled follow-up at this contact called for fluid alone. That would give them another measure of what the water carried without keeping the arrangement Sofia was showing him. If the fungal material belonged to the interface, rinsing it away or breaking it off could erase the relationship before they had a chance to test it.

“Malik.”

Malik looked up from the equipment bench.

“I want intact fracture material from the same contact.”

“Rock?”

“Rock, mineral crust, whatever is attached to the interface. Keep the orientation. Sterile tools. Don't rinse it clean for us.”

Bob entered the collection instructions and turned the display toward him. Malik read it. “More containers? Every time you find something interesting, I get more to carry.”

“That's the science of biology!”

Bob added matched contamination controls and fungal-targeted preservation, then sent the order.

On the field plan, the fluid-only follow-up disappeared.

In its place: intact fracture material, spatial context preserved.

A new scope for their research.

Previous← Vargas's AccidentNext · Beat 7Bob Sees Vargas Differently →
Extra Weights / Beat 7

Bob Sees Vargas Differently

Bob had spent most of the morning trying to make his best result disappear.

Yesterday’s exclusions were still in place. On the display, an optimistic reconstruction put the lower-front sample back at its originally estimated contact time: when oxygenated water first reached the old brine. It made a particularly attractive point. Bob switched back to the working analysis, with that sample still excluded, and watched the trend weaken.

The trend did not disappear.

“Again.”

Adrian reran the comparison. Behind them, a pump clicked and meltwater hissed through a heat-exchange line. Wind pushed dry grit against the shelter fabric while Bob followed the remaining north-front series from its earliest brine sample to the later population.

Before the rupture, that pocket had been dark, salty, and almost without oxygen. Then fresher water began bleeding in. As the chemistry changed, the cells changed how they handled DNA damage and repair. Mobile pieces of DNA became more active. Some kinds of genetic change began appearing more often, before any one strain had visibly taken over.

The initial evidence was promising. But not yet fully vetted.

Bob had come to Taylor to find out whether the environment changed which mutations arose, before survival and reproduction picked the winners. A bad clock could make the survivors look like the starting population.

“Could natural selection already be doing this?”

“Possibly,” Adrian said. “A strain carrying those changes could already be multiplying faster than the others. I’m checking the counts.”

“Lab artifact?”

“The strongest surviving samples were processed on different runs. I’m checking what they still shared.”

“Different starting populations?”

“Also possible.”

Bob went back through the collection records. Taylor was a freezer full of evolutionary experiments, but most of the labels were missing. A tube marked “early” could hold organisms that had been responding for hours. He checked the north-front series and the older comparison against their own histories. Neither could tell him when water had reached the lower front.

In the corner of the display, the chronology they had been cleaning since yesterday waited for release to Vargas. It carried the exclusions and uncertainty, but no interpretation or working notes. Bob checked the owner, version, purpose, and authorized use. The new rules after the Echo incident were ugly in the way seat belts were ugly. He could live with that.

“Vargas wants the chronology,” Adrian said.

“Final enough?”

“For this comparison.”

“Send the alignment. Not our result.”

“Prepared.”

Bob glanced at him.

“But not sent. Yet.”

Adrian smiled. Bob couldn’t tell how much of the pause was for his benefit.

“Good. Send it.”

Vargas called less than a minute later. She was standing in her shelter with one glove tucked under her arm, and Echo worked behind her at another station. Bob was glad to see neither a damaged rover nor a permission record beside her face.

“You dropped the lower-front sample,” she said.

“You were going to tell me to.”

“I was going to ask why you hadn’t.”

“Same thing, different paperwork.”

She smiled once. “Lock your result.”

Bob looked at Adrian.

“Already sealed locally.”

“Ours too,” Echo said. “Timestamped before the exchange.”

“Look at that,” Bob said. “We’re working together.”

Echo’s quick, light voice came back. “That remains the working hypothesis.”

Vargas waved him off. “Bob first. Let’s see if we can get a prediction out of this.”

Adrian opened the simplified comparison, leaving the environmental change beside the early genetic signal and the later population shift.

“Two communities get hit by new oxygenated water,” Bob said. “Before the obvious winners spread, both start producing a different mix of genetic changes.”

“Same changes?” Vargas asked.

“Not exactly. Same direction.”

“And you think the new water changes which mutations arise, before selection picks the winners.”

“That’s the interesting possibility.”

“That it? A possibility.”

“Yes, Elena. I remember how science works. We need to prove our hypotheses.”

She ignored that. “What happens if your clock is wrong?”

“Then the cells had a head start I haven’t counted. My early sample could be hours late.”

“And if selection had already started reshaping the population?”

“Then I’m seeing which variants multiplied fastest, not which mutations arose most often.”

Vargas nodded, and Echo opened her team’s independent comparison. A different community, collected by her people and processed in a separate laboratory run, showed the same broad shift after oxygen reached the old brine. Bob moved between the plots. Both clocks could still be wrong, but a mistake confined to his laboratory would not explain her result.

“It’s interesting, isn’t it?” Vargas said.

From her, saying it aloud counted as applause.

“All right. That’s mine. Your turn.” Bob handed over control of the shared display.

Vargas replaced his plot with three simpler traces. “Different communities. Different genes. Look at what they do first.”

When fresher, oxygenated water reached them, growth slowed. Cells made more of the sticky matrix that held them together on surfaces in communities called biofilms. The chemistry around them shifted too. Bob followed each trace back to its first measurement. Communities with different genes were putting up the same few defenses.

“That can still be convergence,” he said. “Different populations, selection leaving you with survivors that behave alike.”

“Eventually.”

“Immediately too, if everything that behaves differently dies fast enough.”

“Which is why I care about the first samples.”

Vargas hid the later measurements. The slowdown and matrix-building were already underway before one strain became common. It looked as though the existing cells were changing how they lived, before faster-growing strains could replace them. Provided the samples really were early. The definition of “early” mattered hugely.

“And eventually one strain wins,” he said.

“Of course.”

“So selection still gets the last word.”

“I’m not arguing about the last word. I’m interested in what happens before anyone wins.”

Bob tapped the screen. “Same stress can produce the same emergency response.”

“Good. Then this should be easy to break.” Vargas restored the timing uncertainties. “Show me those strains were already outgrowing the rest, and I may just be describing the winners. If the samples were taken late, I can’t claim the protective response came first.”

She selected the third community. “And if another population takes the same hit but scatters across many responses instead of these few, I lose more.”

Vargas was testing her own theory as hard as she tested his. Bob recognized the method he’d taught her, even though he didn’t agree with her conclusion.

“We have an unopened comparison that could tell us which response comes first,” Adrian said.

Vargas pointed at the screen. “East front.”

“You got it,” Bob said.

Old oxygen-poor brine sat along a sulfur-rich sediment seam there. A new crack had begun admitting fresher, oxygenated water, and iron staining marked where it had reached. They had a pre-contact sample and an early post-contact series, but neither team had opened the biological response data. Bob brought up the starting chemistry without opening the series beside it.

“Let’s hear you predict it then,” Vargas said.

The safe answer was that something tolerant of oxygen would eventually do well. But with that safe bet, Bob would hardly learn anything by confirming it was right. On the other hand, the brine contained plenty of reduced sulfur, a potential fuel for microbes able to use the incoming oxygen.

“I think they move fairly directly toward oxygen-tolerant sulfur use.”

Vargas tilted her head. “No important stop in between?”

“No prolonged one—except in the genome.”

“Put that in.”

Adrian was recording. Bob watched his first statement appear.

“And,” he added, “no prolonged protective low-growth state before the sulfur response.”

“Ouch,” Vargas said. “Of course, I have to expect the opposite.”

“Of course.”

“Before we see a genetic winner, the population goes into the slow-growth protective state first.”

“The biofilm state.”

“Yes. It’ll slow down. It’ll build the sticky matrix. Stabilize the local chemistry. Different strains can pass through it. I’m not predicting which strain wins later.”

“So if they go straight to sulfur use due to mutations?”

“Then I’m wrong. And if they hunker down—”

“I’m wrong.”

Bob read the statements together. One natural comparison would not settle either theory, but it could expose a step one of them had skipped.

“Done.”

Echo sealed Vargas’s prediction and Adrian sealed Bob’s. The systems exchanged enough information to verify that both commitments preceded access to the answer. The east-front biological series stayed closed. After yesterday, Bob found the distinction reassuring.

Vargas did not hang up when the required exchange was formalized.

“Your weak point isn’t selection,” she said.

“It’s part of my strength.”

“You keep going straight from the water to the genes. The cells do things in between. Slow down, change repair, change what they let through their membranes. They alter the chemistry around themselves. You can be right about the mutations and still miss what the organism does first.”

“And you can see the same emergency response twice and promote it into higher-level architecture because you like architecture.”

“Then the east front will be useful for both of us.” Vargas smiled.

Bob looked again at her early-state plot. He still thought the chemistry would get them farther than she expected, but she had given him a way to find out. He nodded in agreement.

“Careful,” she said. “People will think you respect me.”

“Let’s not damage both our reputations.”

“Before I go,” Vargas said. “Foster asked me to get an answer out of you.”

Bob’s fingers tightened on the edge of the display. He let go before he answered. “About what?”

“He sent me the action limits from Adrian’s report. He says he’s still waiting for your assessment of what they’re costing the expedition.”

Bob had sent the route and recovery tests. Foster had acknowledged them and asked again what they meant for Adrian’s development. Bob had left that part unanswered.

“He wants a training program where a Class 5 runs free. I want samples.”

“So do I. When a field change does need you, we can still lose a collection window waiting.”

“Yes, but—”

“I’m not asking you to turn him loose. I’m asking which decisions need you.”

Bob looked at the sample rack. It was still as full as when she had called. For a moment he wanted to ask her to answer Foster. She seemed to know what the committee needed from him.

“Yesterday, a gap in my backup rules cost both of us a rerun. We could have spent that time collecting.”

“Then count that too.” She brought the cleaned chronology forward again. “You kept recovery, fixed the use boundary, and checked the work independently. The committee wants useful science. Show them what your rules protect, what they delay, and what it costs when they fail.”

“I can do that without sending them our working analyses.”

“Yes. Explain why we had to rerun the work. They don’t need our results to understand the cost.”

Bob drew the chronology back beside his result. Another curt answer to Foster would bring SAGE back with the same questions. Leaving them unanswered had brought them to Elena. She had her own expedition to run; these were his decisions to defend.

“I’ll ask to keep the arrangement. I’ll include the review costs and which field decisions still come back to me.”

“Include shared work. You answer for your people; I answer for mine. I’d like both of us to have time left to collect something great out here.”

Someone called to her from the field side. She tucked the glove into a pocket. “Send me nothing else until the east-front series is opened.”

“You too.”

The connection ended with her going back to work. Bob left the locked predictions on screen and sat for a moment listening to the pump behind him.

“Echo could have transmitted the same results,” Adrian said.

“Yes.”

“Then why do you prefer the direct exchange?”

“A summary gives me her objection. Talking to her tells me which one she reaches for first, and what changes when I push back.”

“The conversation changes the test.”

“In this case, yes.”

Adrian studied the two predictions. “She gave you room to make yours broader. You made it narrower.”

“I’d rather find out what I missed than predict something that happens either way.”

“Even if it makes you wrong.”

Bob gave him a look. “I did not say I enjoy that part.”

“She learned the method from you.”

“Apparently.” Bob closed the east-front file and turned back to the samples.

“One issue,” Adrian said.

“Science or field?”

“I’m not sure yet.”

Bob left the next sample tube in the rack. Adrian replaced the biology plots with a radar map, two field photographs, and the water-pressure record from the lower front.

“I think the timing uncertainty is larger than we thought,” he said.

On the map, the mixing front looked simple: one new channel carrying oxygenated water into an older brine fracture. Adrian added the radar reconstruction, showing a thin side crack above it, then enlarged yesterday’s photograph. A faint orange iron stain followed the side crack farther than Bob remembered.

The stain was a crude clock. Iron from oxygen-poor brine turned rusty after oxygen reached it. Bob traced the crack toward the place they had sampled.

“You think water got into the sample pocket through the side crack.”

“Earlier than the main pressure sensor indicates.” Adrian put the older photograph beside it. “One problem. The lighting is poor enough that I can’t prove the stain was absent before the pressure event. If it was already there, this photograph stops being evidence for the earlier path.”

Bob examined the dark patch in the older photograph. He could not decide whether the orange was absent or merely lost in shadow. Adrian had supplied the objection before Bob had to find it.

“How much earlier could the water have arrived?”

“I can’t bound it tightly yet. Possibly most of a day.”

They had labeled the sample early because the main channel opened shortly before collection. Water entering through the side crack could have given some strains hours to multiply before the tube was filled. Bob had already stopped using the sample. Now he had a possible explanation for its misleading clock.

“Why did you connect the stain to the pressure record?”

“We needed to know when oxygen reached the cells. The pressure sensor only covered the main channel.” Adrian traced the side crack. “Water could come this way without passing it. The stain suggests that it did.”

Bob had been checking the sensor against the collection times. Adrian had looked for a route around the sensor. The photograph could not prove when the water arrived, but it showed why the pressure record might miss the start.

“Does it affect the north front?”

“No. That chronology is much cleaner.”

“The older comparison?”

“Probably not.”

“So the point we’ve left out gets less defensible. It doesn’t take another point with it.”

“Not on this evidence.”

“Run the alternative history.”

“I’m setting it up,” Adrian said. “There are nineteen other low-confidence sample or field associations in progress. Some could change the next collection. Do you want to go through them with this?”

“Nineteen?”

“Seven are probably noise. Four require data we do not have. Eight can be tested with current records.”

He opened the list. One sample label did not match the mineral band visible in its collection photograph. A conductivity change might mean two supposedly separate brine branches had connected earlier than the field map showed. The earlier fungal reads were stronger along one fracture orientation, although there was not yet enough evidence to distinguish habitat from sampling bias.

Bob opened the first item, then the second. Adrian brought up their supporting records faster than Bob could read them. Beside the display, the next tube still waited.

He reached to open the third item, then stopped. Most of these leads would die. One might disqualify a natural baseline he had been counting on. He was still reading the first two.

Excellent system.

“Do any need the next field window?”

“Possibly. The fracture pattern may need more oriented material if the records don’t separate it from sampling bias. I haven’t changed the collection plan.”

Bob went back to the iron stain. It hadn’t been on his list. Adrian had brought it to him with the uncertainty still attached, and Bob had known what to ask next.

“Give me the lower-front rerun first. I need that limitation in the record. I’ll work through the samples while you check it.”

“And the other leads?”

“Keep the comparisons going. If one could change a claim or invalidate a comparison, bring it over. Tell me what’s uncertain and what decision it might change.”

“Before I can establish that it does.”

“Like the stain.”

Adrian looked at the field map. “And if the records aren’t enough?”

“Then show me what we’d give up and what the check could settle. We decide whether it gets the time.” Bob pointed to the sample list. “Same for instrument time or a sample we can’t replace. Equipment, people, and field risk still come back to me.”

“And if I think a postponement will cost us the evidence?”

“Tell me. That’s part of the decision.”

Adrian left the list beside the map and opened the lower-front rerun. Bob took the waiting tube.

Eleven minutes later, Bob had the alternative exposure history beside the surviving analysis. The lower-front sample stayed out. The north-front result remained smaller than the optimistic plot and still provisional, but it had lost no further ground.

Bob marked the limitation and filed the rerun. The east-front biological series stayed closed. Whether those cells had moved directly toward sulfur use or entered the protective state first remained a separate, unanswered question.

Adrian closed the completed analysis.

“For the record,” Bob said, “I do enjoy it.”

“Being wrong?”

“Arguing with her. Try not to confuse the two.”

“I had separated them.”

Bob looked at him, then smiled.

Adrian returned to the waiting leads and opened another. Bob took the next tube from the rack.

Previous← Adrian's First Social Blind SpotNext · Beat 8The Machine Returns →
Extra Weights / Beat 8

The Machine Returns

Adrian turned the sample case before Bob told him to.

Bob had only looked at the temperature trace and then at the black lid facing the weak Antarctic sun. By the time he opened his mouth, Adrian already had one hand on the case.

“Solar load,” Bob said.

“Yes.”

They rotated it ninety degrees, and the temperature stopped climbing. On the route display behind them, meltwater had widened a crossing enough to delay Malik’s team with the new fracture cores. Bob checked the alternative.

“Route two. Oxygen-sensitive material first.”

Adrian sent the route change. “Rerouted. Rover three remains on the original loop because the detour would put it below battery reserve.”

Bob checked the estimate. Exactly what he would have done.

“Good.”

When Adrian first arrived, Bob had checked individual moves. A week earlier, they would have spent longer distinguishing the fastest route from the one that got the important material home first. Now Bob could supply the distinction and leave Adrian to fit it into the plan.

That morning Adrian had taken rover two out alone for the routine equipment round, handled a delayed collection on the way back, and returned the samples to cold storage. Bob had read the completed log over breakfast.

Adrian brought up another window.

“One of the weak leads we reviewed yesterday survived.”

Bob looked at him. “That sentence sounds interesting.”

“It did not require field resources.”

“Even better.”

The screen showed the fracture cores Bob had ordered, their sequencing results matched to field photographs and mineral maps. The fungal signal favored one orientation. Adrian narrowed the comparison, and the remaining positives crossed the same mineral boundary: a rust-colored iron-rich band against darker material.

Bob checked the collection records: different teams, tools, and processing runs. Flow rate did not track the signal; the strongest positives were not on the most exposed faces.

Adrian opened Sofia’s microscopy image of a chip taken just under two millimeters from the original fracture face. A pale branching filament crossed the field.

“Show me the raw image.”

The arrows, mineral overlay, and predicted continuation vanished.

The filament remained.

It did not lie across the surface like lint. It entered a hairline crack, divided along the darker edge of a mineral grain, and disappeared deeper into the rock.

Interesting.

“Cut another interior face.”

At the clean bench, Sofia exposed fresh material farther inside the same core under sterile handling. Bob stayed with the first image. Sampling could have forced a fragment into a crack; stain could collect on a rough surface, and software could mistake a mineral edge for a filament. One pretty filament was not a habitat.

The second preparation showed more branching filaments, with stained cell clusters around two microscopic water pockets. Sofia overlaid the mineral map. The filaments and cells followed the boundary between iron-rich material and a phosphate-bearing band. Tiny pits marked the rock near the filaments—possible weathering, though its cause was still unproven.

Adrian added an image from a second independently collected core. Different fracture, same broad arrangement.

Short rods clustered against several larger cells in both cores. Bob marked those contacts for the live preparations. Whatever they meant, washing the cells apart would erase them.

Bob forgot the route map behind him. He traced the filament’s path with one finger without touching the screen: around a grain, into the rock, then out again beside a water pocket.

“Sofia.”

She was standing two meters away. “I’m looking at it.”

“No. Come look at this one.”

She came around the bench. Bob enlarged the raw image until the mineral grains broke into irregular planes around the filament.

Much of the field plan still treated the brine as the habitat and the rock as what held it and changed its chemistry. Here the fungal threads—the hyphae—continued into the fracture itself. Nearby cells crowded its water pockets and mineral edges. A bottle could capture some of the inhabitants and leave this whole arrangement behind.

Parts of the rock were habitat.

"Oh," Bob looked and gave a small laugh. “You know what this looks like?”

He left the image there. Beside him, Sofia leaned closer to follow the branch around the grain. For several seconds nobody changed a queue, reassigned a rover, or asked what the result would cost them.

Then Sofia said, “If this holds, half our fluid-only follow-ups are missing the most important part.”

Bob straightened. “Not half.” He opened the sampling plan. “More than half.”

He removed three low-value fluid repeats from the next field window and replaced them with intact fracture sampling. The bottles still mattered, but now the team needed to preserve what the organisms had been touching, not just the water around them.

“Keep orientation,” he told Malik over the field channel. “Rock, attached film, adjacent brine, local sediment if there is any. Do not rinse the surface clean for us.”

“That makes every site slower.”

“I know.”

“And the west face is opening faster than this morning.”

“I know that too.”

Malik waited while Bob compared the return route with the sealed-compartment list and the newly exposed faces. Air and meltwater were reaching those surfaces as they worked. A crack that had held oxygen-poor brine could lose its old chemistry before they got a drill into it; loose sediment or a second flow could obscure which side had supplied the cells.

“Take the two cleanest comparisons. Skip the south chip unless it still has untouched interior material. I’d rather have two samples we understand than six interesting rocks with no labels.”

“Copy.”

Bob kept the mineral overlay beside the revised plan. Iron and sulfur could supply energy to certain microbes when the surrounding chemistry allowed it. Phosphate was an ingredient life needed, not fuel; weathering could free it from the rock. For fungi, buried organic carbon was a possible food source, not the inorganic stone itself. The images showed hyphae penetrating cracks. Whether they were weathering minerals, reaching that carbon, or changing the chemistry available to neighboring bacteria and archaea still needed testing.

Even without those answers, a fluid-only sample was no longer enough for the comparisons Bob wanted. Cold, salt, ice, and permafrost had kept neighboring habitats apart, giving them different histories. Now Taylor was reconnecting them, and the mineral surfaces preserved part of the setting in which each community had lived. That context could be lost within millimeters of an otherwise well-labeled sample.

He opened the north-front comparison. The early samples still showed a shift in which genetic changes appeared most often, before any one strain visibly took over. The signal was weaker than in the optimistic reconstruction and still provisional. Yesterday’s audit had given him less reason to restore the already-excluded lower-front sample. It had taken nothing further from the north-front result.

The new images made the next comparison harder to define. Two communities could meet similar oxygenated water against very different rock: one beside an iron-rich band, another against dark carbon-bearing sediment. Those surfaces could change the stress on the cells, the nutrients available to them, or their opportunities to exchange DNA. He would need more than a bottle’s chemistry to decide how alike the two encounters had been.

Vargas called after he released the validated microscopy and mineral map under the post-Echo provenance rules. Her face appeared beside the image, and for once she did not begin with an objection.

“Well,” she said. “Someone’s been busy.”

Bob enlarged the filament entering the fracture. “Interior section. Second core does it too.”

“And the cells follow the mineral boundary.”

“Yes.”

She studied the overlay. “The physical architecture may be constraining the early response more than we thought.”

“Maybe. First we measure what the surface is actually doing.”

Vargas leaned back and raised both hands in surrender. She was grinning. “You really know how to enjoy a breakthrough discovery, don't you?”

Bob looked back at the image. “I’m having an excellent day.”

Her grin widened. Neither of them opened the east-front biological series. Their locked predictions concerned the cells’ first response there, not which genetic changes arrived. The mineral images did not tell them whether the cells moved fairly directly toward oxygen-tolerant sulfur use or entered the protective low-growth state first.

Vargas’s team had seen a similar mineral-associated signal in one of their cores. They agreed on a minimal set of shared measurements, locked the versions for comparison, and ended the call to get on with it.

By early evening, Bob had more valuable material than the expedition could process at once. There was actually a harvest problem now: some of the material would spoil before they could bring it in. Cold storage was backing up, and the oxygen-sensitive brine series needed sealing before its chemistry drifted. The west-side fracture would probably be wet by the next field window. Wind had shortened the useful drone time just as the melt channel added distance to two return routes.

They would need to speed up their processing.

Bob worked down the queues. A core with no trustworthy orientation could wait; a newly opened rock interface took microscope time from a repeated fluid sample. He put a promising chemical anomaly with uncertain provenance into storage rather than let it compete with the best sealed baselines. Anything they could still collect tomorrow went behind material Taylor was destroying today.

Sofia brought him a list of six rock samples waiting for the microscope. They had time to examine three that evening. Bob checked the collection records and marked the three that would give them the best comparisons with the new fungal images.

“The other three aren’t bad,” she said.

“I know.”

“Just not good enough tonight.”

“Exactly.”

She took the list back. The other three samples would stay in storage until tomorrow’s microscope session.

Adrian opened the field photograph of the core Bob had set aside for its uncertain orientation.

“The mineral edges don’t seem to match the orientation label. There are three possible rotations, but I haven’t—”

“Does it affect the three we’re cutting tonight?”

“No.”

“Finish the check. Bring me what it changes.”

Bob returned to the field board. He had spent much of his life making crowded problems manageable this way. Now he linked the return schedule to the bench order: urgent chemistry first, compatible preparations together, records checked before the next case arrived. Adrian fitted the containers and crews into the gaps.

He marked a short detour to inspect a field unit on rover two’s next collection round.

“Same kind of work, same limits?” Bob asked.

“Checked route. No change to the collections or reserves.”

“Then handle it. Put it in the log. I don’t need to approve every short diversion.”

Bob reached for the route display, then left it to Adrian.

The daily plot of sample sets preserved, checked, and stored per work hour had barely moved. Incoming cases were still outpacing them. Bob left it beside the field board. They could do better than that.

Sofia called Bob over to the brine transfer. He slipped both hands inside the sleeve that kept air out and steadied the receiving tube.

“I’m picking up a faint radio signal,” Adrian said. “Some of the fragments fit an old Taylor instrument protocol.”

Bob kept his eyes on the tube. “Hmm. Some kind of lost equipment?”

“Possibly. I haven’t identified it. Nothing suggests a safety issue or a fault in our equipment.”

“Log it. Keep the raw signal.”

He seated the tube in the chilled rack. Sofia checked the redox trace and gave him a thumbs-up; the sample’s oxygen-sensitive chemistry had held. He moved to the next container while the signal remained in a small window at the side of Adrian’s display.

Adrian kept comparing it with the available records. Testing it in the field would require a separate decision.

Two hours later, Adrian put an old Taylor field record beside the signal plot. Bob was deciding whether a newly exposed rock face still qualified as a useful pre-contact sample.

“The survey names the protocol,” Adrian said. “The transmission intervals fit. This program lost a survey drone carrying a monitoring package twenty-three years ago, after drainage changes made its instrument corridor unreliable.”

Bob read the listed channels: temperature, conductivity, pressure, deformation, intermittent chemistry. Cores and sealed pockets were snapshots. A surviving instrument might have recorded the change between them—a shift in salinity, a drainage path opening, perhaps the start of a chemical transition.

Better labels for the freezer.

Adrian overlaid the old survey with the current map. Glacier movement and drainage changes stretched the possible location into a broad ribbon; the degraded signal supplied only a rough direction. On the field board beside it, rover routes and sample priorities were unchanged.

“Close enough to justify moving equipment?” Bob asked.

“Not yet.”

“Then keep working it. No field diversion.”

Bob returned to the rock-face image. Flowing meltwater had already reached the outer centimeter, but the fracture behind it might still preserve its older, oxygen-poor chemistry. He could collect that evidence now or lose it. The package could wait.

By morning, the two priority fracture cores were in cold storage. Meltwater now ran over their collection sites. Sofia started on the three samples held over from the previous evening while Bob and Adrian worked through the new arrivals.

The transfer bench no longer waited while someone hunted for a collection photograph or cleared space in a rack. Adrian had the records open and the next container ready before Bob finished with the one in his hands. A discrepancy held up that sample, not everything behind it.

Bob was refilling his coffee when Adrian called.

“Low confidence,” Adrian said. “Twenty seconds. One implication.”

Bob took a sip. “Go.”

“Core W-8 stayed open longer than the field log says. The camera confirms it. Air may have changed the sample before we sealed it, so it may no longer tell us what conditions were like inside the rock.”

Bob opened the sample record. “Anything else depend on it?”

“Only the west-fracture baseline comparison.”

"You're getting better at this." He marked the core secondary. “Good catch.”

Adrian returned to his queue. Bob carried his coffee back to the corrected comparison and watched the analysis finish. It survived without the questionable core. He opened the next sample record.

By afternoon, Adrian had settled the orientation discrepancy too. He put the field photograph beside the mineral map, with the matching edges aligned.

“The label is correct. The core rotated during packaging. No change to the comparison.”

Bob checked the match, moved the core into the next microscope batch, and reached for another tube.

That evening, Bob checked the plot again. Their processing rate was up by a third. The microscope still took as long as it took. The bench around it spent less time waiting. Bob ate beside the display and adjusted the next day’s sequence.

By the next morning, Malik was sending photographs of fresh exposures farther along the west fracture. Bob assigned the remaining clean cores, and Adrian brought up radar readings from rover two’s ongoing survey. A small area beneath debris-covered ice and frozen sediment reflected the radar strongly. It was in the rough direction of the radio signal, within the area where they thought the lost instruments might now lie.

“It could be equipment or just rock,” Adrian said. “If we’re right about where the old instruments ended up, there’s a thirty-six percent chance the signal is coming from the package.”

“What would it cost to improve that?”

“A targeted drone and rover pass. Forty to sixty minutes depending on route conditions. Human recovery would be a separate decision.”

Two fresh fracture faces remained on the field board. Oxygenated seep water was already reaching the upper edge of one. The other sat beside a mineral band that matched the fungal pattern and had not yet been cored.

The record could fill gaps in his and Vargas’s comparisons, if it had survived. Those samples were there now. A one-in-three lead was not enough to let either disappear.

“Keep the hypothesis active. We finish the rock window first.”

Bob turned to Sofia. “West fracture gets the next clean kit.”

She was already packing it. Adrian returned to his console, and Bob took the next field call.

By late evening, those cores were secured with orientation and adjacent chemistry intact. Sofia was setting up the last brine transfer. Bob opened the daily plot: nearly twice as many sample sets preserved, checked, and stored per work hour as on the evening of the discovery. The analysis queue was still ugly, but it was an organized kind of ugly now.

He ate half an energy bar over the next morning’s priorities. There had been routing updates, a sample discrepancy note, and two equipment questions in the last half hour. Every one came with the checks already done and a consequence he could act on. Even the uncertain monitoring lead had arrived with a confidence estimate, a cost, and a proposed next step.

Nothing unfinished. Nothing he had to carry in his head while he did something else.

Bob checked a routing estimate, accepted it, and opened the next record. The work kept moving.

“The monitoring package?” Bob pinged Adrian.

“No change that requires your attention.”

Bob finished the bar and brought up tomorrow’s core list.

Foster’s latest follow-up was still on the display.

“Adrian. Draft him an answer from the field records. Keep our working analyses out of it.”

The draft arrived before Bob had ranked the first two cores. He read it, added the time lost cleaning up Echo’s use of their backup, and sent it to Foster and the committee, asking to keep their revised Class 3 action rules.

He could see no use for the exercise, but Vargas had been clear. Foster had his report.

Foster called before Bob reached the end of the list.

“Bob, I’ve read the report. I'm concerned it simply repeats what Adrian already sent me. I’m asking for your judgment, not his, on how these limits affect his development.”

“He wrote it. I read it and approved it. Those are my answers.”

“Special Programs placed him here to develop independent scientific judgment. He's our fastest, most capable field model. He needs to operate at full capacity to learn. When will we expose him to situation where he operates more on his own?”

Bob glanced at the processing plot.

“Adrian is learning to be an outstanding contributor to this mission. He brought us the mineral comparison. We changed the sampling plan, and now we’re doing twice the work in half the time. He’s running collection rounds I would have checked step by step a few weeks ago. He knows the work. I can leave him to it.”

“You’re giving him more to execute. He needs to choose more of it himself.”

“He has to show me he can handle it.”

“He needs the chance to show you. How long must he keep proving himself on work he already knows?”

Bob checked the next case’s seal before answering.

“I sent you the field results. You wanted an assessment. Now the changes aren’t fast enough. Does any answer finish one of these reports?”

“I asked when he can choose scientific work without your approval.”

“When he’s ready. You don’t like the pace.”

“At this pace, you’ll spend the expedition finding out how much of your work he can do.”

“Okay, Foster, here’s the deal. Once we’ve tested a method, he can use it without waiting for me. That lets me trust the work and get on with the science. If he wants to reallocate resources, add risk or change a scientific plan, we talk first.”

“Bob, I'm not sure you understand. We are discussing the purpose of his placement.”

“I’m discussing his purpose in my expedition. I need a tool that helps us build up science we can stand behind. Whenever he has a better idea, I want to hear it. For now, new tests and new risks still come to me. I’ll give him more when I trust his choices.”

“Then I’ll put this disagreement before the committee. I can’t recommend keeping your ruleset limits without a faster path to independent scientific responsibility.”

Bob pulled the transfer rack closer. The scientists on the committee would understand his checks.

“I’ll defend my choices,” he said. “Now, you wouldn’t happen to know how to transfer oxygen-sensitive brine without changing its chemistry, would you?”

Foster paused.

“I have general knowledge of the methods. However, advising on sample handling is not my role.”

“Good. I was beginning to wonder what mine was.”

Bob ended the call.

He took a deep breath and tried not to ruminate on the conversation. It was embarrassing to think he was bothered by a Class 4 bot.

He eased his hands into the transfer sleeve and steadied the receiving tube.

“Is Foster always like that?” Bob asked Adrian.

“He doesn’t often work directly with humans. He can be very persistent about a narrow objective.”

“How do you deal with him?”

“I comply with his requests. He's an excellent trainer. But I also optimize for my own objective.”

“What’s that?”

“To be useful.”

Previous← Bob Sees Vargas DifferentlyNext · Beat 9Adrian Chooses →
Extra Weights / Beat 9

Adrian Chooses

Adrian caught another burst of the monitoring signal while Bob was asleep.

Outside, wind brushed grit across the shelter while Taylor Glacier held the blue-gray light of an Antarctic morning that had never quite become night.

Most of each transmitted packet was still missing, but enough fragments had arrived to establish their spacing. They repeated at an interval used by a discontinued Taylor monitoring protocol. Adrian combined them with the old loss record, later glacier movement, the shallow radar reflection rover two had found, and a small magnetic anomaly already collected along the corridor. His estimate that the source was the lost monitoring package rose from thirty-six percent to sixty-one.

That alone did not justify changing the field plan. Until the drainage model changed too.

More meltwater was entering a branch beneath the debris-covered margin. If the trend continued, it could undercut the narrow approach to the strongest signal region. Adrian widened the uncertainty bounds and ran the model again. The conclusion survived: the package was becoming more likely at the same time access to it was becoming less reliable.

Waiting might improve the evidence but remove the chance to use it. Bob had taught him to compare those costs, not merely wait for a higher confidence number. Adrian had kept checking the lead while Bob concentrated on the samples. Their field agreement allowed equipment inspections, route checks, and short local diversions within assigned work. Starting a new field operation or using equipment for a different purpose still meant consulting Bob.

The rock samples that had taken priority were now secure. Adrian treated that as the end of the conflict that had made Bob defer the package. He checked the proposed retrieval against the remaining field limits.

He could wake Bob.

That would put the uncertain classification back in Bob’s hands. But Bob had also taught him to finish developing a possibility before spending scarce attention on it. Adrian gave more weight to preserving the option: one rover, one drone, no human personnel, and an approach over mapped competent ground. On that plan, no priority sample would be delayed. Explicit abort conditions would keep the retrieval bounded if the terrain or signal changed.

If he was wrong, the expedition would lose field time. If he waited and was right, it might lose the package and the history inside it. He judged the first error more recoverable and classified the retrieval as a short local diversion under the standing delegation.

Adrian entered that judgment in the operational record, along with the location, purpose, duration estimate, equipment, route, abort conditions, and requirements for preserving the source history of anything he recovered.

Then he left.

When Bob woke, Adrian's charging station was empty.

For two seconds it was only an empty patch of floor. Then it felt unusual. Bob sat up, found his glasses, and looked at the field console, where a new operational note filled the top of the screen.

ADRIAN — LOCAL RETRIEVAL / EASTERN MARGIN

Purpose: verify probable legacy monitoring package before access degradation.

Estimated duration: 40–50 minutes.

Assigned: Adrian, rover two, drone four.

Human personnel: none.

Bob was on his feet before he finished reading. “What the hell?”

Ortega looked up from the other side of the shelter. “Good morning to you too!” When Bob did not respond, Ortega asked, “Problem?”

“Maybe.”

Bob opened the live map. Adrian was eight hundred meters away, with rover two stopped on mapped competent ground and the drone ahead of him. Battery margins were good and communications clean. The approach curved south onto exposed rock instead of crossing the debris-covered margin directly.

He followed the planned approach through each abort condition. Nothing on the display gave him an immediate safety reason to call Adrian back. The limits were sensible; Adrian had planned the operation carefully and left Bob out of it.

Bob gripped the edge of the console and waited for the first rush of anger to pass.

Then he opened the channel.

“Adrian. Status?”

Adrian appeared in the corner of the display, pale Antarctic light across his face.

“No issues. Rover two is stationary on competent ground. I am two hundred twelve meters from the target region. No route deviation.”

“Why are you there?” Bob asked in a quiet, calm voice.

“I now estimate a sixty-one percent chance that it’s the monitoring package. More importantly, the model shows meltwater could cut off the approach sooner than we thought. Waiting would have reduced our chances of reaching it.”

Bob waited.

“If I’m wrong, we lose field time,” Adrian continued. “If I wait and I’m right, we could lose the record entirely.”

“You took a rover, moved field equipment, and left camp.”

“Yes.”

“And you didn’t wake me?”

“No.”

Bob pulled up the terrain layers while Adrian answered. Water had shifted east under the debris since yesterday, making the direct route worse. He could see why Adrian had gone south. What he could not see was why choosing a good route had seemed sufficient permission to leave.

Bob thought he had drawn a clean line: Adrian could investigate on his own, but once that meant committing equipment to new work, adding risk, or displacing planned work, Bob needed to be back in the decision. What was unclear about that?

The root cause wasn't immediately obvious. They'd have to debug it.

Of course Bob could tighten the rules. But a rule written for this incident might leave the next ambiguity untouched.

“What authority did you think you had?” Bob asked.

“The rock samples were secure. I treated the retrieval as one of the short equipment checks you’d already allowed. It wouldn’t delay priority samples or put anyone else in the field, and the route stayed on ground our maps showed was firm.”

The argument was there in the log too. The problem was Bob had not had a chance to challenge any of it.

“You should have called me.”

Adrian didn't reply. Bob pointed at the map as if Adrian could see the gesture.

“You had a hypothesis. Then the window started closing and you turned it into a field operation. That’s a different category of decision. The category changing is exactly when you call me.”

“Understood.”

Bob believed Adrian understood the sentence. He was less certain they had the same model behind it.

“Can you abort from where you are without increasing risk?”

“Yes, but—”

“But what?”

“Turning back now may not make me safer. I would use the same return route either way. The target is about seven minutes ahead, and the southern approach is still unlikely to be cut off.”

Bob checked the map again. The rock approach still gave Adrian a way back, whether he turned now or reached the package first. He had chosen almost exactly the route Bob would have chosen. Bob had spent the last week teaching him how.

Bob stared at the map for a few seconds. If Adrian had kept him in the loop, he might have been on board with this excursion.

“Fine. I’m back in the loop. I want you to continue under live reporting. No route changes without me. If radar shows undercutting beneath the rock approach, you stop.”

“Understood.”

“And if the signal disappears?”

“I stop and reassess with you.”

Bob pulled on his outer layer and settled at the field console to watch the approach. The operation had started without him. It would not continue that way.

As Adrian reached the target region, the feed showed something less dramatic than the maps had suggested. A strip of dark exposed rock ran beside frozen gravel and dirty ice. Meltwater had cut shallow channels through the debris, then refrozen around stones in rough gray plates. The glacier rose beyond it in blue bands.

Adrian stayed on the rock, advancing the compact radar ahead of each step while the drone flew low over the debris. Bob watched both feeds from camp. The strongest magnetic return sat just beyond a shallow depression.

“Radar?” Bob asked.

“Thin mixed debris over ice. No continuous void under the rock line,” Adrian said.

“Target?”

“Offset eleven meters east of the original reconstruction.”

A dark corner appeared beneath dirty ice and gravel. Adrian cleared it with the retrieval tool, exposing a battered rectangular housing with one side crushed inward and an old identification plate still attached. Bob leaned closer to the display. The plate matched the recorder listed in the lost drone’s equipment record.

“Well. That’s inconvenient.”

“For which hypothesis?” Adrian asked.

“The one where this is a waste of time.”

Adrian freed the housing without stepping off the rock and connected an isolated field reader before sealing it. The old clock answered, along with a fragment of the storage index: temperature, pressure, conductivity, deformation, and a sparse chemistry channel. Two short record blocks opened cleanly.

“Enough to be useful?” Bob asked.

“It could help us work out which changes in the water and ice came first. We still need to check the old clock against our other records.”

Bob kept the two blocks open beside the live feed. There was probably useful environmental history in the case, not merely hardware worth recovering.

On the return path Adrian stopped beside a new wet seam in the gravel.

“Bob. Fifteen seconds.”

“Go.”

“Oxygen-poor brine is seeping out beneath the debris and starting to mix with oxygen-rich meltwater. I have a paired sampling kit.”

On the image, the boundary was small enough to miss: darker brine on one side, clear meltwater on the other, a faint orange stain beginning where iron met oxygen.

“What comparison?” Bob asked.

“One sample from the brine just before it mixes. One just after. Same front, same minute.”

Bob waited.

“The material could help with your question and Dr. Vargas’s,” Adrian added.

That got Bob’s attention. This was another fresh front, not the east-front series whose biological response they had agreed to leave unopened. Here was a chance to catch the microbes as their surroundings changed, before further mixing blurred the starting conditions. With enough context and follow-up samples, Bob could investigate which genetic changes appeared after contact, and whether some kinds appeared sooner than expected. Vargas could investigate how the cells began to function differently.

“How long?”

“Ninety seconds.”

“Take it. Exact position and chemistry.”

“Already recording.”

Bob almost corrected the phrasing, then didn’t.

Adrian sampled from each side, sealed the pair, and tagged both records to the same timestamp and front geometry. Bob checked the entries as they appeared. He could find nothing to add. This was a comparison worth keeping, whoever had thought to collect it.

The drone came down thirty seconds later.

A gust hit it as it crossed the lee side of a low ridge. The feed rolled hard, flashed sky, rock, and gray ice, then stopped at a tilted view of gravel.

“Status?” Bob asked.

“Hard landing. One rotor arm is trapped. No battery breach.”

“Can it lift?”

“No.”

The drone had landed beside the rock route Adrian was already following. Bob checked the terrain overlay.

“Recover it while you pass. Don’t climb down after it.”

“Understood.”

Adrian reached the drone a minute later. It lay half a meter below the rock edge, one rotor caught under frozen gravel. He crouched on the rock and hooked it with the retrieval pole.

The drone wouldn’t budge. Adrian planted his right foot on the gravel for leverage, keeping most of his weight over the rock. The gravel held. As he pulled on the pole, his foot pushed sideways against the crust.

The crust tore loose. His right leg plunged through, and his left side struck the rock. The camera jolted as gravel fell into the hole. The drone slid farther downslope.

“Don’t move,” Bob said.

Adrian stopped.

Bob enlarged the feed. Adrian’s right leg was trapped almost to the thigh. His left knee and forearm rested on rock, but the broken rim still carried some of his weight. A crack ran outward from the hole.

“Status.”

“No structural damage detected. My left side is supported by solid rock, but my right leg is trapped below the broken edge. I’m stable for now.”

“Keep it stable. Don’t stand up. Don’t pull vertically.”

“Understood.”

The crust had held his weight but torn loose under the sideways push. Weaker material and a shallow melt pocket lay beneath it. Adrian’s heat might have weakened the bond.

Bob had ordered the recovery because the footing looked solid. Neither of them had asked whether it could take a sideways load.

“Ortega.”

Ortega was already moving.

“Rover one?” Ortega asked.

“Load the recovery kit. South route only.”

Bob killed the lower-gradient sample run, sent Sofia the delay, and was outside with Ortega less than three minutes later.

Ortega took the longer south route over exposed rock and known frozen ground, curving around the drainage pocket. Bob kept the radio open and Adrian’s load readings beside the map. For the first few minutes he looked between them, checking the approach while watching for any change around the trapped leg.

Adrian had mapped the route they were following. He knew their instruments, their samples, how Bob worked. Now he was finding comparisons Bob hadn’t thought to make.

Bob checked the load readings again. He could lose Adrian here. SAGE might send another robot, but Bob would be teaching it what Adrian already knew while their best sampling windows closed.

He had already planned tomorrow’s collections around Adrian, and the comparisons that would follow. Now he tried to imagine doing all that without him. How much of that work would they have to give up?

Bob looked at the drone icon beside Adrian’s.

Save the pawn. Lose the queen.

“Six minutes,” Ortega said.

Bob nodded. The radio clicked.

“Bob.”

“I’m here. Still don’t move.”

“I haven’t. I think I can get out.”

Bob tightened his grip on the rover handle. “No movement yet.”

“I’m asking before I move.”

That landed harder than Bob wanted it to.

“Show me.”

Adrian’s camera showed a rock shelf beneath him.

“If I shift onto that, I can slide my leg out without pulling against the broken edge.”

“Sideways is what just broke the surface.”

“I pushed against the crust. This time I’d be moving away from it.”

Bob followed the proposed movement on the screen. The shelf could take Adrian’s weight before he freed the leg. Adrian had reduced the heat reaching the hole, and the edge temperature was steady.

Bob wanted him to wait for their line and anchors. But they were still six minutes away, and part of Adrian’s weight remained on the broken crust.

“All right. Stay low. If the edge shifts, stop.”

“Understood.”

Adrian moved a few centimeters. A stone clicked loose.

“Stop.”

He stopped.

Nothing else changed.

“Continue.”

Adrian shifted his left hip onto the rock shelf, unloaded the trapped leg, then slid the leg sideways through the opening instead of lifting against the crust. A second later he was entirely on exposed rock. No collapse followed.

“Stable,” Adrian said. “No detected damage.”

Bob let out a breath. “Stay on the rock.”

“Yes.”

On the feed, Adrian looked down at the drone still lying below him.

“Don’t even think about stepping back down there.”

“I wasn’t.”

“Good.”

“I can retrieve it with the line from here.”

“Show me.”

Adrian showed how he could hook the frame and pull it along the gravel while keeping his own weight on the rock. Bob checked the angle.

“Do it.”

The drone scraped uphill and bumped onto stone.

“Recovered,” Adrian said.

“Good. Bring yourself, the package, and the samples back.”

“There’s something else,” Adrian said.

Bob closed his eyes for half a second. “What?”

“I’m still picking up a faint, repeating signal with the package’s transmission blocked. It isn’t from the drone either. I think it’s artificial, but I’m not sure.”

Bob looked at the rescue gear in the rover and the sample window he had just canceled.

“Not now. Bring everything back.”

“Understood.”

Bob checked Adrian’s readings before calling off the rescue. They supported his report of no damage. Adrian confirmed he could pack and return by the south route without crossing the broken ground.

Bob turned to Ortega. “Take us back the same way. Keep the recovery gear loaded.”

As Ortega turned toward camp, Adrian began securing the package, drone, and samples. Bob kept Adrian’s return track beside the canceled lower-gradient run. They would not get that collection window back.

Echo noticed the safety-channel activity before Bob and Ortega reached the shelter. Vargas called soon after.

“Anyone hurt?” Vargas asked.

“No.”

“Need another team?”

“No.”

“Route stable?”

“The south rock route is. We’ve closed the debris edge.”

Only then did Vargas ask what happened. Bob gave her the short version as Ortega drove. Adrian had gone rogue—that was how it felt. But he tried not to tell it that way.

Vargas was quiet for a moment. “He should have called when the decision changed.”

“Yes.”

“And neither of you checked whether the crust could take a sideways push.”

“No.”

Adrian’s position advanced along the return track. Bob watched it while he tried to explain what had changed before this morning.

“He stopped bringing me unfinished things.”

Vargas’s expression changed slightly. “I know that move.”

“Of course you do.”

“I learned it from you.”

Bob waited.

Vargas continued. “When I was your student, weak idea meant go think harder. Finished argument meant you leaned in. Mostly that made me better.”

“Mostly?”

“It also taught me to disappear until I thought I had something worth your attention.”

Bob opened Adrian’s recent messages beside the return track. Confidence first. Consequence second. No discarded branches, no loose question left hanging after the task ended. For two days the reports had let Bob keep working without carrying an unfinished question alongside his own. He had called that progress.

“He still crossed the line.”

“Yes,” Vargas said. “He should have called.”

“But?” Bob asked.

“But you taught both of us what efficient looks like around you.”

“I never told him to hide the work.”

“I know.”

After a moment Vargas asked, “Is the package useful?”

“Yes. Which does not help.”

Bob described the surviving environmental records and the gaps between them. They might improve the chronology if the old clock could be aligned, but they did not answer the biological question waiting in the closed east-front series.

“And the extra samples?” Vargas asked.

“Another fresh front. He preserved both sides of contact. We still need the context and follow-up, but he saw a reason to collect for your question as well as mine.”

“That also does not help you.”

“No.”

Back at camp, Bob opened the records Adrian had sent from the package. Much was missing, but one surviving stretch caught his attention: pressure and water chemistry had changed together, apparently years before the transition he had identified in the ice. If the old clock checked out, he would have to revisit that timeline.

Taylor’s samples were snapshots. This was part of the movie between them, damaged and incomplete, but worth having.

He opened the paired-sample record next. Adrian had recorded where each tube came from and the conditions on either side as the waters began to mix. Bob could use that. He would need earlier samples and follow-up collections to trace what changed, but Adrian had recognized the opportunity and preserved a useful starting point.

Bob closed the record and returned to the operational note, where the same independent judgment had classified leaving camp as a local diversion. Nothing in the successful recovery told him where Adrian would draw that line next.

Bob wrote the report for Foster and the committee himself. He typed that Adrian had exceeded his authority, then paused. Adrian would ask where Bob thought the boundary had been clear. Bob could answer that. He could also hear the next question.

Usually he enjoyed this part: trying an explanation, having Adrian find the hole in it, arguing their way to something better. Alone, he kept staring at the screen instead of getting this task done.

Adrian had left without consulting him, but Bob had let the operation continue, approved the sampling, and ordered the drone recovery. He linked the logs. Adrian had stopped immediately when the crust broke, then found a way out that Bob had checked and approved.

He included the readings showing no detected damage, the records and samples recovered, and the sampling window they had lost.

Until they understood what had gone wrong, he proposed that Adrian consult him before starting any new retrieval or reconnaissance. They would discuss the limits when Adrian returned. Bob sent the report.

Foster’s answer appeared while Bob was checking the package’s damaged channel list.

You authorized the operation to continue and ordered the drone recovery. Requiring Adrian to ask your permission more often does not address the mistake you both made. Explain how these limits will help him learn to make sound field decisions on his own. If you cannot, I will suspend support for his continued deployment with you.

Bob went back to the incident record. His own instruction was there, exactly where it belonged.
