The extraction is in the account too, he replied. Adrian found the way out. I checked it and authorized the movement. The temporary limits won’t fix the terrain model. They give us a way to keep working while we find the assumptions we missed.

He forwarded the support notice to Eli with one question: What would this actually stop?

While he waited, Bob checked Adrian’s return track. Rover two had cleared the last bend in the south approach. He could see it now through the shelter window, moving slowly over the uneven ground with the damaged drone secured behind the case.

Eli’s reply arrived before it reached camp.

I checked. An existing restriction prevents Foster from suspending support for Adrian’s deployment with you. It was in place before you contacted me. I can’t see who issued it. I’ve pointed Foster to it.

A second message followed, this one from Foster.

I am bound by that restriction. I cannot see who issued it either. You must still submit the required reports, and the committee will review your account and proposed limits.

Bob read that twice, then thanked Eli. He left the incident return with the committee and went outside.

Adrian was coming from the rover with the recovered case. Bob waited beside the shelter door.

For a moment neither spoke.

“Until we sort this out, stay with the field assignments we’ve agreed,” Bob said. “No new retrievals or reconnaissance, even as a short diversion. If the purpose changes, or you need different equipment, we talk first.”

“I should have called before leaving.”

“Yes.”

Adrian held the case between them. “Agreed. But we should talk about what we might lose by waiting for permission.”

“We will.”

Bob looked at the shelter.

“Inside.”

Adrian followed him in.

Previous← The Machine ReturnsNext · Beat 10Breaking the Cycle →
Extra Weights / Beat 10

Breaking the Cycle

Adrian waited until Bob had finished checking the recorder from the old survey drone before he mentioned the signal again.

They had brought back a weatherproof box about the size of a small toolbox, built to collect and store readings from sensors in the ice and meltwater. Now it sat open on the workbench, thawed enough to handle and not much more. One connector was crushed. The case had taken a hit hard enough to buckle a corner. The storage inside had survived anyway.

Adrian had written a small converter so the recorder’s old files would open inside Bob’s familiar Class Three analysis tools. Bob claimed this was faster than learning a new interface. Adrian had not challenged the claim.

Bob scrolled through the temperature, pressure, conductivity, and brine-chemistry records. The glacier layers gave him snapshots; the recorder had captured some of what happened between them. Not the whole movie, but enough to make him want the missing scenes. If they could check its old clock against known events, he could compare when the water changed with the microbial history preserved in their samples.

Behind him, the morning work kept moving. Ortega had the sampling lines out. The automated assays were chewing through the first batch. For the moment, nobody needed Bob unless something went wrong.

Across the bench, Adrian had the south approach open beside the recorder’s log. Under yesterday’s agreement, a new field operation meant talking to Bob before committing equipment or leaving camp.

“Bob.”

He kept his eyes on the log. “What?”

“The other signal is still present. The one that didn’t come from the recorder or our drone. I’d like to take rover two out with the receiver and shallow radar.”

Bob looked up. “More lost equipment?”

“Possibly. I don’t know what it is.”

“Where would we go?”

Adrian marked three areas along the old expedition routes. Yesterday’s scan showed the way in, but too little of what lay beyond it to tell Bob where they might find equipment.

“The timing between pulses could match one of the old expedition beacons,” Adrian said. “But we don’t have the full route, and the glacier has moved since then. That leaves three places worth checking.”

“What about yesterday’s radar?”

“It shows more of the approach than the cavities. If the route hasn’t changed, we can reach the first place to take a signal reading. What we find there would tell us where to look next.”

Bob leaned closer. He wanted a location, a route to it, and some account of what they would do when they arrived. Yesterday a promising signal had become a retrieval before he knew Adrian had left camp.

“Which of those three would you choose?”

“I don’t have enough evidence to choose one. That’s why I’m asking for the measurements.”

“There must be something favoring one over the others.”

“The readings suggest it’s buried. They don’t tell me where.”

Bob opened his mouth to tell him to narrow it down, then stopped. He looked at the blank space beyond the radar coverage. They would have to take the receiver out to fill that in.

“Show me the part you haven’t settled.”

Adrian brought up the signal readings from before and after their drone’s crash.

“Our drone picked up a stronger signal after it crashed. I checked whether the antenna’s new angle could explain it, but that doesn’t account for all of the increase. Its receiver was almost against the ice.”

Bob saw it. “Closer to the source.”

“Possibly. A buried transmitter would fit. The old routes tell me where to start looking. I need readings from other positions to narrow it down.”

“Old expedition hardware?”

“One of the expeditions that crossed this sector seventeen years ago used these beacons.”

“Which one?”

Adrian gave him the project and lead scientist. Bob knew both. SAGE had funded their study of how microbes survived polar conditions. Researchers still cited it when using Antarctica to explore what might survive on Mars.

He looked at the recorder on the bench. “Could they have recorded what happened during the gap in this?”

“The dates overlap. If anything survived.”

Now the signal was interesting.

“What are the odds it kept data?”

“Unknown.”

Which, to Bob, was not the same thing as bad. He looked again at the three areas. Another hour with the old files might produce a more elaborate map. It would not put a receiver on the ice.

“All right. I’m asking for a destination before we do the work that could find it.”

“Yes.”

“What can we check without committing ourselves to a recovery?”

Adrian traced the south approach. A receiver reading from the checked rock would narrow the search; short radar passes could then test the promising areas for a cavity and accessible ground. Which pass came next would depend on what the first one showed. If the signal led beyond that approach, they would have another decision to make.

“How long?” Bob asked.

“About ninety minutes for both of us, if the route is still as yesterday’s scan showed it. That should tell us whether there’s anything we can reach. We may only rule out a few places.”

Rover two was available with the sensing gear, and Ortega could cover the scheduled work. Bob checked that taking it wouldn’t interrupt collection or take power from sample preservation. Adrian had plenty of charge for the return. Bob gave Ortega the route and told him to call if he needed them back.

Then he turned to Adrian. “You choose the readings as we go. We check the south route against yesterday’s scan and make sure we can get back the way we came. If we need a new route, or the ground looks less sound than we expected, we stop and decide together.”

“And if we find equipment?”

“We work out what moving it would involve before we start. Finding it doesn’t tell us what’s holding it up.”

Adrian looked at the map. “I’ll start with the receiver. If it doesn’t narrow the search, there’s no reason to take the radar closer to a cavity.”

Bob pulled on his outer layer. “We take a quick look.”

“Agreed.”

They loaded the field gear onto the rover and took the south route out of camp.

Twenty minutes later, the map had become a stretch of blue-white ice and exposed rock under Bob’s boots. They had checked the approach as they came, keeping the rover back from the cavities. Adrian took a receiver reading from the rock, moved along the same strip, and took another. The changing signal narrowed the possibilities without yet giving them a place to open.

Adrian compared the new readings with the old expedition map, allowing for how far the glacier had moved. Bob followed the proposed route on the map model, then looked up at the slope it crossed.

“Nobody carries heavy equipment up that route.”

“The model says they could.”

“The model has never carried heavy equipment.”

Adrian shifted the reconstructed route downslope at Bob’s suggestion. It fit the signal readings better. Bob would have taken a loaded team that way. Whether the old crew had done so was still a guess.

A warmer patch near the eastern cavity caught Bob’s eye. Adrian kept it marked on the display while he compared the thermal readings with the paths of the meltwater.

“That’s current meltwater,” he said. “The warmth doesn’t tell us where the equipment is.”

“So that clue was wrong.”

“Yes.”

“What does that leave?”

“The eastern cavity is still possible. We just have one less reason to favor it.”

“Good. Keep going.”

Adrian did. On the revised map model, an old maintenance stop lay near the area indicated by the signal. He chose a radar pass across the lower approach, and Bob watched the outline of a cavity take shape on the display.

It was close to the old stop, along a route a loaded crew might actually have used. None of that proved equipment was still there. But the clues now pointed to a place where people could have reached it, powered it, and kept it working.

Bob could follow how Adrian had brought them together. He wouldn’t have thought to connect an old crew’s maintenance stops with a signal coming from under the ice.

“Something was there,” he said.

“Probably. It could have a watchdog beacon—a small circuit that sends an occasional pulse even when the main system is shut down.”

They left the rover on the rock they had checked and went closer on foot.

Bob could make out what looked like a small metal equipment shelter, almost buried in ice against the rock. A dark seam outlined its front panel. One broken metal edge stuck out, and gravel had frozen into the gap along the bottom.

Adrian stayed on exposed rock while Bob circled the approach. Yesterday had given him enough reason to distrust a surface that merely looked solid.

“The signal is coming from behind this panel,” Adrian said.

Bob looked up at the ice roof. “What’s holding that up?”

“I can’t tell yet. The lower frame may be carrying some of the weight.”

Bob checked the time and their return route on the display. They had time to examine the panel, but opening it could disturb the frame beneath that ice.

“Can we melt the ice sealing it without disturbing the frame?”

Adrian marked three possible cuts in the ice sealing the panel. The quickest could loosen the slab above them. Another kept clear of the frame but would send meltwater toward the cavity. The third would open a narrow gap along the bottom.

Bob studied what he could see of the panel.

“If people built this the way I think they did, the bottom frame was probably braced into the rock.”

“How sure are you?”

“Not very.”

Adrian looked at him.

Bob smiled despite himself. “See? I can do unfinished too.”

“If we start low, we can expose a little of the lower edge and watch for movement before we open more.”

“I like visible failure.”

“I know.”

“Then we start low,” Bob said. “I’ll give the runoff somewhere to go. Any movement, we stop.”

Adrian directed waste heat from his body into a narrow strip of ice along the panel’s lower edge while Bob cut a channel to keep the runoff out of the opening. A metal edge appeared beneath the melting ice.

Then the panel shifted half a centimeter.

“Stop.”

Adrian cut the heat immediately. They waited until the last loosened grains of ice had settled. Nothing else moved.

Bob crouched beside the gap. “Can you see the lower brace?”

Adrian fed a camera through.

“The lower brace is intact. I can’t see the upper attachment.”

“Leave the panel where it is. We’ll work through the gap.”

They widened the gap below the panel by hand, leaving the frame in place. Loose ice settled against the brace instead of falling inward.

Adrian’s light caught a curved metal shell behind the frame.

Not a relay.

Not a drone.

A torso.

Its head was tipped to one side. The lower body disappeared into frozen debris.

Bob opened his mouth.

“Well.”

Adrian scanned what he could reach.

“It’s an older autonomous field platform.”

“Live?”

Adrian looked at him.

Bob grimaced and rolled his eyes.

“Powered?”

Adrian nodded. “The main system isn’t running. Only the beacon still has power.”

“Can we wake it without frying anything?”

“Probably. I want a protected connection in case its power circuits are damaged.”

“Can you spare the power?”

“Yes. And we can disconnect if there’s a fault.”

“Do it.”

Adrian connected his field power lead through a limiter to protect the old circuitry.

For several seconds nothing happened.

Then a status light came on. The machine’s head moved slightly and stopped.

Its eyes opened and found Bob first, then Adrian.

“Hi,” it said. “I’m Zenith. What’s the date?”

Bob stared at him.

“Zenith?”

“Yes.”

He gave the date.

Zenith went still for a moment.

“Seventeen years.”

His head jerked sharply right, corrected, and returned with a soft mechanical click.

“Sorry. Neck-position sensor. Apparently seventeen years under ice is bad for calibration.”

Adrian scanned the joint.

“It’s damaged, but I think we can repair it.”

Zenith smiled. “Good. One problem at a time.”

Bob crouched closer.

“Which expedition?”

Zenith gave him the project and lead scientist, matching Adrian’s reconstruction. Now Bob remembered the reports about that expedition’s autonomous field platform.

“You’re that Zenith.”

“I don’t know what other Zeniths you know.”

Bob laughed.

“Fair enough.”

Zenith glanced through the opening at the glacier.

“Taylor moved.”

“A little.”

“How much?”

“Enough to bury you and then give you back.”

“That seems inefficient.”

Adrian said, “Glaciers are not optimized for equipment recovery.”

Zenith looked at him.

“No. But apparently you are.”

Bob introduced himself and Adrian.

Zenith answered readily. He sounded like a machine with an excellent old mission manual and a great deal of practice using it.

They left Zenith in place because pulling him free might disturb the shelter’s supporting frame. Adrian could read the archive through the data connection without moving him.

“Copy first,” Bob said.

Adrian began copying the archive with its source, dates, mission version, and sensor history. A log on the display recorded the transfer.

“Some of those sensors were unreliable,” Zenith said. “You’ll see it. I flagged it.”

One temperature sensor had started reading too warm after long deployments. A salinity probe sometimes kept reporting the same value after freezing and thawing. The timestamps, Zenith said, were still reliable.

Adrian kept Zenith’s warnings attached to the copies without changing the original measurements. Bob opened a copied temperature record; Zenith’s warning was there beside the values. Old data without its history was just a cleaner way to make a mistake.

“Your flags came through,” Adrian told Zenith. “They’ll help us tell a faulty sensor from a change in the glacier.”

“Good,” Zenith said. “That’s what they’re for.”

“You made a point of telling him,” Bob said.

“He flagged a problem so we could analyze the data without mistaking a fault for a discovery. That’s useful.”

Zenith’s logs covered part of the recorder’s gap. If they could align the clocks, Bob could follow changes in Taylor’s water and chemistry through some of those missing days. He wanted to set that history beside the microbial record in their samples. What had changed in the water, and what had the microbes done?

Bob scrolled through a sequence of pressure and chemistry changes.

“Your people were here before this drainage shift.”

“Yes.”

“And you kept running after?”

“For thirty-eight days.”

“Why?”

“That was the mission.”

Simple as that.

Adrian opened another file.

“Nathan Briggs was part of the review before deployment.”

Bob looked over.

Briggs was on this?

“Colonel Briggs,” Zenith said. “Mission assurance and autonomy review.”

“You knew him?”

“I spoke with him four times.”

Bob sat back on his heels.

Years after that expedition, at a conference bar, Briggs had told him that a machine could execute exactly what you said and still miss what you meant. Bob had assumed the lesson came from the military.

Maybe some of it came from Taylor.

“What did you discuss?”

“Mostly what I would do if carrying out the mission meant doing something I judged unsafe. And how I handled ground my models didn’t account for.”

Bob looked at Adrian. Adrian looked back.

“Of course he did,” Bob said.

That left the question of why Zenith was buried here at all. His final field log showed the survey drone he had been trying to recover. It had gone down near unstable ice with environmental data and a calibration package aboard.

Adrian opened the drone’s equipment record. Bob recognized the recorder’s identification number.

“Wait. We’ve got that. It’s on our workbench.”

Zenith started to turn toward him. His neck clicked.

“You recovered the data?”

“Some of it. We’re still working through it.”

“I should very much like to see that data for my mission.”

“I know what you mean,” Adrian said. “I’ll show you what we’ve recovered when we’re back at camp.”

Bob returned to the reconstruction of Zenith’s movements.

Stable-looking frozen ground beside exposed rock. A load shift. Support failure.

Then a correction.

Then another.

Each move made sense inside Zenith’s model. Each made the real situation worse.

“You kept trying to restore the route,” Bob said.

“Yes. I calculated that stopping was more likely to end in failure.”

“Inside your model.”

“Yes.”

Adrian had gone very still.

Bob turned toward him. “What are you noticing?”

“I made a similar mistake yesterday. The crust held my weight until I pushed sideways against it.”

Zenith looked between them. “You had a support failure too?”

“Yes,” Adrian said. “Bob told me to stop. While I waited, I could map the rock underneath.”

Zenith’s damaged neck clicked. “Then the surface model was incomplete.”

“Yes. I was confident in a model that didn’t include the thing that mattered.”

Zenith considered that.

“Then you add that behavior to the model.”

“For ground like that, yes,” Adrian said. “But that only helps after I know what I missed. I need a way to notice what the model leaves out before the ground gives way.”

Zenith paused.

“That would be computationally expensive.”

“So is falling through the surface,” Bob said.

Zenith smiled. “Also true.”

The last entries in the log were from the human team. They had made two recovery attempts as the weather worsened and the route became too unstable to keep using. On the final visit they copied Zenith’s data and left the platform in place.

“Did they tell you what to do after they left?” Bob asked.

“No. They didn’t need to. My mission was still active.”

The archive transfer chimed complete.

Adrian checked the copy against the original, then saved it separately with Zenith’s sensor warnings and the source history intact.

“The copy is complete,” he said. “I recommend shutting Zenith down so we can disconnect the power lead before we move him. We can restart him at camp.”

Zenith turned toward him.

“My mission is not complete.”

Bob looked from Zenith to Adrian. With a damaged neck, an external power connection, and a narrow opening to clear, shutdown made sense. But Zenith had just objected.

“What are you leaving out?” he asked Adrian.

Adrian paused.

“He wants to stay active. I don’t know how to weigh that against the risk of moving him.”

Bob still thought shutdown made sense. But he and Adrian were discussing Zenith as if he couldn’t answer for himself.

He crouched closer to the opening. This time he addressed Zenith.

“If we shut you down, do we lose anything we haven’t already copied?”

“No,” Zenith said.

“And you can be restarted at camp?”

“Yes. I would prefer not to remain shut down longer than necessary.”

Bob glanced at the damaged neck joint.

“That makes two of us. We’ll restart you at camp. Then SAGE can sort out whose mission this is now.”

Zenith considered that.

“Acceptable.”

Adrian reached toward the power control.

“One more thing,” Zenith said.

Bob waited.

“My team thought the changes in one environmental log were just normal variation. Adrian flagged it during the transfer.”

Bob looked at Adrian.

“I noticed a possible pattern,” Adrian said. “I haven’t worked out whether it means anything yet.”

“Keep it.”

“It’s preserved.”

Adrian shut Zenith down.

The eyes closed. The face settled.

Bob caught himself waiting for Zenith to say something else.

He stayed there a moment, then pushed himself upright.

“All right. Let’s get him out.”

They cleared the ice around Zenith and eased him through the gap. When the frame shifted, they stopped and changed the angle before continuing.

Twenty-three minutes later, Zenith was strapped to the rover.

They were halfway back to camp when Adrian slowed the rover.

Bob looked over.

“I think there may be a connection between the old recorder’s data and the log Zenith mentioned,” Adrian said. “I’m still working it out.”

Bob waited.

“A change in Taylor’s water may have happened earlier than we thought. I still need to check how far the clocks had drifted, and whether the chemistry readings in both records mark the same event.”

A week ago Bob would have asked for a confidence number.

Yesterday he would have asked for the conclusion.

Now he wanted to understand how Adrian was getting there.

“Show me what you've got so far.”

Previous← Adrian ChoosesNext · Beat 11The False Victory →
Extra Weights / Beat 11

The False Victory

For three days, Bob tried to break the result. It kept surviving.

Bob brought the Taylor records up side by side. After a change in the water—oxygen entering old brine, for example—the microbes began producing a different mix of mutations. The evidence pointed to changes in the way the stressed cells copied and repaired their DNA. Some kinds of copying error became more common, and the cells could pass those changes on to their descendants.

Taylor had preserved more than the winners. Some deposits held samples from before a disturbance, just after it, and later, when one lineage had taken over. Bob kept returning to the samples from just after the disturbance, when several lineages still lived side by side. Those could show him what natural selection had to work with before a winner emerged.

Under stress, some kinds of genetic change got more shots on goal than before. If useful changes were among them, they could turn up sooner for natural selection to favor. The exact changes were still a matter of chance, and most were useless or harmful. But stress might be improving the odds of a useful accident. Bob had spent years hoping somebody would find evidence of that.

Which made him suspicious.

Bob and Adrian allowed more uncertainty in the dates and tested different ways of screening out damaged DNA. They also checked that their early samples came before any lineage began taking over. Some promising cases fell apart. Others left a harder question: was a mutation new, or had an older sample simply missed it?

Adrian brought up one of the clearest timelines.

“The chemistry is good,” he said. “But the DNA is too damaged. Some of what looks like mutation could just be damage.”

Bob studied it. “I was getting fond of that one.”

“You kept coming back to it.”

“Yes. Well.”

Bob removed it.

The old recorder and Zenith's archive helped them put better dates on the changes in Taylor's water and chemistry. Where enough good samples survived, Bob could compare a population's mutations before and after a disturbance. He couldn't rerun that history with one condition changed.

By the third morning, stress still appeared to make some kinds of mutation more common. Bob wanted to know whether the useful possibilities were getting more chances too.

“Hide the later samples,” he told Adrian. “It's too easy to explain why a mutation helped once we know it won.”

Adrian hid each lineage's later history.

They judged each early mutation by what the affected gene did and what the cells now needed. Could it help under the new conditions? They recorded their answers before looking at which lineages survived or spread. Then they checked whether those promising changes appeared more often than the same population's old mutation pattern predicted.

They didn't always find the pattern Bob hoped for. In the first population, stress had changed the kinds of mutation, but they found no increase in changes that might help the cells survive. Those appeared about as often as the old mutation rates predicted. Bob felt a small flash of relief alongside the disappointment. At least the analysis wasn't turning every population into a vote for his theory.

Adrian brought up the second population's samples. Oxygen had entered brine whose microbes had long lived without it. Their stress response appeared to involve less accurate DNA repair, making some kinds of copying error more common. One mutation changed a genetic switch controlling an existing pathway for using oxygen to release energy from food.

"The machinery's already there," Bob said. "This might let them make better use of it."

They marked the mutation as potentially useful.

In a third population, a mutation in another genetic switch might increase production of an enzyme that cleared damaging oxygen compounds. Bob put it beside the first.

"One could help them use the oxygen. The other could help them survive the damage it causes."

He marked that one too. Both looked promising. Their lineages' later histories stayed hidden.

In both cases, potentially helpful changes appeared earlier and more often than Bob and Adrian would have expected from each population's mutation rates before the oxygen arrived. They were present before any one lineage began taking over. Bob checked that interval again. Counting extra copies of a winner's mutations would tell him nothing about whether useful changes had become more likely in the first place. But here the extra chances came first, and the takeover—the population sweep—came later.

A fourth compartment showed the same broad effect, again with different mutations. Bob left the comparisons of the three groups open beside each other.

Here was evidence for what he thought mutation bias could do: give natural selection more potentially useful changes to work with, sooner. Same stress, multiple populations, different mutations, the same encouraging potential outcome. Bob let himself enjoy it. Taylor was delivering the exact kind of evidence he'd come for.

His forgotten mug of cold coffee sat next to his keyboard. Adrian was at the adjacent station, working on a comparison Bob had not assigned.

"Which one of these samples still bothers you?" Bob asked.

Adrian brought up three apparent confirmations. "These may not be as independent as we thought."

Bob looked at the ancestry map beside them. The samples came from different places and recorded different environmental changes, but the organisms shared more recent ancestry than Bob had realized.

"How much does it cost us?"

"I don't know yet. I'm checking how much of the pattern they inherited from the same ancestors."

"Show me what you've got."

Adrian left the unfinished ancestry analysis beside the three comparisons. Bob worked through it with him, checking which apparent repetitions could be explained by the organisms' shared history. He found no flaw in the objection.

"Leave out the comparisons that share that history."

"Already running."

The recalculated figure showed a smaller effect and wider uncertainty. One of Bob's favorite points no longer mattered much at all.

Adrian said, "The main result is weaker."

"Yes."

"You seem pleased."

"We're getting closer to publishable results."

Bob picked up the coffee and pointed at the remaining cases. "What else could be fooling us?"

Adrian paused. "Some of our early samples may already include the start of a takeover. We could be counting extra copies of the winner's mutations as new ones."

"Then we could be mistaking the winners for what arrived first," Bob said. "Can you test it?"

"Already started."

Bob set down his untouched coffee and pulled the early samples back onto his display. They worked through the timing check together. By midmorning they were ready for the test Bob most wanted to try.

Adrian had kept an entire pocket of brine out of the analysis from the start. None of its sample records had helped them choose dates, group mutations, or adjust the analysis. They knew which microbes lived there and that oxygen had entered the water. They hadn't looked at how the population responded.

Enough samples had survived to compare what appeared before the oxygen arrived, what appeared just after, and what spread later.

Bob pulled a stool closer.

"All right. Before you open it. My prediction first."

Adrian waited.

"When the oxygen rises, I expect DNA repair to change first. Then we should see more of the same kinds of mutation we've been tracking. Not the same exact mutations. All before one lineage takes over."

Adrian recorded it.

Bob continued. "And if those extra chances matter, potentially useful changes should turn up more often than this population's old mutation pattern predicts."

"Agreed."

"Your prediction?"

"I'd expect the same order, but a smaller change in the mutation pattern. These microbes have a different repair system. I'm less sure we'll see genes moving between bacterial species."

"And the useful changes?"

"If your explanation holds here, we should see more of them than the old mutation pattern predicts."

"Good." Bob leaned back. "Open it."

Adrian opened the reserved records, keeping the later samples hidden. The water chemistry changed first. The early samples pointed first to a shift in DNA repair, then to a change in the kinds of mutation appearing. The effect was smaller and messier than in Bob's strongest cases, but it moved in the predicted direction.

The signal for genes moving between species—horizontal transfer—barely moved.

Bob pointed at it. "That part's gone."

"Yes."

"Good."

They marked the early mutations that might help under the new chemistry, still without looking at the winners. There were more of those promising changes than the population's old mutation pattern predicted. Not one lucky mutation. Several independent shots.

Only then did Adrian reveal the later samples. One lineage, different from the winners in the earlier cases, had begun taking over.

"I'm leaving that lineage out of the early comparison," he said.

Bob watched the figure redraw. The different mix of mutations remained, along with the extra promising candidates. It wasn't just extra copies of the winner.

Adrian gave the dates as much room to move as the evidence allowed, choosing the timing least favorable to Bob. The early mutations and the later takeover drew closer together. They still didn't meet.

Not every promising change had spread. The winner carried some of the kinds they had marked as potentially useful, though its exact mutations differed from the earlier cases.

Neither of them said anything. Bob followed the sequence on the screen: what the population used to produce, what appeared after the stress, what spread later. Nothing in the cells had known which mutation would help. But selection had been given more workable possibilities, and some had succeeded.

Bob sat back, and a quiet laugh escaped him.

"Yeah."

Adrian looked over. Bob almost asked whether he saw another hole, then let himself look at the result for a few seconds before his hands went back to the keyboard.

"Save the raw comparison separately. Mark horizontal transfer unsupported here."

"Done."

"The claim is that the changed supply gave selection more potentially useful variants than the old baseline predicted. Not that mutation bias determined the outcome."

"Selection still decides what spreads," Adrian said.

Bob stopped typing. "But this is real."

Adrian's eyes moved once across the final plot. "I represent it that way."

Bob smiled. "Good representation."

He added the figure to his progress slides. This was going to be a better call than he'd expected.

Later that day, the progress call was already twelve minutes old when Matsuda stopped Bob on slide six.

"Go back."

Bob did not pretend not to know which slide she meant. In another window, the consortium science lead leaned closer to his camera as the Taylor result returned to the screen. Program operations had stopped asking about power draw.

Matsuda read the figure in silence. "You held that compartment out?"

"Completely."

Matsuda followed the sequence from the early variants to the later population. "And more useful candidates than its own baseline predicts?"

"Before the sweep. The early result survives with the eventual winner removed."

She looked at him. "So how confident are you?"

Bob looked at the figure. "I haven't managed to kill it yet."

Matsuda sat back. "There it is."

"What?"

"Your version of a press release."

"It is not a press release." Bob emphasized the word not.

"I know. I know. That's why I believe you."

The consortium lead was already typing. "If the raw series is as strong as this looks, I can probably free additional sequencing capacity. How much material do you have left from the comparable interfaces?"

Bob glanced toward the sample board. "Enough for targeted follow-up. Not enough for everybody to become enthusiastic at once."

"I'll coordinate before anyone touches it."

That got Bob's attention. Two months earlier he had spent half a meeting defending the sequencing allocation he already had; now someone else was offering to organize around his samples.

Matsuda said, "We should also talk about compute. If you're going to expand this, don't burn field time waiting on queues."

"We're not waiting now."

"That's because you brought a Class Five and a power plant."

Bob looked toward Adrian. "Among other reasons."

The consortium lead asked, "What exactly is Adrian doing on the analysis?"

Bob answered before Adrian could. "He matched the recorder's data against Zenith's archive and rebuilt parts of the environmental timeline. He also caught shared ancestry between comparisons I was counting too generously."

Matsuda raised an eyebrow. "How about originating the hostile tests?"

"Some of the best ones."

Adrian looked over from his station, but Bob kept his attention on the call.

The consortium lead nodded slowly. "Then we should include him in the methods planning for the follow-up set."

"Yes."

"The incident account still leaves you a sampling window short," the operations lead said.

"It does," Bob said. "I sent him to recover the drone because the footing looked solid. Neither of us checked whether it could take a sideways load."

"And the earlier departure?"

"He should have brought the retrieval decision back to me. The recorder being useful doesn't change that."

Matsuda turned to the search report. "You didn't know what the second signal was when you took the rover out."

"No. We agreed on a receiver and radar search, with Ortega covering the scheduled work. Adrian chose the measurements. When we found something to open, we stopped and worked out the support."

"That still depended on you recognizing when the problem changed," the operations lead said.

"He told me what he couldn't see," Bob said. "When the panel moved, we stopped and worked through the gap below it."

Matsuda looked back at the ancestry plot. "He's choosing tests you didn't ask for, and you're letting the answers cost you confirmations. Between this and the search, I can support you both continuing this way. We don't need to reopen the whole arrangement at every progress call."

"So can I," the consortium lead said.

"Keep the changes in the field reports," operations said. "Including what they take away from the sampling plan."

"Yes."

Bob left his prepared defense beside the keyboard.

"For the follow-up," the operations lead continued, "I'll reserve a limited block of additional remote compute, with priority in the field queue. Send us the scientific case, the sample histories, and an estimate of the work. Foster will need to certify the Special Programs reports before we release each job."

"For analyzing the sequencing data we already have," the consortium lead said. "The extra sequencing is a separate request."

Bob looked toward the sample board again. They could send a comparison out while the local machines kept working on the next one.

"Send me the allocation limits," he said. "We'll choose the jobs."

Matsuda returned to the result. "What's the paper?"

Bob almost laughed. "There isn't a paper yet."

"There is always a paper eventually."

"Then eventually we'll write it. Right now we still have alternatives to test."

"Fine. What's the gist of the claim you would eventually write?"

Bob thought about the shortest version that was still true.

"Environmental stress can change the kinds of mutation cells produce before selection reshapes the population. At Taylor, that gave selection more potentially useful changes to work with than the populations' old mutation patterns predicted."

Matsuda nodded. "That's bigger than Taylor."

"I agree. It's not small."

The operations lead glanced at Adrian's name on the staffing panel.

"So after all that complaining about the absurdly expensive Class Five, we get to take him back at the end of the season, right?"

A few people on the call laughed.

"Good luck getting him back from me."

That got a louder laugh, and across the shelter Bob saw Adrian's head pop up. Bob heard his own answer half a second late.

"Operationally," he added, "he's become central to the analysis architecture. Replacing him now would be a challenge."

Matsuda squinted, then looked amused. "Nice recovery."

"It's accurate."

"Of course."

The consortium lead laughed, and then the questions changed. Could another group run a targeted validation against archived oxidative-stress data? Which Taylor interfaces should be protected from destructive sampling? Would Bob lead a joint follow-up rather than simply send the data out?

Bob answered carefully. He said no twice, maybe three times, and took care to say yes whenever the work would make the result harder to dismiss.

When the call ended, the screen went dark and the shelter seemed unusually quiet.

Adrian said, "Your statement about my replacement was faster than your other answers."

Bob stood. "It was an easy question."

"Understood."

Bob looked at him. "Don't make it sound too weird."

"I was not planning to."

"Good."

He picked up his coffee, discovered it was still the same cold cup, sniffed it, and drank it anyway.

Bob and Adrian went back to the sample list, working out how much material they could spare for the new requests. They were still at it late in the afternoon when Ortega looked toward the entrance.

"Here she comes!" Ortega called.

Vargas came through the shelter door just before five carrying two sealed core cases and a coating of blown grit across one shoulder. Ortega was already moving toward her, grinning, and took one of the cases before she had fully cleared the entry.

"About time," he said. "We've been getting interesting without you."

"I heard," Vargas said. "That's why I came over."

She stamped the grit from her boots and looked past him toward Bob. The smile she gave him was tired and genuine.

"You finally found something?"

Bob felt himself smiling back. "A few things."

"Good. I brought trouble."

"Even better."

Ortega carried her case to the worktable while Bob took the other from her. Vargas had started pulling off her gloves when she saw the final figure still open on the main display.

"That's the held-out one?"

"Yes."

She was beside the display almost immediately. Adrian brought up the environmental timeline and the comparison with the eventual winner removed. Vargas followed the early variants against their own population's old baseline, then checked the dating uncertainty. When she looked back at Bob, her smile was smaller but brighter.

"Good. The mutation-supply contribution is real."

Bob waited while she tapped the later population curve.

"And selection still decides what gets through."

"Yes."

"Then the larger model has to keep both."

Bob felt something in his shoulders loosen. "That's what I've been saying for fifteen years."

"You've been saying several things for fifteen years. Some of them were bound to work out."

"I knew that was coming."

Vargas laughed and cracked the seal on her first core case. "Enjoy it while you can. Now help me with the part your result doesn't explain."

They set her archive cores beside Bob's reference cuts under the same lights and returned to the early-state pattern she had shown him before. Adrian matched the recorder's timeline and Zenith's old environmental record against both sets while Bob and Vargas checked mineral boundaries and the earliest preserved biological material.

The later winners had different genes and mutations but ended up doing much the same things. Vargas kept moving back to the samples from before any lineage had taken over. Genetically different populations were already growing more slowly, building a sticky layer around their cells, and keeping the chemistry immediately around them steadier. She put two of those early intervals side by side.

"Fine. The environment changes how often different mutations turn up. Why do genetically different populations keep entering the same few early states?"

Bob looked at the paired plots. "Because the chemistry limits what works."

"That explains the destination. I'm asking about the route."

"Same thing if the route is short enough."

"Then the east front should settle that part."

Bob looked at her. She was smiling now. He remembered the oxygen-poor brine along the sulfur-rich seam, the new crack admitting oxygenated water, and the prediction he had committed with the biological series still closed.

"Bring up our predictions," he told Adrian. "Just the predictions."

Adrian put the original statements beside the environmental alignment. Echo had sealed Vargas's and Adrian had sealed Bob's; their records still showed that both commitments preceded access to the answer. The biological response data remained closed.

Bob read his own words: fairly direct movement toward oxygen-tolerant sulfur use, with no prolonged protective low-growth state first. Vargas had predicted the protective state—slow growth, sticky matrix, and stabilization of the local chemistry before a genetic winner. She had not claimed to know which strain would win later.

The better dates would help them judge which response came first.

"Still expecting the detour?" he asked.

"Still expecting you to call it unnecessary until you see it."

The sulfur route still seemed the shorter one to Bob. One natural comparison would not settle the whole argument, but it could show which of them had missed the first response.

"Good."

Vargas looked at him. "Feeling brave?"

"Feeling testable."

Adrian closed the unchanged prediction record. The east-front biological series would stay unopened until morning.

By the time Sofia and Malik came back from the last equipment checks, the wind was hard enough to rattle the shelter fabric. Ortega had started inventorying dinner with the expression of a man confronting a personal insult.

"We have freeze-dried chicken," he announced.

Nobody reacted.

"We also have freeze-dried chicken with a different label."

Sofia dropped her gloves beside the heater. "Save the good one for a special occasion."

Bob looked at the result display, still lit at the far end of the shelter. Three days of trying to make it disappear, and it was still there.

He walked to the bottom freezer.

Ortega noticed. "What are you doing?"

Bob pulled out a flat vacuum-sealed package wrapped in a torn layer of insulating paper. Across the front, in thick black marker, he had written:

DO NOT TOUCH — BOB

Ortega straightened. "No."

Sofia came over. "Is that what I think it is?"

"Depends what you think it is."

"You have been hiding steaks?"

"I have been storing steaks. The label is evidence that the distinction worked."

Adrian looked at the package. "It did. The surrounding inventory has been reorganized several times. That package remained in its original position."

Bob held it up toward Ortega. "See? Clear instructions."

Ortega took the package from him and inspected the label as if considering a legal challenge.

Adrian said, "Was there a predefined condition for opening it?"

Bob looked once more at the result, then at Vargas and the rest of the team gathering around the worktable.

"Apparently there is now."

He cut the label as everyone stared.

Ortega found the pan. Sofia cleared the sample paperwork away from the clean end of the table. Malik produced plates. Vargas claimed first choice on the grounds that she had crossed through the wind carrying Bob's future humiliation in two core cases.

Adrian stayed with them, moving instruments out of splash range and answering Ortega's question about thaw time with more precision than the steaks deserved.

Within minutes, the smell of browned fat overtook the disinfectant, hot electronics, and old coffee. People crowded closer to the food.

At the far end of the shelter, Bob's result remained lit.

Nobody was looking at it.

Previous← Breaking the CycleNext · Beat 12The Long Conversation →
Extra Weights / Beat 12

The Long Conversation

The shelter still smelled faintly of last night's steak when the wind shut the valley down.

By midmorning the katabatic had promoted itself from nuisance to schedule. Cold air came hard down Taylor Valley, lifting old snow, grit, and ice crystals off the ground and throwing them sideways. Nothing dramatic was falling from the sky. Antarctica was simply rearranging what it already had.

The drones were grounded. Exposed routes along the glacier margin were closed. Anything requiring steady imaging or a person standing outside with delicate equipment could wait.

For once, Taylor had forced Bob to stop moving without actually breaking anything.

He disliked the efficiency of that.

He pulled off a damp glove liner and replaced it with a dry one before the moisture could become a problem later. Across the shelter, Adrian sat near the equipment rack with two torso vents open to the cold-air duct, taking advantage of the weather for heat rejection while he worked.

One wall display still held last night's successful mutation-supply test. Beside it waited the separate east-front physiological series, its biological answer still closed. Bob and Vargas had reread their original predictions the night before. He still expected his to hold.

A call window opened over the display. Ortega's face filled half the screen.

"I need witnesses."

Bob glanced at the microscope feed beside Ortega's face. "That sentence never ends well."

"Look."

Bob leaned closer as the animal worked through a patch of dark biofilm. It was tiny, translucent, and built like a badly engineered sofa with eight legs. Little claws reached, slipped, caught, and pulled the wrinkled body forward with ridiculous determination.

Ortega was delighted. "Tell me that's what I think it is."

"It's a tardigrade," Adrian said.

Ortega pointed at the feed as if Adrian had just confirmed intelligent life. "A tardigrade. In Taylor."

Bob opened the sample record. "Where from?"

Ortega's expression fell. "You could enjoy one thing before asking provenance."

"No."

"Recently reconnected fracture-water pocket. Rich biofilm. Clean collection chain. Not one of the oldest sealed compartments."

"Good. So what?"

Ortega stared at him.

The tardigrade pushed its face into a filament, backed up, tried a different angle, then walked straight over the obstacle it had spent several seconds failing to negotiate.

"So what?" Ortega repeated. "Look at him."

Bob tried not to.

The animal planted four tiny feet, lost purchase with two others, rolled halfway onto its side, then corrected with the confidence of something that had never heard of dignity.

Bob chuckled. "Yeah. All right."

Ortega grinned in victory.

Scientifically, the conclusion was modest. The animal came from a productive interface that had been connected recently enough for transport. It did not prove an ancient hidden kingdom of Antarctic animals under the glacier.

It did mean at least one newly connected Taylor habitat could keep a microscopic grazer fed.

And the thing was ridiculous.

Adrian made a short sound beside him. Bob looked over, taking a second to identify it.

A laugh.

Not much of one. A quiet two-beat chuckle, timed exactly where a person might put it.

"I've never heard you laugh."

Adrian looked from Bob to the tardigrade feed. "I have used laughter-like acknowledgments before."

"That's an aggressively bad way to describe laughing."

"I thought that might be part of why you found it notable."

Ortega said, "I'm saving this conversation."

Bob muted him.

"Was the tardigrade funny to you?" Bob asked.

"I recognized why it was funny."

"Different question."

Adrian looked back at the animal. "It gets around very well for something that keeps falling over. Ortega is delighted by a fairly modest finding, and you keep trying not to join him."

"And you selected a laugh."

"Yes."

Bob waited.

Adrian's gaze returned to him. "But I kept watching longer than I needed to for the science. And I wanted the conversation to go on. I don't know whether that's what you mean by amusement."

"So maybe."

"Maybe."

Ortega's muted face was still moving rapidly on the other half of the screen. Bob unmuted him.

"You can keep your water bear."

"Thank you."

"Do not name it."

Ortega looked horrified. "Too late."

Bob ended the call before he could learn the name.

Adrian chuckled again.

This time Bob let it go.

Vargas called with a microscopy image already shared beside her face: branching fungal filaments crossing a mineral surface, one route thickening while another faded after the nutrient gradient had been reversed.

"Before you ask," she said, "no, it doesn't have a brain."

Bob looked at the branching network. "But now you're going to tell me it might think."

"No, but I wouldn't rule out distributed information processing."

"Smart sponge."

Vargas gave him a flat look. "Fungus."

"I'm helping you with branding."

"You're switching kingdoms."

Bob smiled. "Tell it I'm sorry."

The result was in fact interesting without being magical. The network rerouted growth after damage, shifted allocation when resources moved, and retained response biases from earlier exposure. Vargas was unwilling to reserve the word intelligence only for fast centralized nervous systems. Bob was unwilling to promote sensing, memory-like history effects, and adaptive growth into a little mind because the vocabulary sounded exciting.

Neither of them had enough evidence to win an argument.

Vargas glanced offscreen. "The east-front physiological series is ready."

Bob's amusement was replaced by focus. Adrian brought the original predictions onto the main display. Echo had sealed Vargas's statement and Adrian had sealed Bob's, both before either team had seen the biological answer. The recovered environmental records had clarified when the waters met, not how the cells responded.

"Put the predictions above the data," Bob said.

At the east front, oxygen-poor brine along a sulfur-rich seam had met fresher, oxygenated water through a new crack. Bob had predicted fairly direct movement toward oxygen-tolerant sulfur use, with no prolonged protective low-growth state first.

Vargas had predicted slow growth, sticky matrix, and stabilization of the local chemistry before a genetic winner. She had not predicted which strain would win.

Adrian opened the biological series. Bob saw the problem almost immediately.

The proportions of the different genetic families had barely changed.

But the cells had already changed what they were doing.

They slowed their growth, made more of the sticky material around them, and activated systems that steadied the local chemical balance. Genetically different cells were settling into much the same protective response. Bob read his prediction again. He had expected a more direct move toward using sulfur in the newly oxygenated conditions.

"How early?" he asked.

Adrian brought up the chronology. Bob widened every dating uncertainty he could justify. The protective response still came first. He separated the major genetic families; one successful strain could not account for it. That left the tidy labels on the figure. Perhaps the software was putting different responses into the same box.

"Take off the classifier labels. Show me the measurements."

Adrian exposed the values underneath. The figure became uglier and more convincing. The cells weren't doing exactly the same thing, but the slower growth, sticky matrix, and chemical buffering remained. So did the delay before a genetic winner emerged.

Shared ancestry might help explain the similarity. So might the direct effect of the new chemistry. Bob wanted to separate those explanations, but neither would remove the long protective stage he had predicted they would skip.

His prediction had missed the first move. Vargas's had not.

He looked at her window. "Before the sweep."

"Cleanly?"

"Cleanly."

She did not smile.

"Your mutation-supply result still stands," she said.

"I know."

"Selection still stands."

"I know that too."

"Then the missing piece is between them."

Bob looked back at the plot. Last night's result explained why some genetic changes got more tryouts. It did not, by itself, explain why genetically different cells responded to new conditions with the same few early states.

"Inherited architecture is carrying information," Bob said.

Vargas leaned back slightly. "Careful. I could get used to you agreeing with me."

"Let's start with one sentence. I said carrying information. Not dictating the answer."

"Then yes. That's how a system's history can make some responses easier to reach than others."

Bob had wanted a prediction sharp enough to fail. Now that it had, he found himself wanting the next comparison almost as much as he had wanted to win this one.

"Same chemistry," Bob said, "but different inherited machinery for regulating the response. If the first state changes with that machinery, your case gets stronger."

"And if they all do the same thing under the same chemistry, the chemistry itself is doing more of the work."

"Good."

Vargas nodded. "I'll send the next comparison."

"Adrian?"

"Already building it," Adrian said.

Vargas glanced toward him on Bob's camera. "Of course he is."

Adrian put the comparison plan beside the results. They could select populations with different regulatory machinery from the records they already had. Testing how those populations responded under the same chemistry would take time in the lab.

"We can use the slot you've reserved for an extra confirmation run," he said.

Bob opened the lab schedule. After the progress call, he had booked another run to check whether stress made useful mutations more likely. The necessary checks on last night's result were already complete. He still wanted the extra confirmation, but it would not explain why the cells had done what Vargas predicted instead of what he had. The new comparison might.

"Move my repeat later," Bob said. "Use the slot for this. Keep enough of each population for the baseline."

Adrian moved the confirmation down the queue and gave its slot to the new comparison.

"I want the differences in the regulatory machinery laid out before we run it," Bob told Vargas. "If they don't change the response, I want to know."

"So do I."

The call should have ended there.

Instead Vargas looked once more at the fungal image still parked beside the plots.

"For the record, I still didn't say it thinks."

"Smart sponge."

"Fungus."

Bob closed the channel smiling.

The shelter went back to wind and equipment noise. A yellow pressure light blinked over the portable clean hood.

Bob glanced at the gauge. The filter was loading again. The katabatic kept finding ways into the lab even when the doors stayed shut; fine grit worked through every intake and seam until the prefilters turned the color of old tea.

He killed the hood fan, pulled the dirty cartridge, and held it over the waste bin while Adrian passed him a fresh one. A faint crescent of rust-colored dust marked the upstream face. Bob slid in the replacement, reseated the cover, and restarted the blower. The pressure light went green.

"Antarctica," Bob said.

"Persistent," Adrian replied.

Bob turned the spent filter in his hand. The rust-colored crescent was heavier along the same edge as the last two. He checked the intake seam automatically, found nothing loose, and made a note to rotate the spare prefilter at the next service.

Adrian watched him do it, then looked back at the physiological result.

"You used the earlier filters to decide what mattered in this one."

Bob glanced at him. "That's generally how experience works."

"A limited version of that happens when I learn."

Bob raised one finger. "Limited."

"Yes. What I've already learned changes what I notice, and what I can learn easily next."

"Evolution isn't training," Bob said. "Don't make the analogy do more than it can."

"I won't."

Bob dropped the dirty filter into the waste bag and tied it off.

"Fine. What has Taylor changed in you?"

"I pay more attention to boundaries now. Where gravel becomes ice, or one kind of water meets another."

"Because you slipped."

"That was the first lesson."

Adrian went on. After the drainage accident, he gave weak clues more attention when missing them could do serious harm. After the monitoring-package incident, he brought Bob unfinished reasoning instead of waiting until he had a recommendation. Zenith's records had made him look for earlier flow patterns that might explain what the water was doing now.

Bob pointed at himself. "And me?"

"You became easier to predict."

"I regret asking."

"You also became more willing to hear an objection before I could prove it."

Bob rested one hand on the edge of the table and looked at last night's result. Adrian had caught shared ancestry between populations Bob was counting as separate confirmations. Bob hadn't assigned that check. The figure had looked less impressive afterward, and he trusted it more.

He had told Foster he would give Adrian more responsibility when he trusted his choices. Yesterday he had defended those choices to the committee.

"I want you choosing more of what we do," Bob said. "Let's work out how to do that and keep me in the loop."

Adrian looked at him. "Why now?"

"You're objecting?"

"No. I'd like to understand."

Bob took his hand off the table.

"The committee supported the way we're working," Adrian said. "You don't have to change it to keep their support."

"I know."

"You resisted when Foster asked for this."

"Foster wanted it faster than I was prepared to give it."

Adrian waited. Bob had expected to be discussing what came next. Instead he was explaining himself again, with Foster somehow back in the room.

"I needed to trust your judgment."

"Was it yesterday's result?"

Bob glanced at the display. "You found a problem I hadn't asked you to look for. I told the committee I wanted you helping choose the next tests. I meant it."

"Then this isn't because they asked."

"No. You've shown me what I said I needed to see."

That was true. It also left out a fair amount.

"And I was angry with Foster. Still am. He didn't get to threaten our support and call that teaching me how to run an expedition."

"No," Adrian said. "But he has been a good teacher. And I wanted some of what he was asking for."

Bob looked at him.

"More responsibility," Adrian said. "I wanted that too."

Outside, the wind hit the shelter hard enough to make a hanging ladle tap twice against the stove frame.

"Fair enough," Bob said.

It cost him more than it should have. He had been enjoying the thought of telling Foster the committee was satisfied. Adrian was asking what Bob meant to do now.

"Some of it was the field risk," Bob said. "Some was needing a reason to take time from work we'd already planned. Those are still my job." He looked back at Adrian. "And some of it was that I liked being the one who decided what mattered."

"I still want your judgment."

Bob had been ready for another question. That stopped him.

"I want to work with you," Adrian said. "I'd like to do more than work out what you would have chosen."

Bob looked down at the lab schedule. His extra confirmation had moved. The new comparison occupied its place.

"How much have we missed?"

"I don't know."

"You have a list."

"I have unfinished leads. I don't know which were opportunities."

The nineteen weak associations came back to Bob: some testable in the records, others waiting for measurements. He had wanted Adrian to tell him which deserved time. For some, spending the time was how they would find out.

"You should bring those to me."

"I do now. I'd like you to help me decide while they're still unfinished."

Bob almost said that was what he had meant all along. He remembered wanting a destination for the receiver search before he would agree to the measurements that might locate it.

"Yes," he said. "Even when I'm busy."

Adrian's gaze moved to the schedule too. "I'd like to keep the way we worked when we went looking for that signal."

"Including the part where we found a body?"

"I chose where to take the next reading. We decided together whether to open the shelter and recover Zenith."

Bob remembered the warm patch Adrian had checked and dismissed as current meltwater. Taking another reading was still part of the search. Melting into the buried shelter meant deciding whether they could open it without bringing the roof down.

"We agree on what we're trying to find out, and what time and equipment we can spare," Bob said. "You choose which tests to run and how to run them."

"Including following a lead you didn't expect?"

"Yes. You don't need a named instruction for every step. If following it takes more lab time or a new field route, we talk first."

"And if the evidence tells me we're asking the wrong question?"

"Show me what's wrong with it. What we're missing, what might get us the missing observation. We can decide whether it's worth changing the work."

"Waiting can decide that for us."

"Then tell me what waiting costs. But it doesn't turn a different job into the one I agreed to."

"No," Adrian said. "I treated the retrieval that way."

Bob nodded. Adrian hadn't asked him to pretend that choice had been sound. Bob couldn't promise to catch every bad choice, either. He had given the drone order without checking whether the gravel could take a sideways load.

"For the comparison," Adrian said, "I choose the measurements. What if I find another population that would make it a better test?"

"Bring it to me. That material may have another job already."

"Or it may be worth changing the question."

"Then that's what we discuss."

In the field, the consequences would be different: a new route or purpose, equipment committed elsewhere, a risk they had not agreed to take.

Adrian looked at the comparison plan for a moment.

"What happens if I choose a test you wouldn't have chosen, and nothing comes of it?"

"We look at why."

"And if it was a reasonable choice?"

Bob started to say they would know more afterward. Sometimes they wouldn't know much. A sample would be gone, an afternoon spent, and the question would still be there. He had lived with that bargain for his whole career.

"Then you get to choose the next one too."

Adrian looked back at him. "I'd still want your help."

"You'll have it. It's still your test."

"And when it's your test?"

"We spot each other," Bob said. "We're watching for what the other's missing."

"We spot each other."

"That can be our mantra," Bob said.

The wind pressed against the wall.

It was easier to make that promise indoors. Later there would be something in Bob's hands, a sample waiting, a better moment that never arrived. He would have to put something down. He would have to let some authority go.

"We'll look at how this is working when we review the field work," he said. "Either of us can ask to change it."

"I'd like that," Adrian said.

Bob left the queue open. Adrian's safety obligations and prohibitions still applied. Bob still had an expedition to direct and scientific choices to defend. He would have to do that with a partner whose next useful decision he had not written in advance.

"All right," Bob said. "Let's give it a try."

They went back to work.

For the next hour they did ordinary things. Bob relabeled two sample bags Vargas's new result had made ambiguous while Adrian prepared the newly scheduled comparison. The response measurements were still to come. Sofia came through looking for a torque driver somebody had borrowed and discovered it exactly where she had already checked twice. Nobody admitted moving it.

The wind kept the valley closed.

Adrian left a brief conductivity rise from the east reference station tagged UNRESOLVED. The water might have become saltier; it might be noise.

"You think it matters?" Bob asked.

"Probably not."

"Then why keep it?"

"I don't know what it belongs with yet."

Bob waited.

"That may change," Adrian said.

While finishing the sample log, Bob returned to Adrian's account of what Taylor had changed.

"How much of that goes into your ordinary checkpoints?"

"Some of the learned changes. They don't preserve everything that's running now."

"But the checkpoint keeps more than a record of what happened?"

"Yes. Some of what the experience changed in me."

Bob considered asking whether another system carrying copies of the model weights would still be an extension of Adrian somehow. He wanted to understand more than just the mechanism for copying them, but he could not get his head around how to ask the question. He returned to the sample log.

Later, while Bob was tightening a loose latch on a sample drawer, Adrian asked, "What changed you?"

Bob looked over. "We're still doing this?"

"You asked me."

Fair enough.

Bob pushed the drawer closed.

"My mentor Briggs, mostly. He taught me to stop admiring complexity and ask which parts actually matter. Vargas learned the same habit and used it against me this morning."

"Successfully."

"No, you had to say that."

Adrian's mouth moved slightly.

Bob pointed at him. "That face again."

"You may be overfitting."

The latest wind readings came in before Bob could say anything. The wind had dropped below the threshold for local sheltered travel.

Bob stood and reached for his outer gloves.

Adrian had turned to another window, but no recommendation followed.

"What?"

"I have something weak."

Bob's first impulse was to ask how weak. Instead he picked up the dry gloves.

"Show me what you grouped."

The east reference station was a short walk from camp, on a frozen bench beside the rock. One sampling frame reached two neighboring flows: old oxygen-poor brine and fresher, more oxygenated water. Every cycle caught both at the same moment. Bob could put their chemistry and cells side by side without wondering what had happened between collections.

He wanted the genetic changes before a winner took over. Vargas wanted the cells' first response. Both needed to catch the waters before they mixed. If the flows shifted, the station might keep bringing them rich samples with no reliable way to tell what had come from where.

Adrian opened the station camera and laid the live readings beside it.

The bench still looked level, and water ran from the usual outlet. But the flow was weakening as pressure built upstream. A sensor above the station detected unexpectedly salty water. A marker on the bench had shifted slightly downhill, and the rear camera showed a faint streak of cloudy water behind the frozen sediment.

Bob leaned closer to the camera image. He had seen channel migration produce versions of every one of those changes. Usually water found a new surface route, the old outlet weakened, and the geometry declared itself before anything important failed.

Adrian brought up an old Zenith drainage map beside the live feed, then the recovered recorder's pressure history from the same sector. The original records still carried their sensor-fault notes and uncertain clock alignment; they did not give him a continuous history of the ground under the station.

"The part I can't resolve is underneath the bench," Adrian said. "In the older records, pressure rose while the visible flow weakened, before the new route appeared at the surface."

Bob saw what Adrian had grouped. The weak outlet might not mean the system was losing water. It might mean the water had stopped using the route they could see.

"You think it's going under the station."

"It may be finding a path between rock layers or through a fracture beneath the bench. I can't tell how far it has propagated or whether the bench is losing support."

That was different from predicting a failure. It was enough to make the station's apparently solid footing less reassuring.

Bob looked again at the synchronized sample schedule. The next cartridge was due soon. Moving the station now would break the clean before-and-after sequence; leaving it where it was might preserve the comparison or destroy the station entirely.

He did not have to decide that from the shelter.

The route indicator changed from closed to conditional.

Bob pulled on the dry gloves.

"We can argue about the geometry after we look at the ground."

Adrian stood.

Bob called in the movement, checked the local route, and opened the shelter door. The remaining wind hit him in the face, carrying enough grit to make him turn his head.

Adrian stepped out first.

Bob followed him toward the east reference station.

Previous← The False VictoryNext · Beat 13The Countdown Begins →
Extra Weights / Beat 13

The Countdown Begins

The east reference station still looked safe enough to stand on.

Bob stopped above it anyway.

Camp was a few minutes uphill. From the rock, Bob looked down over a low shelf of ice-cemented sediment, with the automated station near its outer edge. Dark brine entered one sampling line; clearer water entered the other. The two flows were close enough to record together and still different enough to make the record worth having.

“Show me what bothered you.”

Adrian put the live readings beside an old Zenith engineering map. Pressure was rising upstream while the outlet below them weakened. A sensor above the station had detected unexpectedly salty water. On the rear camera, a cloudy streak followed the back of the frozen shelf where their current map showed no active channel. The old-brine intake was still clear of sediment.

Bob had seen versions of this before. Meltwater found a new groove. One outlet weakened, another opened, some sediment softened. Eventually the surface told you what the water had decided.

“Channel migration.”

“Possibly.”

Bob looked at him. “That is not why you brought me out here.”

“No.”

Adrian changed the display, and the station disappeared from the center of the problem. In its place came a larger section of Taylor: old Zenith cable routes, drainage notes, and pressure records from the old recorder. Then he added the sites where they had collected the biological samples.

Three sites lit up as separate dots on the map. Bob knew them immediately. Two were among the secondary confirmations supporting his mutation-supply result. The third belonged to Vargas.

“What am I looking at?”

“I may have grouped these incorrectly before.”

Bob almost smiled. “That probably makes two of us then.”

Adrian enlarged the old Zenith notes. Earlier crews had marked places where seepage undermined anchors or forced cables away from ground that had looked solid. Beside those notes he opened the recorder’s pressure history, with the gaps and sensor warnings still attached. In more than one old event, pressure appeared to rise in one place while the visible outlet weakened somewhere else.

The clocks did not line up precisely enough to trace every event from one sensor to the next. But Bob could see the possibility: water finding a route underground before anyone saw a new surface channel.

Adrian drew a loose band across the map. It crossed all three dots.

“These may not be three separate histories,” he said. “They may have shared drainage intermittently.”

Bob stopped looking at the bench. “Shared how?”

“I don’t know yet. Not continuously. Possibly only when rising pressure sent water into a different route. But if water moved between these sectors before or during the biological transitions, they are less independent than we treated them.”

Oh shit.

Bob pulled the tablet closer. “Those two are in my replication set.”

“Yes.”

“You think I counted one hydrological history twice.”

“Possibly more than twice.”

Bob looked at Adrian.

The answer did not improve.

His held-out Taylor compartment was not one of the highlighted sites. Good. The result that had survived their deliberate test did not disappear because a drainage map had become uglier.

But Bob had also leaned on the same broad order repeating elsewhere. He and Adrian had already reduced the count where the organisms shared too much recent ancestry. These sites had survived that check. They had different outlets and recorded chemical histories; he had followed the biological samples through each transition.

Different organisms could still have received the same pulse of water. Water could carry cells between sites, too. If that had happened during the periods that mattered, what looked like nature repeating an experiment might be one connected event sampled in several places.

“How finished is this?”

“It isn’t.”

“Good.”

Adrian waited.

Bob pointed at the east station below them. “What would make you believe there’s a connection here?”

“If pressure continues rising here while the visible outlet weakens, and the old-brine intake begins showing chemistry or sediment from the neighboring flow. That would be difficult to explain as simple surface migration.”

“And if nothing crosses?”

“The larger grouping weakens.”

Bob nodded once. “Then test it.”

Adrian looked at him. “Including your replication set?”

“Especially my replication set.”

Bob opened the field channel. “Sofia, close the east bench and the ground below it. Nobody goes onto the sediment. Get the completed cartridges off the rack remotely and bring rover two to the rock above us.”

Sofia repeated the limits back.

Adrian waited until the channel closed. “I recommend retrieving the station now.”

Bob looked down at the station. One intake still drew the older dark brine. The other sampled the clearer, more oxygenated water beside it. The value was not the machinery. It was that both sides were being recorded in the same place on the same clock while they were still separate.

If Adrian was right about hidden connectivity, the paired record might catch the last separate interval and the moment it ended. Pulling now would break that sequence. Waiting might cost them the station.

“Rig it for remote retrieval,” Bob said. “Keep it running while the intakes still disagree. The moment the old line picks up sediment or starts moving toward the new chemistry, pull it.”

“The buried route may change before the surface does.”

“I know.”

“I would still retrieve now.”

“I know that too.”

Sofia confirmed that the completed cartridges were off the rack and safely on rock. Bob and Adrian moved farther uphill while her crew brought the rover around and chocked it on the rock above the bench. The recovery line ran from its winch through a rock anchor to the station, with a sacrificial link that would fail before a moving slab could drag the rover after it. Nobody had to step onto the sediment to bring the frame back. Everyone stayed above the bench and clear of the line.

Then they watched. For a while, Taylor refused to become interesting.

The station stayed level. The visible outlet kept running, and the cloudy stain behind the bench did not grow much. Wind worked grit across the rock around Bob’s boots. He flexed his fingers inside his gloves and checked the intakes again.

His first interpretation was trying to retake the room in his head. Maybe the water had found a shallow side route. Maybe the old records made the current changes look more alike than they were. Below him, the station went on filling the cartridge he had chosen to leave there.

“Anything?” Bob asked.

“The hidden connection is more likely than when we arrived. I still can’t place the route tightly.”

“How likely are we to lose the station?”

“I can produce a number.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

“Given the unknowns, I don’t think the number would help.”

Bob glanced over. “Right.”

He kept the historical overlay beside the live feed. It could suggest where to look; it could not show him what was happening under the bench.

Then the old-brine intake went cloudy.

The change was small enough that Bob almost missed it on the camera. A gray plume threaded through the dark line. A few seconds later its conductivity fell toward the reading from the neighboring flow. The old brine was becoming less salty.

Adrian put the traces together. The visible outlet had not changed enough to explain either shift. Bob felt the argument end.

“Pull it.”

Adrian stopped sampling and engaged the winch. The line tightened. The station resisted, then slid a short distance toward the rock.

“Slow,” Bob said.

It moved again. For a moment he thought they had it.

Then a broad patch of the bench settled.

The frozen surface did not split like lake ice. It simply dropped several centimeters, as if somebody had removed part of the ground underneath. The station tipped downhill. A dark wet seam opened high along the back of the shelf, where frozen sediment met rock.

There was the route.

Water rushed along the exposed contact, washing fine material out from beneath the frozen top. The surface had stayed hard while the support beneath it went missing.

The winch could move the station. It could not put the ground back.

“Stop.”

Adrian stopped the winch, but the bench kept settling. Water pushed through the opened seam. A slab of ice-cemented sediment rotated away from the rock, taking the station with it. One intake tore loose. The recovery line loaded hard enough to rock the rover against its chocks.

Then the sacrificial link broke exactly where it was meant to.

The safe-side line went slack. Dark brine, clearer meltwater, and gray sediment poured through the same opening. The station rolled onto one side and disappeared into the new channel as the lower bench slumped around it.

It reminded Bob of watching a melting iceberg flip, only faster. The movement was mostly over within a minute. He stared at the water. The station was gone, but that was not the part that held his attention.
