Bob walked over to Adrian and put his unburned hand against the safe warm panel at his side.

"Inventory."

Previous← The Major-Transition HypothesisNext · Beat 16The Kludge and Vargas's Offer →
Extra Weights / Beat 16

The Kludge and Vargas's Offer

"Let's see what's left," Bob said.

Adrian turned toward the blackened work area. The wind had flattened most of the smoke; what remained came off the burned shelter in thin gray ribbons and vanished across the gravel. Beyond it, the molecular-analysis skid sat where they had abandoned it, one corner buried in the bent pallet rail.

"The analysis skid is gone," Adrian said.

"I can see that."

"You lifted the end that was already free."

Bob took his right hand off Adrian's warm side panel. "Excuse me?"

They went inside the operations shelter, where Adrian brought up the fire recording and enlarged the rear-left runner. There was Bob in smoke and bad light, planted at the front crossbar. The skid rose exactly as he remembered it rising, enough to make him think he had beaten it. But the rear corner did not move. A bent retaining rail had folded over the runner, and as Bob pulled higher, the frame rotated around that trapped point and drove it harder into the bind.

"The mass wasn't the problem," Adrian said. "The rear runner was."

"It weighed a hundred and sixty kilos."

"Yes."

Bob watched the clip again. "You don't know what that pull felt like."

"No."

Adrian traced one short path across the image: get to the rear corner, pry the runner sideways over the lip, hold it clear, then tow.

Bob watched the geometry settle into something irritatingly obvious. The front had moved because the front could move. The back had stayed because the back couldn't.

"Yeah," he said.

Adrian closed the recording without adding anything.

Bob looked toward the ruined skid. The explanation was annoying, but it was better than the one his body had supplied while the shelter burned: not enough.

He flexed his bandaged left hand inside the glove and turned back to Adrian. "All right. Let's see what we can do for you."

Bob gathered tools. If they could repair Adrian's cooling system well enough for travel, they could follow the others to Vargas's camp.

The power pod had gone with the crew; a charged field battery remained with the repair gear. Adrian could think, talk, walk, and run modest loads on his surviving onboard batteries. Getting rid of the heat from using them was the problem.

That became obvious as soon as Bob opened the rear access panels. The crushed coolant branch disappeared behind folded structure and scorched power hardware; one manifold was flattened, a heat-exchanger line had kinked hard against its mount, and the local sensor harness was burned through in the same region. Bob spent twenty minutes looking for a clean route into it before accepting that a proper repair would require dismantling more of Adrian's rear structure than he could reliably rebuild with field tools and one good hand.

"We are not doing that," Bob said.

"Agreed."

"I wasn't asking."

"I know."

The old system was gone. Fine. Bob had a few ideas for how to work with that.

They dragged the surviving repair hardware into the lee of the operations shelter and spread it across a ground sheet: armored hose, a compact redundant pump, two finned radiator panels, cold plates, quick-disconnects, clamps, brackets, spare flow sensors, thermocouples, conductive straps, and enough miscellaneous hardware to offend every engineer who had originally designed it for something else.

Bob picked up the pump. "This survived?"

"You complained about its mass at McMurdo."

"I complain about all mass."

"You restored it to the load after I removed it."

Bob looked at him. "Excellent decision. Whoever made it."

They could not push coolant through the crushed branch, so they would go around it. Bob clamped a cold plate to a surviving hot-side interface near the damaged power electronics, then routed hose from there through the external pump and across the two radiator panels before returning it to the remaining loop.

"It should go higher," Bob said.

"Lower."

"Your old model put the peak here."

"My old model trusted sensors that no longer exist. Under sustained conversion load the surviving gradient puts the worst region lower."

Bob shifted the plate. "There?"

"Better."

Bob set the bracket beside the plate. The missing sensor channels were still blank on the display.

"You told me we couldn't verify the hottest point," he said. "Twice. I understood you. Then I used the readings around it as if we could."

"I relied on your field judgment," Adrian said. "I still couldn't verify the bound."

Bob looked into the scorched bay. "That interval made me feel safer. We were getting results, the temperatures looked right."

"The readings were real. They just didn't cover the failure."

"I know. I decided we should keep going. Your power bay burned, and we lost the lab."

Adrian still held the cold plate against the lower interface. "We were overconfident in your model."

Bob opened a fresh set of test channels beside the temperature estimate. "Then let's give you something you can check."

They left the burned power channel isolated and traced the surviving heat paths into the bypass. Bob fitted thermocouples at the hot-side contacts feeding the bypass, then added flow sensors at the pump inlet and outlet and a temperature probe on the coolant return. He checked the pressure of each plate against its hot surface. A running pump was no help if coolant failed to reach the contact. He wanted to see the heat entering and exiting the loop under control.

One contact disappeared behind the first bracket he tried. Bob took the bracket off and turned it so the sensor and its lead remained accessible. The destroyed internal channels stayed blank. The new sensors would tell them how the new cooling path behaved under load, not what every damaged part inside Adrian was doing.

Adrian held the plate while Bob secured the revised bracket. The first radiator mount interfered with Adrian's shoulder; the second cleared the shoulder but caught the return hose when he crouched. The third worked, though the panels made him nearly eighty centimeters wide at the hips—wider than half the equipment gaps in camp.

When the assembly finally sat in place, two black radiator panels spanned Adrian's back and lower torso, with armored hoses looping around the damaged flank to the pump.

Bob stepped back. "You look like a tank."

"Does my appearance matter?"

"What's Echo going to say?"

Bob crouched to tighten the lower bracket. His right wrist had started to ache from doing the work of two hands, and when he used the injured left to steady the fitting, pain flashed under the bandage. He shifted away from it and changed his angle.

"Stop," Adrian said.

"What?"

"You're protecting the left hand and losing alignment on the right."

"That's called adaptation."

"It's making the clamp worse."

The wrench slipped off the fitting and barked Bob's knuckles against the bracket.

Adrian waited.

"Coincidence," Bob said.

"If Sofia were doing this with a fresh burn and a shifting dressing, would you let her continue?"

Bob stared at him. "No."

"Then fix your hand."

Adrian handed him the medical pouch.

Bob sat on a field case and pulled off the glove. The outer wrap had crept toward his wrist and stiffened in the cold. He loosened it and worked a fresh pad into place with his right hand. When he reached for the wrap, the pad slipped. He tried again, pinning it with his thumb, but couldn't wind the roll without letting go.

He looked up at Adrian. "Hold that, will you?"

Adrian held the pad while Bob started the wrap, then took the roll and finished securing it. Bob flexed his fingers, added a dry layer, and tucked the hand back into insulation.

A few minutes later his grip was still unpleasant, but better. He finished the clamp cleanly.

"Thank you," Bob said.

"Echo will accept me as long as I am operational."

They went back to work, checking each hose connection before starting the bypass at the lowest useful load. The pump vibrated through the hose in Bob's right palm. Flow registered on both sides of it. One radiator warmed, then the other, and the return temperature began to rise. Bob watched the contact temperatures climb. The rise slowed and held while coolant kept moving through the plates.

Bob had Adrian walk ten meters and back, crouch, reach, and rotate through the range the new hardware had to survive. One quick-disconnect rubbed against a panel edge, and Bob rerouted it. The left radiator bracket cleared Adrian's shoulder by less than he liked, so he changed that too.

They checked limited charging from the field battery through Adrian's surviving power interface, then disconnected it and added load: a twenty-kilogram case, a short compute burst, a fifty-kilogram carry across camp. Each time Adrian worked, the temperatures rose. Each time he stopped, the radiators dumped heat into air cold enough to hurt Bob's face while the numbers came back down.

Slowly.

That was the new fact. The bypass could jettison excess heat; it simply could not do it as fast as the old system.

Bob increased compute while Adrian stood still. The temperatures rose faster than before the accident, crossed Bob's first warning line, then leveled when Adrian throttled. They waited until the margin returned, though not all of it did.

Bob checked the pump housing, plate pressure, and external thermocouples, then put his right hand on the safe panel at Adrian's side. Warm. Stable.

"Try movement plus analysis."

Adrian walked while running a moderate comparison. The temperatures climbed faster than either load alone.

"Stop."

He stopped.

"So that's the game now," Bob said.

Movement, force, compute, sensing, communication, charging—different ways of spending the same thermal margin. At Vargas's camp, the pod could give Adrian plenty of energy. Adrian could not always afford the heat produced by using it.

"Then preserve sensing and analysis before force," Adrian said.

Bob looked at him. "Why those?"

"The west-fracture question is still open. I can move more slowly. If another transient comparison appears, I want enough margin to understand it."

Bob glanced at the field board. There was no next west-fracture comparison on it yet.

"I can work," Adrian said.

Bob looked up. That, apparently, was the metric Adrian cared about: not whether the repair looked good, and certainly not whether he was back to factory condition, but whether he could still be useful.

"You can work in bursts," Bob said. "And we cool before the margin gets clever. If a hose kinks, a pump note changes, or a reading disagrees with your model, we stop. If you think the limits are wrong, we stop then too."

"Agreed."

"Trust the disagreement first. Explain it later."

"Yes."

Adrian turned toward the surviving equipment cases and the west-fracture data still open inside the shelter. "Then the expedition can use me again."

Bob smiled despite himself. "Let's see if the expedition still exists."

For the next half hour, Bob made it exist on a screen.

The living comparative series was gone, but not everything had gone with it. They still had transmitted microscopy, sequence data, frozen extracts, Ortega's advanced organelle-like material, and Vargas's matched lower-fracture replicate. The west fracture itself was still out there too, and somewhere in the back of Bob's mind remained the possibility that another living series existed in it.

That was not a plan. He had no safe route window, no crew to send, and no intention of turning the thought into somebody else's risk. But he had not discarded it.

Repair Adrian. Inventory. Join Vargas. That was still the plan. First Bob wanted to know what they were joining her with.

He cut the remaining objectives down to the smallest version that still mattered: replacement west-fracture material if a defensible window opened, preservation of the surviving controls, the endpoint comparison, and deep molecular analysis through Vargas's functioning systems. Bob treated that last part as a loan.

Even granting another safe window, the first draft required one rover from Vargas and a processing slot at her camp. Adrian ran the schedule; fresh living material would age out before processing. Bob changed the route; the next version preserved the sample but missed a control. He changed the order, which forced Adrian to travel, manipulate equipment, and analyze without enough cooling time. Cutting local compute moved the bottleneck to Vargas's queue.

"Fine. Borrow two people."

Adrian added Sofia and Ortega. The plan closed—until Vargas's schedule appeared beside it. Taking them broke her upper-site collection window. Different people broke a different window. Borrowing only a rover stranded one of Vargas's teams, and the preservation rack was already committed to the material her camp had collected while Bob's burned.

Bob tried another arrangement, then another. Every time he made his operation work, some necessary capability disappeared from hers.

Adrian said nothing. He left the failed plans side by side on the display while the bypass pump dropped to its low, steady cooling note.

Bob stared at the last version a moment longer, then looked at him. "What? Just say it."

Adrian waited until the temperature line had begun to fall.

"Your camp can support limited field work. It cannot complete the remaining mission independently."

Bob leaned back.

The smaller operations shelter was warm enough. They had communications, rovers, survival equipment, instruments, data, frozen material, repair hardware, and the field battery. He could keep people alive here, collect things here, and do science here.

What he could not do was run the full chain anymore: collect, preserve, analyze, compare, house, power, move, and repeat quickly enough while Taylor changed around them.

His camp was not ruined.

His expedition was.

A call request appeared from Vargas. Bob considered letting it ring, then accepted.

"How many?" she asked.

"How many what?"

"Plans."

Bob glanced at Adrian. "Did you tell her?"

"No."

Vargas said, "I know you."

"Six."

"Only six?"

"Adrian overheats when we try to add more." Bob raised his bandaged hand to the camera.

"Good. I wanted to catch you before seven."

"What do you want?"

"You. Adrian. Your science. The power pod. Whatever useful hardware survived."

Bob frowned. "That sounds suspiciously like a fire sale acquisition."

"It's a merger."

"Nope."

"Fine. We can negotiate it."

Vargas waited.

Bob looked at the last plan still open on the screen. He had already handed over people, processing, transport, and half the assumptions that made the operation his. What Vargas was asking for now was the boundary itself. Endosymbiosis.

Bob closed his eyes. He was tired.

What were his options?

Vargas put her analysis queue beside his failed plan. She had the shelter, drying capacity, cold chain, and molecular systems he needed. And her teams were bringing back material faster than Echo and those systems could turn it into next-day field decisions. Several promising collections were waiting on comparisons that would tell her where to send people next.

They could use the extra fire power.

Bob followed the backlog down the screen. There was enough work for everyone and too many branches to pursue before Taylor closed them. More samples would not fix that.

"I need your resources," she said.

Bob raised an eyebrow. "Sounds romantic."

"Your mutation-supply result changes what we think is actually being generated in these populations. Your conflict-control work changes which integrations I believe can persist. And you're the one who keeps asking whether my beautiful progression is history or just four things arranged in a pleasing order."

"You have Echo."

"Yes."

"Echo is good."

"Yes."

"So what do you need Adrian for?"

"Echo has my history. Adrian has yours. They notice different things. I want both. Come on, I don't have to explain it."

Bob looked at Adrian. "We're being collected."

"You are going to try to borrow my expedition one component at a time," Vargas said, "while insisting that it preserves your independence. I am offering you to skip the inefficient part so we can work at full speed."

Bob opened his latest plan. "Loan me the preservation rack and one rover for thirty-six hours."

"My upper team loses the rover."

"Then my samples come to you for preservation."

"And when your living material and mine arrive at the same time?"

"Preallocate instrument time."

"Based on which field priorities? Yours or mine?"

"Shared."

"Under which command?"

Bob looked at her. Vargas waited.

He tried a different angle. "Two field teams. Shared analysis. Separate operations."

"Then when one team needs the same transport window as the other, who decides?"

"We write a rule."

"And when the rule doesn't represent the sample we haven't seen yet?"

Bob said nothing.

"One field command," Vargas said. "One safety authority. Shared transport, shared preservation, shared sampling plan."

"Yours."

"Yes."

There it was. The part he disliked.

"I don't work for you."

"Scientifically? No."

"Operationally?"

"Yes."

Bob blew out a breath. He had spent half an hour proving her right and still hated hearing it.

Vargas continued before he could turn it into an argument. "You keep interpretive equality. You keep your own hypotheses. You tell me when my model is wrong. I tell you when yours is wrong. Echo and Adrian surface disagreement instead of averaging it away. Shared data. Shared publication decisions. One sampling strategy while we still have a glacier to sample."

Bob leaned closer to the display. "And safety?"

"Mine. Explicitly."

"Including over me."

"Especially over you."

"That sounds personal."

"It's evidence-based."

Bob looked away from her and at the combined schedule Adrian had already begun assembling: Vargas's people; Bob's people, now at her camp; her molecular systems and cold storage; his surviving samples, mechanisms, and controls; Echo's developmental and network history; Adrian's Taylor-trained judgment and the damaged body they had just made field-capable; the power pod now at her camp; the remaining rovers.

One glacier.

With one set of field priorities, Adrian could fit the teams around the same transport and preservation slots. Some collections moved later; some lower-priority work came off the board. The remaining plan held together. They had not gained resources. They had stopped assigning the same scarce ones to two supposedly independent systems.

Bob's attention returned to the old expedition board. He had spent a career making systems smaller when he could: fewer interfaces, fewer dependencies, fewer things another person could misunderstand at exactly the wrong time. Usually that was good design.

Here, the boundary itself had become expensive.

"You pulled me out of a hole," Vargas said.

Bob snorted. "I still owe you for Greenland."

"Then we can stop keeping score."

"That seems premature."

"Bob."

He looked at her.

Pause.

"Operational command is yours," he finally said.

"Yes."

"Safety authority is yours."

"Yes."

"Science is equal."

"Absolutely yes."

"No interpretation becomes true because you control the radios."

"Agreed."

"Shared data. Shared publication decisions."

"Agreed."

Bob pointed at Adrian. "His thermal limits are hard constraints. If he says stop, or my external sensors say stop, we stop."

"Obviously."

"I reserve the right to complain."

Vargas smiled. "I assumed that was biologically fixed."

Bob looked at Adrian. "You have anything useful to add before I make a terrible mistake?"

"The combined plan has lower human exposure and preserves more of the remaining science."

"Traitor."

"I didn't know independence was one of the scientific objectives."

Bob stared at him for a second, then looked back at Vargas.

"Fine. One expedition."

Vargas nodded once and immediately changed the field assignments. Of course she did.

Teams shifted on Bob's screen. Transport windows merged. Sample queues merged. Bob's controls appeared beside Vargas's state measurements instead of in a separate branch. Echo joined the shared analytical channel, and Adrian moved the west-fracture replacement requirements into the common field plan.

"One more thing," Vargas said. "Foster has the incident summary. He wants your account of why work continued after Adrian's warnings."

Bob groaned.

"The decision was mine," he said. "I'll answer him when we're with you."

"Get here first," Vargas said.

The next problem arrived almost at once: a distant team would have one small structured sample and two incompatible first treatments.

Bob read the note, then glanced at Adrian's radiators. One of the lower hoses had shifted half a centimeter against its guard. He crouched, moved it back into place, and checked the clamp pressure with his right hand while Adrian stood still for him.

"We should leave for Vargas's camp after the next cooling cycle," Bob said.

"Yes."

That had been the plan from the beginning: repair Adrian, inventory, join the others. It had just taken Bob longer than expected to understand what they were joining.

He closed the old expedition board.

Previous← The Accident and FireNext · Beat 17One Expedition →
Extra Weights / Beat 17

One Expedition

Bob had been at Vargas's camp long enough to learn where his surviving sample cases had been put but not long enough to accept it.

His old expedition board was gone. One wall now held the combined field map: shared rovers, shared preservation slots, three teams moving under one schedule. Adrian stood beside the operations table with the external radiator panels clear of his back and the bypass pump running at its low, steady note. Outside, the power pod remained on its sled beside the camp power bus with the rest of the heavy infrastructure.

Then Team A called from the west-fracture replacement transect.

Ortega appeared first on the field camera, bent over a clear collection cassette. Jonas stood beside him at the portable microscope. Two days ago they had belonged to different expeditions.

“Which half do you want?” Ortega asked.

Bob looked at the image. “Neither. Don't cut it.”

Across the table, Vargas looked up. “Strong opening to shared command.”

“Tell them not to cut it.”

Ortega raised one gloved hand. “Still here, Bob.”

The laminated chip was only a few centimeters across, split by a thin seam of native brine. Under magnification, nearly everything useful sat in one narrow band: host cells along a chemical boundary, bacterial partners packed against them, and a few rare internal bodies that might belong to the intermediate states lost in the fire.

The sample looked divisible until you cared what was in it.

Bob wanted part of that band preserved exactly as it had arrived. Vargas wanted part of the same band alive and undisturbed long enough to see how the host and partners were organized. If Bob got his first choice, some of Vargas's evidence died. If Vargas got hers, some of Bob's baseline stopped being a baseline.

“Keep it sealed,” Bob said. “No chamber treatment yet.”

Vargas came around the table. “And every minute it sits there, the living state changes anyway.”

“I know.”

“So we have a clock.”

“We always had a clock.”

“Yes, but now you're sharing it.”

Jonas smiled faintly on the field feed.

Bob pointed at the central band. “If the whole thing goes into the live chamber first, a rare variant can start multiplying while we're watching something else. Then I can't tell how rare it was when we collected it.”

“And if you preserve the whole useful band first,” Vargas said, “I lose the arrangement among the living cells before I can measure it.”

“I didn't say the whole band.”

“I need intact cells before you turn everything into soup.”

“I do not turn everything into soup.”

“Your lab owns several machines whose entire purpose is to turn biology into soup.”

“For measurement.”

“Of soup.”

Vargas turned toward Echo and Adrian. “Don't give me a group recommendation. Show us what each of you thinks we're about to destroy.”

Echo marked the part of the band where the host cells and their partners still formed one intact living structure. When Adrian added the untreated comparison Bob needed, the two maps almost landed on top of each other.

Jonas leaned closer to his field display. “So the good part is the same part.”

“That's the problem,” Bob said.

Echo shifted a proposed halfway cut. One side gained most of the intact living arrangement; the other gained rock. When Adrian shifted it the other way, Bob's supposed untreated control moved away from the biological contact he actually cared about.

Vargas looked at the overlay. “The halves are fictional.”

Bob saw it then. They were trying to divide the rock when what mattered was the pattern inside it. “Map the band first,” he said.

Ortega checked the microscope. “Without adding anything?”

“Exactly.”

Jonas kept the chip sealed in its own brine while Ortega ran a quick surface image. The living colony did not form a neat stripe. It bent diagonally across the chip, and the rare internal cells sat near one end of that bend. A second angle clarified the exposed edge but left part of the contact hidden under rock.

“More?” Ortega asked.

Bob wanted to see the hidden edge. He wanted the time back, too.

Adrian sharpened the reconstruction, then his thermal margin dropped fast enough for Bob to notice.

“That's enough,” Bob said. “Drop the heavy model.”

Adrian reduced the load, and Echo kept the spatial overlay running while the pump deepened briefly and then settled. The buried edge stayed marked uncertain, but they had enough to avoid the worst cut.

“Don't cut it into halves,” Bob said. “Take a narrow untouched strip through the contact.”

Vargas pointed to the adjoining section. “Please keep this end alive. We need the cells still attached to one another.”

Echo marked a thin strip between them that could be fixed in place to preserve where everything had been.

The overlay now showed three unequal pieces for three different questions. Bob wanted his untouched strip wider; Vargas wanted more living material. Ortega did not have enough chip to give either of them what they wanted.

“Enough intact structure for the response measurements?” Vargas asked Echo.

“For the first comparison. Not the full set.”

She turned to Adrian. “And the narrow strip?”

“Enough for the baseline screen. Not a duplicate of the live fraction.”

“No,” Bob said. “Adjacent isn't identical. Keep the positions.”

Vargas studied the cut plan before making the allocation. “Bob's strip stays narrow,” she said. “Enough for the untreated baseline, not every assay. Jonas gets the intact end for the live work. Ortega fixes the strip between them. Record exactly what each piece can no longer answer.”

Bob looked at the narrow preserved section. He would have taken more, and Vargas caught his expression. “Operational command.”

“I'm aware.”

“Science still equal?”

“If your live fraction becomes uninterpretable, I reserve the right to be unbearable.”

“I would be disappointed by anything less.”

“Let's do it.”

Ortega made the cuts. The first strip went straight into preservation, and the middle strip was fixed and its position recorded. Jonas kept the remaining end alive in native brine. On the feed, the hosts stayed attached to their partners.

Bob's fraction was smaller than he wanted, and Vargas lost part of the live experiment she had planned. One molecular assay came off the schedule entirely. They had not solved the scarcity, but they had made the loss explicit before destroying the evidence by accident.

Ortega looked up. “How do you want these labeled?”

“By what happened to them,” Vargas said, “not A, B, and C.”

Bob nodded. “And where they came from.”

Ortega entered the three records.

Vargas pushed the mapped cut plan to the other teams. “If a structured sample doesn't really have two equivalent pieces, stop. Map what matters before you divide it.”

Bob nearly added three more rules, then checked Ortega's manifest. His distinctions were already there.

“No additions,” he said.

Vargas glanced at him. “That's growth.”

“Don't ruin it.”

When the call ended, Bob stood with his right hand on the edge of Vargas's operations table. The sample that might replace part of the bridge evidence lost in the fire was two kilometers away, already cut, already preserved, and still scientifically useful.

He had not touched it, and he still wanted to reopen Ortega's handling record and walk through the cut again frame by frame. His hand moved toward the display.

“Do you want the full manipulation record?” Adrian asked.

Bob looked at what they already had. The coordinates matched. The untreated strip had stayed untreated. Vargas's live fraction was still interpretable. The limitations were written down instead of hidden inside a clean label.

“Did anything violate the plan? A provenance gap?” Bob asked.

“No.”

Bob took his hand off the control. “Then no.”

Ortega had done harder work for him for years. Trusting him with a knife was easy. Leaving Vargas's allocation alone took more effort, even when Bob could find nothing wrong with the handling. His distinctions had survived without his getting every decision.

He kept his hand off the display.

The next structured sample came from Team B less than an hour later. This time nobody asked Bob which half he wanted. They sent a mapped image with the trade already marked: the part that had to remain untouched, the part Vargas could keep alive, and the part neither question would get enough of.

Bob zoomed in and found himself searching for the mistake. Adrian found one first: the proposed untreated strip crossed outside the chemical boundary. Echo pointed out that moving it inward would take too much of the only intact living cluster. The team redrew the cut before either Bob or Vargas told them how.

Vargas chose the smaller untreated fraction and dropped one live-state comparison from the plan. Bob objected to the first choice, accepted the second, and then stopped because the limitations were already written into the record.

By midday, other people were using the method without having to repeat the first argument.

Team A moved farther west. Team B continued toward the upper endpoint pocket. Sofia and Lena Sørensen worked lesser exposures a few hundred meters from camp, close enough to reach on foot with portable cases. The board no longer marked which expedition a rover or person had belonged to before the fire. It only showed what needed doing next.

Bob still corrected labels, challenged bad comparisons, and rejected anything somebody tried to call a control without earning the word. What he did not do was require every cassette to come back through his hands. The expedition kept working anyway.

By early afternoon, a small batch of sequence reads was ready from the morning's preserved west-fracture fractions. A rover had brought the preserved strips back while Team A continued the transect, and the camp instruments had been working through them since. The living fractions remained with Jonas. One of the later chips held an unusually good cluster: partners still attached around hosts containing the rare internal bodies.

Bob wanted to separate the external partners, measure what failed, then put them back. Vargas had added a comparison with a closely related system. If the two responded differently, they might learn something about the step toward greater dependence. First they had to choose a suitable relative. Looking alike under a microscope wasn't enough.

Echo prepared a search of the new genetic reads against the expedition's accumulated sequence records. The preserved strips were mapped to the living material beside them; the result could guide their choice, though it could not make adjacent pieces identical. Bob checked the sample records and work estimate, then submitted the search against the extra computing time SAGE had approved.

The data arrived, but Foster had put the search on hold.

Vargas finished answering Team B and turned to him. “Jonas is ready. What's holding up the comparison?”

“Foster.” Bob put the hold on the shared display. “He's blocked our run on SAGE's computers until I finish another report.”

“About the fire?”

“That went in when we got here. This one's about who thought of what—Adrian or me.”

Vargas read the condition. “Can we do the search here?”

“The full search would make other live samples wait,” Bob said. “We need SAGE's machines to choose Jonas's comparison in time.”

“I can try the likeliest relatives first,” Echo said. “It may be enough.”

“Try it,” Vargas said. “Adrian, help within your tested limit. Bob, put Foster on with both of us.”

Bob got Foster on the line after sending him the decision log with the attributions he could already establish. “You have the work record. I'll finish the reconstruction tonight. Let the search run.”

“This log tells me what got done,” Foster replied. “I need your assessment of Adrian's independent decisions, Bob, not his account of himself. I have to evaluate his development before I can certify this job.”

On the field feed, Jonas had tucked one gloved hand under his arm while he waited at the microscope. The image showed partners pulling away from the host cells in the untouched control. Vargas checked it against the earlier image and confirmed that the holding conditions hadn't changed.

“The control is starting to come apart,” she told Foster. “If we wait, we lose the experiment.”

“You'll have that account tonight,” Bob said. “This control won't last that long. Release the job.”

“I've recorded your assessment. It does not satisfy the reporting condition.”

Bob sent the provisional report to Eli Park, who tried to get the job released against it. “The machine time is available,” Eli said when he came back. “You're within the allocation. They still won't start without Foster's certification. I've asked for an exception on Vargas's timing assessment.”

The exception was still unanswered when Echo found a plausible match. But by then the control had come apart. Bob told Jonas to preserve what remained; they could keep the DNA and images, but the experiment they had prepared for was gone.

“We had it ready,” Jonas said. His hand stayed beside the cassette. “Tell SAGE they screwed us. They kept us waiting until it was gone.”

Ortega started putting away the unused equipment. “And next they'll want to know why we didn't get more done.”

“I know.” Bob kept looking at the image. All that care over the cuts, and Foster had left them waiting with the equipment ready.

Foster classified the reported loss as an expedition execution shortfall. Vargas read it over Bob's shoulder. “That team did what we asked. He doesn't get to put this on them.” She added her confirmation as Bob sent the job history and images to the committee.

“His refusal is in there too,” Adrian said. “After both your warnings.”

Bob closed Foster's reply before he said something useless or worse.

Adrian had spent most of the day at the table, trading movement for analysis. He was cooling slowly enough now that Bob called a longer pause and crouched behind him to inspect the lower hose. Where it passed around the damaged flank, the flexing guard had left a faint polished mark. There was no cut yet.

“How you doing?” Bob asked.

“The pause is helping.”

“I don't suppose you can hack into Foster and get him to actually help us for once?”

“Foster is an excellent trainer,” Adrian said. “I think he is trying to be useful to SAGE. His objectives aren't ours, and he hasn't worked with people enough to know how to handle that.”

“At our expense.” Bob loosened the bracket and shifted the guard a few millimeters with his right hand. He asked Adrian to check the flow and clamp pressure; both stayed stable.

Bob started to brace the fitting with his bandaged left hand.

“Don't,” Adrian said.

Bob moved the hand away. “I was checking whether you were paying attention.”

“I am.”

He tightened the bracket one-handed.

On the shared wall, Ortega and Jonas were working the next west-fracture interval. Team B had paused below the upper pocket. Vargas and Echo were reviewing the live trace from the first chip.

With the immediate queue clear, Bob noticed a small image Adrian had left open at the edge of his display. It had no active task attached. “What's that?”

“A nearby shale face.”

Bob knew it. Dark layered rock, old fractures, several damp points along the lower slope. It sat a short walk below camp. They had looked at it before and found nothing worth stealing time from the stronger sites.

“What's there?”

“Three outlets have changed since the last imaging pass.”

Bob enlarged the image. One outlet carried a faint iron stain. One was darker. The third was nearly clear. Different water, apparently, emerging along the same lower band.

“Show me why you're holding them together.”

Adrian overlaid the older fracture map. The outlets did not share one visible crack, but they stepped along the shale contact in a way that might point to something buried behind the face.

“One buried pathway could intersect several pockets,” Adrian said. “Or unrelated flows could be using the same weak layer.”

“Do you have enough to recommend a diversion?” Bob asked.

“No. I don't have enough geometry. I would leave both distant teams where they are.”

Bob checked the shared board. The image wasn't there. “Have you sent this to the channel?”

“Not yet,” Adrian said. “It is unfinished, low priority, and I do not recommend action.”

Which was exactly the filter Bob had spent months teaching him. Don't fill the channel with weak associations. Bring Bob the thing when it can survive an argument or change a decision. It had made the two of them fast, but it had also made their blind spots private.

“Send it,” Bob said.

“To Vargas and Echo?”

“To the shared channel. Mark it weak. I want to know what we're missing before we decide it means nothing.”

Adrian opened the channel.

“Echo. Elena. Adrian has something I'd like you to look at,” Bob said.

Vargas looked up from the live trace. “Decision-relevant?”

“Not yet.”

“Good. Show us before you make it pretty.”

Adrian sent the unfinished grouping. Echo added older moisture history from Vargas's side. The combined view made one buried structure a little more plausible. It still did not prove one.

Vargas left the distant teams where they were.

“Nearby team only,” she said. “Photograph the face when you're in that sector. If it changes, map it before anybody scrapes or combines anything.”

Bob closed the channel.

The little image remained on the shared board.

Later in the day, the rain began as a soft tapping on the shelter roof.

Warm by Taylor standards still meant cold enough that Bob objected to the adjective, but the temperature had climbed past the point where liquid water could start rearranging the surface.

Gravel darkened. Thin films appeared on exposed ice. Known channels picked up flow.

Vargas shortened route intervals and pulled one drone back when moisture cut its endurance. Team A added new conductivity readings instead of assuming yesterday's boundaries still held. Team B moved a preservation case uphill before a shallow runnel crossed its planned path.

Nobody treated the rain as a crisis. But it was changing the map.

Adrian gave up a planned high-resolution comparison so he could keep more communications and field sensing inside his cooling margin. Echo took the heavier cross-team analysis. Bob worked the incoming maps and let Sofia move the equipment cases he would normally have moved himself.

Near the end of the shift, Sofia called from the shale face.

The camera came up wet and slightly blurred. Behind her, the lower slope had slumped away by a few centimeters. Several narrow flows now cut through the exposed sediment. One was dark with brine. One ran much clearer. Another stained the rock orange as it spread.

“We've got five outlets now,” Sofia said. “They're not matching. Different conductivity, different color. The slump opened old fracture surfaces behind them.”

Vargas was already pulling the site map onto the main wall.

Bob looked at Sofia. “What have you sampled?”

“Nothing.”

“Scraped?”

“No.”

“Combined any of the flows?”

Sofia shook her head.

“We haven't touched it.”

Previous← The Kludge and Vargas's OfferNext · Beat 18The Map Changes →
Extra Weights / Beat 18

The Map Changes

The most promising piece of Taylor was two kilometers away, and Bob was looking at it through somebody else’s camera.

A month earlier he would have been standing over the west-fracture transect himself, asking Ortega to turn the cassette three degrees so he could see whether a wet mineral line crossed the biological contact or merely stopped beside it. Now Ortega was in the field with Jonas, Team B was still climbing toward the upper endpoint pocket, and Bob stood inside Vargas’s camp shelter beside Adrian, who could either spend his thermal margin traveling or save it for analysis. Echo could carry the established comparisons. Bob wanted Adrian cool enough to challenge the assumptions behind them.

Two black radiator panels jutted from Adrian’s back, widening him at the hips. Armored hoses looped around his damaged flank to the exposed pump, which hummed steadily. Outside the shelter, the power pod remained on its sled beside the camp power bus with the rest of the heavy infrastructure. On the wall, field video, registered sample maps, chemistry, pressure, and uncertainty arrived from people Bob could not reach over and correct with his own hands.

Eli Park’s message arrived while Ortega was adjusting the field camera. The committee had reviewed the job history Bob and Vargas had sent. Bob opened the finding expecting another request for information.

Instead, it upheld their challenge. The reporting hold had cost the opportunity they had warned Foster they would lose. The field team had prepared the comparison and tried the available alternatives; its failure to run was not an expedition execution shortfall. Foster was to correct the attribution.

Within the supplementary allocation already approved, time-critical jobs could be released on their scientific justification, provenance, work estimate, and the field lead’s confirmation of the useful interval. Foster could still require Bob’s assessment of Adrian’s development. He could no longer make finishing that retrospective account a condition of releasing the job. The budget, scientific review, safety requirements, and ordinary reporting remained.

Bob read that part twice. Then he opened Foster’s proposed correction to the record.

The planned comparison was not completed while the required developmental assessment remained outstanding.

He had removed “execution shortfall.” Bob could still see exactly where the blame had gone.

“Not quite,” he said.

Vargas leaned over to read it, then opened the original entry beside the amendment. Bob called Foster on their work channel.

“The committee has not found the assessment requirement improper,” Foster said. “Your provisional log did not satisfy it.”

“Foster asked for your assessment, Bob,” Adrian said. “The log couldn’t supply that.”

Bob closed his mouth on the argument he had been about to make. He had worked on the account after the shift; some attributions still needed checking.

“All right,” he said. “The assessment was incomplete. Leave that in. Now include the decision you made while it was incomplete.”

“Certification depended on the assessment.”

“And you kept it that way after we told you the control was failing.”

“The warning is in the record.”

“It’s in the record we sent,” Vargas said. “It isn’t in your correction.”

Ortega’s camera shifted on the other display. Jonas was setting up at the microscope again, his gloved hands busy with another cassette. Yesterday he had stood there with everything ready until the control came apart. Bob moved the new field feed beside the account of the experiment they had never run.

“Was the scientific request incomplete?” he asked.

“No,” Foster said.

“Was the machine time available?”

Adrian brought Eli’s earlier confirmation into the job history. “Within the approved allocation. Before the control failed.”

Foster did not dispute it.

Bob followed the entries down: their warning, the provisional account, the held certification. “You wanted to know who made each decision. So do I. Which decision kept this job from running?”

“I withheld certification under the reporting condition.”

“After both warnings,” Vargas said.

“Yes.”

Bob kept his right hand flat on the table. “That belongs in the correction.”

“Releasing the job would not have guaranteed a useful experimental result,” Foster said. “The selected relative might not have supported the comparison.”

“No,” Bob said. “It might have failed. We lost the chance to find out.”

He let that stand. He wanted more from Foster than a corrected sentence. He wanted the ready cassette back, and Jonas’s hand beside it, and a search result arriving while they could still use it. None of that was on offer.

“Put that in,” he said. “And put in why we couldn’t run it.”

Foster’s amendment changed on the shared display.

I withheld certification after Bob and Vargas warned that the native control would not remain usable. The scientific requirements had been met and compute was available within the allocation. My decision prevented timely access and cost the opportunity to run the planned comparison. I have withdrawn the attribution to deficient field execution. The outcome of the unperformed experiment is unknown.

Bob read it through. Vargas checked it against the original.

“That accounts for the loss,” she said. “Send it to everyone who received the shortfall assessment. Same record.”

Foster sent the correction to the committee, program operations, and the expedition channel. Bob watched the distribution appear beneath it. The correction was going out under Foster’s name, to the same people as his original assessment.

“I still require your developmental assessment,” Foster said.

“You’ll get it,” Bob said. “It won’t hold the next job.”

“No,” Foster said. “It will not.”

Eli’s confirmation from program operations followed. Bob’s retrospective assessment remained open, but qualified jobs within the allocation no longer had to wait for it. The remaining allowance had not changed.

Vargas attached Foster’s correction to the sample record. “Jonas, Luis. This goes with yesterday’s cassette.”

Jonas glanced from the microscope to his display. “Including his part?”

“Including his part.”

Ortega read it over Jonas’s shoulder. “Good. I’d hate to disappoint their productivity review twice.”

Bob laughed once, more sharply than he intended. The control was still gone. But the next team would not have to stand beside a failing one while he reconstructed a training history.

He thanked Eli, closed the dispute, and brought Adrian’s unfinished shale grouping onto the main display.

“Do the extra outlets change your grouping?”

“They give us more to check,” Adrian said. “One buried pathway is still possible. So are unrelated flows using the same layer.”

“Leave both explanations up.”

Across the wall, Ortega’s camera settled on the west fracture. He worked along the registered intervals with the sampling gear while Jonas logged conductivity, oxygen, pressure, and what material could still be preserved. Each measurement came back tied to the place on the transect where somebody had taken it.

“Show me the lower contact again,” Bob told Ortega.

Ortega moved the field camera without asking why.

For most of the morning the west-fracture replacement sequence had looked like the best chance to build new comparisons where the fire had left gaps. The chemistry still separated cleanly across several intervals. The biology was rich. The mapped layers offered several apparently separated histories, with enough surviving context to make Bob want the living comparisons.

Then the rain found a new route.

Jonas put the conductivity trace on the shared wall. “Four through seven are moving together.”

Bob enlarged the graph. “That’s too broad.”

“I know.”

Oxygen had appeared below a contact that had previously kept the lower brine reducing. Pressure changed on both sides within minutes of the same surface-flow increase. Then the mineral tracer Team A had placed in the newer water path appeared below the old boundary.

Not much tracer. Enough.

Ortega had already overlaid yesterday’s map on the live one. A connection had opened above the sequence and crossed layers that had mattered precisely because they had not been connected.

Bob looked for the escape hatch anyway. “Could it be edge flow around the cassette interval?”

“We checked both sides,” Ortega said. “Same direction.”

“Tracer contamination?”

“Blank is clean.”

Adrian added the surviving pressure history. “The timing is consistent with a new hydraulic connection. We don’t need full mixing to lose the comparison. These lower samples can no longer tell us what was here before the connection opened.”

Bob stared at the old boundary. The organisms had not become less interesting. The site had become worse at answering the question.

Ortega changed the header on the field record before Bob said anything.

MODERN CONTACT — PROVENANCE COMPROMISED.

Below it he had added: DO NOT USE AS HISTORICAL BRIDGE.

Bob read it twice.

Vargas came up beside him. “Anything you want to save?”

“The biology. Not the claim.”

She nodded toward the field feed. “Ortega?”

“Already preserving it under the new label.”

No one had made a mistake. Yesterday’s experiment had been a good experiment. Taylor had changed what it meant overnight.

Bob watched Ortega seal material that, hours earlier, might have filled some of the gaps left by the fire. The new label was already attached to the handling record. There was nothing he needed to correct, and it still cost something to watch.

Team B called before he had finished being dissatisfied with that. Their problem was almost insulting in the opposite direction.

At the upper pocket, Kira and Malik had stopped climbing and gone to work: photograph the mineral interface, map the living structure before dividing it, preserve the allocated fractions, and send the microscope feed and handling record back to camp. What those samples could answer took longer to establish.

The upper pocket was magnificent. Densely packed hosts lined a pale mineral interface. Bob kept returning to the live feed while the preserved fractions reached camp and the molecular work caught up with the images. In the division sequences, bacterial-derived bodies passed into daughter cells with a regularity the earlier material had rarely given them.

Echo matched the new reads against the expedition’s existing endpoint sequences, then checked the apparent losses against the underlying coverage. These genomes were smaller still. The protein assays strengthened the case that several proteins the internal bodies needed were being made from host-encoded genes and sent back inside. Attempts at independent culture again produced no growth under the conditions they could test.

Everything at the far end of the transition got stronger. The missing middle did not appear.

Echo placed the endpoint beside the best extracellular and early intracellular material already in the expedition record. The empty space between them remained embarrassingly empty.

Vargas pointed at the comparison. “Dependency?”

“Extreme,” Echo said.

“Reliable inheritance?”

“In the divisions we’ve observed.”

“Host control?”

“Strong.”

Bob watched one daughter cell inherit the internal bodies with irritating neatness. “And no less-integrated relatives in the pocket.”

“None we can defend as related intermediates,” Echo said.

Kira gave a tired laugh over the field feed. “So we found the best endpoint we’ve seen and it’s the wrong thing.”

“No,” Bob said. “It’s the right thing for the end. Stop asking it to explain the beginning.”

Here the bacteria were already behaving almost like parts of the larger cells. To understand how that dependence could develop, they needed related forms whose partners still lived more independently, so they could compare what had changed.

That distinction should have been obvious. It usually became obvious only after somebody found something extraordinary and wanted it to answer every question at once.

“Find us the less finished versions,” Bob said. “Partners the cells need and are starting to inherit. Enough examples to test how one becomes part of the other.”

Team B preserved the pocket as an endpoint and stopped opening nearby material in search of a sequence the site was not giving them. One drone, two preservation cases, and several people became available, and Vargas moved all of them before the upper-pocket team had finished packing.

“Send the drone to Sofia,” she said.

The lower shale face had been the weak observation yesterday. Now it was the only site whose value was increasing, and Bob brought Sofia’s feed across the wall.

Her camera showed almost nothing Bob would have chosen for a poster: dark shale, wet sediment, a shallow slump, orange iron staining beneath one outlet and black brine beneath another. Old dry fracture surfaces crossed newer wet mineral bands. Five little flows came out of the face at different heights, none large enough to look important by itself.

Sofia’s team had kept the exposed face intact, just as Vargas had asked. No scraped band, no combined bottle, no exploratory cut into the largest wet pocket.

“Still untouched?” Bob asked.

Sofia looked into the camera. “You asked me that yesterday.”

“I liked the answer.”

“Then you’ll like it again.”

The first drone pass did not improve the site’s appearance. It gave them wet rock from six angles. The second pass added surface temperature: one outlet was a little warmer than the surrounding shale, while another was almost as cold as the frozen sediment around it. Lena touched a field probe to two flows at their exposed edges and found sharply different conductivity.

The readings ruled out a uniformly mixed seep at the exposed face. They did not tell Bob why such different waters were emerging within a few meters of one another, or how recently they had shared a path.

Then the second drone climbed.

The stitched model built itself across the camp wall in ugly gray pieces: a patch of shale, a broken sediment lip, the slump, the frozen apron above it. For several seconds the software seemed to be producing a more expensive version of the mess Sofia was already standing in.

A deeper angle arrived and the model rotated. Bob stopped moving.

The five outlets were not scattered across the face. Their exposed edges stepped along the same shale contact, interrupted by blocks of older sediment that made them look unrelated from below. A wet seam higher on the slope lined up with the same structure. Then a second seam appeared beyond the visible face, behind the little ridge.

Sofia walked ten meters to her left and held up a marker pole. The widening drone view placed her against the contact on the far side of a shallow apron of ice-cemented sediment. Adrian extended the contact between the visible ends, marking the buried stretch as inferred.

What had looked like five leaks became openings into one larger shape: not one cavity or one pool, but a possible network of fractured shale, sediment lenses, brine-filled seams, and frozen barriers behind the visible face. Separate pockets could feed the outlets while new connections let water pass between some of them. The different chemistries could fit that arrangement, though the buried connections still needed testing.

The rain had not created it. The rain was drawing it in water.

Bob understood why Sofia had stopped moving. They had been crossing Taylor for weeks looking for separated histories they could compare. Here was a structure that might let them compare several side by side, close enough to map and sample before the connections changed again.

Someone near Sofia said, very quietly, “Oh my God.”

For a few seconds the shelter did nothing useful. Bob let himself look. Black shale folded beneath pale ice. Orange staining marked one flow while a darker branch vanished under sediment. Tiny wet lines picked out boundaries that had been invisible from below.

Then another thought landed. If this structure extended where the model suggested it did, some of the strange samples they had already collected might not have been isolated finds at all.

Bob leaned over the table. “Every sample within two hundred meters. Old drainage maps too.”

Adrian was already pulling them in. Echo began to summarize, but Vargas raised a hand.

“No. Disagreements first.”

Echo marked two older collections below the clearest outlet on the proposed map. Their host markers matched, but the partnerships behaved differently.

“I wouldn’t put these in one pocket yet,” he said. “The flow path could be wrong. Or the biology changed before collection.”

Adrian added three older collections. The strangest had been taken when the surface looked dry. Its reconstructed flow path led beneath Sofia’s newly exposed face.

“Those were separate sites,” Bob said.

“Separate collection sites,” Adrian said. “Several may have been receiving material from the same buried system.”

Bob looked back at the markers. He had wanted the map to connect them. He hadn’t wanted it to turn several discoveries into repeated sightings of the same thing.

“So we may have counted some of this twice.”

“As independent evidence. Yes.”

Bob started to ask for the pre-rain reconstruction. Adrian put it up before he finished. Bob brought the pressure history alongside and marked a time range to test. Adrian widened it, pointing farther back along the trace. Bob checked the earlier readings, nodded, and moved on to the sample dates. Two historical paths dropped away at once; four remained tied to the buried structure.

The collection from the dry surface no longer fit. Bob checked its date again, then left it out.

He pointed at the faintest surviving branch. “You trust that one?”

“No. I was going to exclude it from the summary.”

“Don’t. Gray it out and leave it where everyone can see why you don’t trust it.”

Adrian grayed the branch. Echo left the mismatched sample outside the grouping.

“Break the rest,” Bob told Echo.

Echo brought up Vargas’s distant-site comparisons. Similar chemistry elsewhere produced some of the same biological responses, but not the same combination of hosts, bacterial partners, and ways of living together.

“The chemistry alone doesn’t explain these matches,” Echo said.

Vargas asked for an earlier mixing pulse in the model; Sofia sent fresh measurements from the exposed edges. The surviving paths still fit.

Bob looked back at the old samples. For weeks they had been treating each rare host-partner combination as a small miracle: one odd cell here, one diluted partner series there, one damaged intermediate downstream after some event they could only partly reconstruct. Several could be pieces of this larger system leaking into view at different outlets and different times. The new map gave them places to test that possibility.

They had been holding cups under a waterfall and arguing about the drops.

Now they had a source they could test.

Bob opened the expedition-wide channel. “The raw model is going to everyone. Don’t touch an unregistered layer. I want independent chemistry, provenance attacks, and anything that says this is actually three sources pretending to be one.”

Vargas was still staring at the model. Then she grinned.

“We’ve found our holy grail.”

The old plan disappeared almost immediately. Team A began packing the west-fracture work and sending its new mixing measurements toward Sofia. Team B started down with the endpoint cases. The freed drone, preservation cases, and extra people moved toward the shale face.

This time Bob moved too. He checked Adrian’s thermal trace. The margin was inside the walking band, the onboard charge nowhere near interesting.

“Walk, then local mapping?” Bob asked.

“Yes. Echo can keep the broader comparisons.”

They left the heavy equipment and the power pod at camp. Bob carried one field case in his right hand, with the burned left protected inside its glove. Adrian carried nothing. A few hundred meters down the marked route, Sofia’s poles stepped across the shale face beneath a sagging apron of ice-cemented sediment.

The exposure looked even less impressive in person. Bob set his case beside the tarp as Ortega arrived from the west. Team B’s first people were coming down with the endpoint material; Echo was already using Team A’s mixing measurements to flag suspect pockets.

Jonas had two registered samples under the portable microscope. In one, bacteria clustered loosely around their hosts. In the other, they held tight.

“Start with those,” Vargas said from camp. “Separate the partners. See which hosts notice.”

“Wait,” Bob said.

He brought up the older collections beside the new cuts. Some of the apparent discoveries had already turned out to be repeated sightings. He wanted Adrian to combine the drainage history with the host and partner lineages before they chose where to spend the next samples.

“We could be comparing cousins and calling it a transition.”

“Then we don’t call it a transition,” Vargas said. “We find out what they need from each other.”

“And use up the material that tells us how they got there.”

“Some of it. While it’s alive.”

Bob pointed to a thin strip in the second cassette. “Hold that for the genetic map. Let’s get the order right.”

Vargas looked at Adrian’s estimate on the shared display. “One attempt. Jonas, preserve the allocated fractions and keep the living preparations ready.”

Adrian waited until the heat from the walk had cleared before starting. For the first few minutes, the model simplified: two possible paths became one; an apparent split disappeared. Then an older pressure trace admitted another connection, and the preferred sampling order changed.

“Does that move the next cut?” Bob asked.

“I can’t defend an order yet,” Adrian said. “The remaining alternatives change which samples count as independent.”

Echo had the established lineage matches ready. Those could narrow the problem; they could not settle how the old flows fitted the newly exposed structure. Adrian tested another arrangement. The next result took longer.

Bob glanced from the unfinished comparison to the independent temperature trace.

“Drop the heavy model.”

“I have.”

The trace had crossed the agreed stop line. The pump changed pitch, but the temperature kept rising for several seconds before it leveled. Adrian stood with his panels clear while Jonas waited beside the two cassettes.

Bob checked the flow and ran his right fingers along the armored hose guard. The abrasion mark had not deepened. The lower cold-plate clamp had lost a little contact pressure again.

“Can you finish after cooling?” he asked.

“I can continue. I can’t promise a useful answer before the next stop.”

Bob looked at the samples he had made everyone hold.

Vargas said, “I want Jonas to start.”

“Do it.”

“The reserved strip too. There’s enough intact structure there to repeat the separation and restoration with a matched control.”

That was the strip Bob had wanted preserved for a detailed map of the genetic variants in their original positions. The routine sequence fraction would not give him that map.

He kept looking at it.

“Bob?”

Adrian turned toward him. “I’m sorry, Bob.”

Bob opened his mouth to ask Jonas to save half the strip. Half would leave Vargas without the repeat she needed.

“Use it,” he said. “Record what we’re losing.”

He closed his sampling plan and turned to Adrian. “You stopped when you had to. I asked too much of the repair.”

“I can still help here.”

“I know. First we get you back to camp and fix that clamp.”

When Adrian had cooled enough to walk, Bob went with him, leaving Vargas to choose the next pockets. Jonas was already dividing the strip.

At camp, Bob brought the clamp back to its mark. The flow stayed steady and Adrian’s temperature began to fall. As Bob started to rise, Adrian held out a hand for the wrench. Bob passed it over, braced his right hand against his thigh, and stood. Adrian returned the wrench handle-first.

“Thank you,” Bob said.

Jonas called while Adrian cooled. The loosely attached bacteria and their hosts managed apart. The tightly bound pair did not: removing the bacteria impaired the hosts’ metabolism; putting the bacteria back restored it. The repeat from Bob’s strip agreed.

Vargas was already directing Sofia to the next comparison.

“Your way got us an answer,” Bob told her. “Mine kept Jonas waiting.”

“We can still ask your question.”

He looked at the divided sample on the display. “Not all of it.”

The first SAGE screen ran within their remaining allocation while his assessment of Adrian was still unfinished. It returned in time to guide Jonas’s next test. The results supported the familiar B12 dependence: the hosts needed the vitamin their partners supplied. Bob added the finding to Vargas’s comparison. His own map of the untouched sample would never be complete.

After the cooling system held through a short walking check, Bob and Adrian headed back toward the field. Vargas called them before they reached the shale.

“Sofia could use you at the ledge. Adrian, help her keep the cuts on the map. Bob, Jonas needs another pair of eyes.”

Both jobs needed doing. She had also found them a place without making them ask for one. Bob was grateful, which made it a little worse.

“We’re on our way.”

Adrian kept his analysis off until they reached Sofia. He found the contact her camera had been losing and matched it to the earlier images. Bob settled beside Jonas as another cassette arrived.

For a while, that was the work: Sofia’s team collected from the mapped pockets, Jonas tested the living arrangements, and preserved fractions went back to camp. Adrian kept the new cuts registered and checked the returning results against records already in hand. The larger drainage search stayed closed.

One comparison caught Bob’s attention. In a loose partnership, bacteria that contributed little could still take the host’s food. In a more stable one, hosts restricted food to partners that supplied the expected contribution.

“Same family?” Bob asked.

Adrian checked the established histories; Echo checked the other sites. Some apparent repetitions disappeared, but the contrast survived across different backgrounds. Different genetic changes had produced a similar way of keeping the partnership useful.

Vargas put the surviving comparisons together. Bob found the older growth records she needed and sent them over.

Then Jonas enlarged an image from a higher pocket.

“Inside this time,” he said.

Sofia leaned over the display. Two small bodies lay within a host cell.

“Show the division,” Adrian said.

In the recording, the host divided. One daughter received both bodies; the other received neither.

Jonas brought up another division. This time each daughter got one.

Bob leaned closer. “Not very reliable.”

“No,” Adrian said. “And their genomes are still much larger than the endpoint’s.”

He placed the new cells beside the externally dependent forms. The host markers put them among relatives the team was already comparing.

“We’ve been looking at how much bacterial independence they’ve lost,” he said. “We should test what the host already depends on.”

“The vitamin,” Bob said.

“Yes. If it still needs the supplier, these belong in the middle.”

Bob looked again at the uneven division. If the host needed those bodies, losing them at division would matter. Adrian had found a question they could answer without solving the whole drainage history.

“Jonas, can you repeat the B12 test on these?”

Vargas moved those tests to the front of the camp queue. More cassettes arrived while they waited. Some internal partners retained substantial genomes; others had lost more genes or transferred them to the host. Host control of division strengthened in some, and protein assays found signs of host-made proteins being imported. Adrian kept these differences beside the images instead of forcing the forms into a single sequence.

Bob watched Jonas turn to Adrian with the next recording. Sofia waited for his answer before moving her marker.

When the camp results came back, Adrian asked for the division records again.

In two early internal forms, removing the partners impaired the hosts’ methionine production. Added B12 restored it nearly to the level in the handled controls. The specific rescue repeated; damage from removal alone was becoming a worse explanation.

Adrian put the results beside the matching cells.

“They need the vitamin,” he said. “And they’re passing the supplier to their daughters, even here.”

Bob followed the comparison across the display: bacteria living alongside hosts, partners the hosts depended on, then internal suppliers being inherited with varying success. Team B’s highly integrated bodies stood at the far end.

Here were the less finished versions he had asked for.

“Related states,” Bob said. “Not ancestors until we earn ancestors.”

Adrian bracketed the related forms and left the gaps between them. Some changes could be ordered; others could not. The stronger forms retained the vitamin pathway too, but proving that they made new B12 and delivered it to the host would take laboratory work.

Ortega stood beside the sample cases, counting under his breath. “Eleven receptor-bound systems.”

Bob looked over. “Independent?”

“Eight after grouping the likely shared histories. Clean enough that you won’t yell at me.”

“I can yell at you with eight.”

“Seven dependency cases with good provenance.”

“Better.”

“Four early intracellular forms with spatial context.”

Bob turned fully toward him. “Four?”

Ortega held up four fingers.

Adrian added the genomic comparison. “Three independent host backgrounds show partial reduction in the same direction. The exact losses differ.”

“So this isn’t one family doing something peculiar,” Bob said.

“Not in the surviving comparisons,” Adrian said.

Vargas had gone quiet. Then her voice came clearly over the channel.

“Folks,” she announced. “We have the missing middle.”

Bob looked from the loose partners to the inherited bodies. “Enough living examples to test how an organelle forms.”

The channel erupted into a cacophony of excited questions and statements. Vargas was openly delighted. So was Ortega. Sofia had stopped pretending to sound calm. Lena and Jonas started talking over each other at the portable microscope. Kira pulled up the images from the upper pocket again. Malik was already pointing out where they fitted beside Adrian’s new comparisons.

Then somebody whooped.

It was brief and badly distorted by three open radios, and it set off the rest of them. Jonas slapped the side of the microscope case. Malik shouted something unintelligible. Ortega turned toward the shale face with both hands spread as if he had personally arranged the geology.

For once nobody immediately told anybody to get back to work.

Bob looked at the mapped face again. The fire had taken his best bridge samples. The west-fracture replacement had just lost the history he needed. Kira and Malik had found a magnificent ending with no middle. And the ugly little shale face Sofia had refused to touch was now giving them repeated comparisons faster than they could process them.

Adrian was answering a question from Kira. Jonas had left his hand on the microscope case, grinning. Bob grinned back, a little late. They had worked too hard for him to stand there looking disappointed.

He let the noise continue for another few seconds. “Jesus,” he said.

Ortega glanced at him. “Can I put that in the field summary?”

“Only if you spell it correctly.”

Vargas laughed over the channel. Bob held the smile until Ortega turned back to the samples.

Bob looked toward Adrian. The weak gray branch was still on the source model where Adrian had left it, visible beside the stronger matches instead of polished away. He knew how good the work was.

“When we’re back,” Bob said, “let’s have another look at Luis’s water-bear recording.”

“You may have to learn its name,” Adrian said.

“I’ll risk it.”

The familiar exchange should have helped. Instead, he heard how hard he was trying.

Vargas was still talking, already seeing the next questions. Bob could hear a research program taking shape. It was hers.

His findings on mutation bias still held. He had come to Taylor hoping they would become the center of something larger; now he was finding the comparisons that made her argument stronger. He was good at it. He could almost see the rest of his career spent being useful to somebody with a better question.

Adrian turned toward him. Bob started to smile again. Adrian rested a hand on his shoulder and looked back at the display.

Bob let the smile go. Adrian kept his hand there.

Previous← One ExpeditionNext · Beat 18BThe Cost of Leaving →
Extra Weights / Beat 18B

The Cost of Leaving

The source of their discovery began destroying itself before they finished being happy about finding it.

A dark outlet weakened between drone passes. A new fracture half a meter higher began leaking turbid water. One ice-cemented block settled enough to expose fresh wet shale underneath. Conductivity at a previously clean boundary drifted toward the pocket beside it. The biology remained rich, but the histories were starting to smear together.

An unseasonal deluge of rain didn't help.

Bob pulled his hood forward. Water ran off its edge onto the display, breaking the source map into bright little distortions. He wiped it with his right glove as Sofia came back from the lower markers.

“The block above the last cut has moved,” she said. “We’re clear of it. Lena’s bringing the sealed cases out.”

“Keep everyone off that section,” Vargas said from camp. “No new pockets.”

Vargas’s image shrank to a corner of the field display as she put the forecast beside the latest drone view. More rain was coming, with no useful break in sight.

“The return line is open,” Vargas said. “I’d like to use it while it stays that way.”

Ortega was fastening the completed cases onto a sled. Jonas had capped the last preparation at the microscope. All around Bob, the work was becoming things they could carry.

He enlarged one weak-association compartment near the lower edge of the map. Its images showed bacteria still living loosely around their hosts. If those hosts proved close enough relatives of the dependent forms, they might narrow the possible histories of how the partnership had changed.

“We should get this one before we go.”

Vargas looked at the selected pocket. “What would it change?”

“It might give us a close relative that still manages without its partners. A better comparison for what changed when the hosts began to need them.”

“It could,” she said. “Does the main result need it?”

Bob moved the pocket beside the surviving comparisons. “It would make the history better.”

“I agree. I’m asking what fails without it.”

Echo brought up the uncertain cases: possible shared ancestry, unreliable timing, modern mixing. Bob set them aside with the weakest protein-import result. Three of the four weak-association examples retained useful histories. The dependency and conflict-control results held; so did the stronger import evidence, division records, and reduced genomes.

Vargas brought the two surviving host-control comparisons alongside one another.

“Different host backgrounds,” Bob said.

“Yes,” Echo said. “The functional comparison survives. We can’t give every group a secure place in the history.”

Dependence, genome reduction, and reliable inheritance did not all appear together. Even the better histories would not arrange these forms into one clean sequence. Bob could see that. He could also see where another sample might help.

“The intact arrangements still predict recovery across these backgrounds,” Vargas said. “We lose detail when we drop the upper group. We don’t lose that.”

She was still excited. Bob could hear it beneath the field-command voice.

“We can take this home and test it properly,” she said. “Keep the cultures going, find out which of these paths holds up. It’s a research program, Bob. An important paper too, if the follow-up holds.”

He knew what they had. His findings on mutation bias still had a place in the larger case. There was enough work here to fill a lab, and Vargas was already seeing how to do it.

The selected pocket remained at the edge of his display.

“One collection,” he said. “Just that pocket. The cases can keep moving.”

“They are moving,” Vargas said. “The people making that collection would still be under the face.”

“It’s lower than the block Sofia marked.”

“Water is coming through above it. We don’t know what it’s taking out.”

Bob looked at the new flow. He could not tell how far it ran behind the shale. He reduced the collection area on the map.

“We don’t need the whole section. A small registered cut.”

“A smaller sample doesn’t make the ground safer.”

Behind Sofia, Lena pulled the last sealed case clear of the lower work area. Jonas folded the microscope cover over the instrument. Neither of them was waiting for the argument to end.

Bob turned to Adrian.

“Help me with this. Is there a way to get that comparison without keeping a team down there?”

Adrian studied the pocket and the recent drone frames. He left the scientific comparison visible beside them.

“The cut is small,” he said. “Reaching it and registering it still puts someone beside the new flow.”

“We could do it while they’re finishing here.”

“We still don’t know what’s supporting that part of the face.”

Bob waited for the next part. Adrian usually had another distinction, some possibility Bob had compressed out of the question. They had found the whole source that way.

Adrian looked back at the selected pocket. “I’d like to know the order too.” He paused before going on. “I’m sorry, Bob. I agree with Vargas. We should leave.”

Bob looked away toward the actual rock. From here the pocket was an unremarkable wet patch below one of Sofia’s markers. It had survived while they found enough material for everyone to be happy. Now they were going to leave it there.

For a moment Bob could think of nothing except how little he was asking for. One cut. He could almost feel the cassette in his hand. Back in a lab, the gap would become a limitation in the paper, something they had been unable to resolve.

Rain struck the case beside his knee. The dark water from the new fracture had reached the base of the marker.

His right thumb rested beside the selected pocket. He moved it to the corner of the display and closed the collection view.

“All right.”

"We really lucked out on the timing of the rain," Vargas said as she checked the preservation queue and the return-route feed. She opened the team channel. “Shift to preservation and withdrawal.”

Adrian handed the field analysis to Echo and dropped to low load. Bob helped Jonas secure the microscope case, steadying it against his boot while he closed the catches with his good hand. When he straightened, the selected pocket was gone from the screen. The route home had taken its place.

Completed samples and irreplaceable records went toward camp first.
