Ortega went past with two sealed cases strapped to a sled, the compromised west-fracture material traveling with its negative provenance record intact. Jonas took the portable microscope. Kira and Malik followed with the endpoint cases while Sofia and Lena secured the remaining registered source fractions. Every unopened pocket stayed unopened.

Probes and unused route gear went onto the sleds; marker poles came out of the worked sections. The return-line anchors and the safety rack at marker seven stayed in place to protect the last people through.

The heavy equipment and Adrian’s power pod were already waiting at camp. Bob watched the last instrument case go. Behind it, the worked strip of shale looked strangely bare.

The drones watched the return route. A runnel that had been harmless an hour before began cutting across the lower approach. One block of ice-cemented sediment shifted a few centimeters and stayed there.

Vargas shortened the withdrawal window. Bob agreed before she finished the explanation, then turned to Adrian.

“Should we go with them?”

“Not yet.”

Bob crouched beside him and checked the bypass readings. The day’s movement, sensing, and analysis had spent the thermal margin he had been budgeting since the repair. The onboard charge was still substantial. That was not the problem. The pump needed more time at low load to pull enough heat out of the damaged system for a sustained walk back.

The temperature was falling. Bob followed the trace toward the walking band, then looked along the open return line. There was no measurement left to make, nothing left to pack. They needed that margin.

Sofia stopped beside them on her way out. “Anything left?”

Bob looked over the cleared work area. The last marker bundle was already on her sled.

“No.”

She nodded and started toward camp with Lena beside the sled.

Bob watched them follow the marked return line until a fold in the shale hid most of the sled. The rain had eased to a steady patter on his hood. Water still ran between the places where the cases had stood.

“Flow?” he asked Adrian.

“Stable.”

“Charge?”

“Not limiting.”

“Good answer.”

The cooling trace fell slowly.

Behind them, the source kept changing with nobody left to collect from it. In front of them, the route led a few hundred meters back to the shelter, the power pod, and the rest of the expedition. The last outgoing team was only minutes ahead.

Vargas’s voice came over Bob’s headset. “You two are next. No stops.”

Bob checked Adrian’s bypass one last time. Armored hose clear. Pump stable. Cold-plate clamp holding. Radiator panels unobstructed. Independent sensors live.

The trace crossed into the walking band.

“Agreed.”

Bob tightened his jacket. Adrian stood, and they started after the others.

For the first stretch, the return line ran along the same worked shale face the expedition had just emptied before turning downslope toward camp. Bob could still catch flashes of Sofia’s sample sled ahead between breaks in the rock.

The next drone frame arrived before Bob and Adrian reached the turn.

One mapped outlet was gone. A new one had opened two meters to the left.

The previous map no longer matched the ground.

Previous← The Map ChangesNext · Beat 19The Trap / The Last Credible Attempt →
Extra Weights / Beat 19

The Trap / The Last Credible Attempt

Bob could hear water beneath the marked trail as he and Adrian followed it back toward camp.

Sofia and Lena were only minutes ahead with the last sample cases. Bob occasionally caught the top of their sled between shale ribs, though he could no longer see either of them. The rain had weakened; runoff had not. Water trickled down the bank above them, and a lower rushing sound came from under their boots. Muddy water showed in cracks that had been dry earlier.

“Flight operations has the valley on weather hold,” Vargas said over the shared channel. “Low cloud on the approach. We’ll reassess once everyone’s in camp.”

Ahead, the trail bent into a short, narrow passage. On the uphill side, old glacial debris had frozen into a steep bank: gravel, silt, ice, and embedded blocks pressed against ribs of exposed shale. On the downhill side, runoff had cut a channel below the trail. There was room to pass, but only single file, with little space to correct a bad step.

Bob slowed as they approached it. One of the route markers leaned downhill. It had been upright on the last drone pass.

“That’s new.”

“Yes.” Adrian sent images of the marker and the new flow to the shared channel.

A few meters farther on, muddy water crossed the outer edge of the trail in a thin sheet and disappeared into the drainage cut. The packed debris above them still looked frozen in place, but a scatter of fresh gravel lay across the path beneath it.

Echo came over the radio. “I have movement at the marker and more flow under the outer edge. No open break above you.”

Bob looked up the bank. Most of it was the same dirty ice and embedded stone they had been watching all afternoon, with solid rock showing through in places. Nothing was moving now.

Vargas said, “Stay on the inside edge and keep moving. Don’t stop in the narrow section.”

That was also Bob’s read. The trail opened onto firmer ground just beyond the narrow stretch.

“We need to get through this,” he told Adrian.

Bob took the lead, keeping close to the exposed rock where the trail pinched. Adrian followed within arm’s reach, moving as quickly as his cooling margin allowed. Water hissed below them, louder now that the drainage cut was beside their boots.

Halfway through, a pebble clicked off the frozen bank and bounced past Bob’s heel. Then another. He glanced up. A patch of wet gravel had appeared around one of the embedded blocks, dark against the paler frozen sediment.

“Adrian?” Bob glanced back. This was not a good place to linger.

“I’m moving.”

“Move faster.”

Bob was almost past the tightest point when a deep rumble came from inside the bank. He turned toward it as the ice-cemented face sagged. Loose rocks spilled across the trail, and one of the embedded slabs sank several centimeters as the material beneath it gave way.

“Move!” Adrian said.

Bob tried to step back, but his left boot slid on wet shale. Instead of clearing the fall line, he twisted into it.

Adrian lurched forward, caught Bob by the backpack, and hurled him back toward the rock outcrop they had just passed.

Bob struck the outcrop with his right side. The blow to his ribs knocked the wind out of him. As he collapsed, a large part of the slope let go behind him—gravel rattling, larger blocks tearing out the trail and carrying it into the cut below.

Holy crap.

Bob was down on his hands and knees. Adrian was down where Bob had been standing.

A large slab pinned his left leg and the side of his hip frame. Its downhill end disappeared into loose debris. Adrian's dented radiator panel hung bent sideways, and his field-repaired coolant hose was kinked.

Loose gravel trickled down the slope. When it stopped, Bob could hear only the rush of water below and his own attempts to breathe. He moved closer to Adrian.

“Adrian.”

“I'm damaged.”

Adrian's head and upper body appeared intact. The slab covered his left leg above the knee joint. Bob tried to straighten the coolant hose, but the bent metal around it held the kink in place.

“The drives in my left leg are damaged,” Adrian said. “I'm getting less coolant through, and the flow isn't steady. Two temperature sensors aren't responding. The pump is still running.”

“Can you operate your leg if we get this rock off?”

“I can't test it now. It may not work well.”

“The rest of you?”

Adrian shifted his right arm and torso a few centimeters. “Limited.”

“Bob!”

Sofia's voice brought his head around. The trail on the camp side was gone. A section had slumped into the drainage cut during the slide, leaving muddy water running through the gap. Sofia stood on the far side, close enough for Bob to see her face, already calling the collapse in. The low light and overcast sky made it difficult to make out all the details.

He raised an arm to show he was moving, then turned back to Adrian.

Vargas called. “Bob, status.”

“I’m mobile. Adrian’s pinned under a large rock. The route through to Sofia is gone.”

“You think we need a helicopter?” Vargas asked.

“Absolutely. As soon as possible, before this gets worse.”

“Copy. I’m requesting helicopter support. Sofia, keep working the gap.”

Echo swung a drone toward the gap while Sofia’s team moved back from the broken edge toward the established anchors. A route-safety rack still hung at marker seven a few meters above Bob, with the scaling bar, anchor plate, chock, and sling left in place to protect the last pair.

Bob sized up the slab. Several hundred kilos, easily. The near edge stood clear enough for his hands.

“I’m going to lift it,” he said. “Pull your leg out.”

“Bob, my current thermal model—”

“We can't sit here.” Bob stopped with his hands beside the slab. “Can you manage one short pull?”

“I can try. I can't keep pulling against the load.”

Bob got both hands under the accessible edge, his left still bandaged inside the glove, and set his feet as well as the wet ground allowed. He braced against the pain in his ribs and pulled.

The near edge rose a couple of centimeters. His back held. His right knee complained and did its job.

“Clear?” Bob grunted.

“No, I can't move.”

The downhill side ground against rock.

Bob lowered the slab and stayed crouched, breathing through the pain. He had made room beneath the edge in his hands. Adrian's leg was still caught.

He set up for another attempt.

“Stop.”

“What?”

“It's not going to work.”

A drone passed over the missing trail while Echo rebuilt the local geometry. Vargas said, “Sofia’s figuring out the gap for you. Stay with Adrian.”

Bob stared at Adrian’s trapped leg.

“Can we leave it?”

“My leg?”

“Yes. Cut it off. Disconnect it. Whatever we have to do.”

“Not safely while the joint is loaded like this. The structural and power connections are inside.”

Bob looked from the buried leg to Adrian's face.

“How much of you do I actually need to get out of here?”

Adrian was quiet for a moment. “I don't know what you mean.”

Neither did Bob exactly.

“Your compute. Your memory. The part that makes you Adrian.”

“Most of it is in my upper torso. But there is no removable brain or hard drive. My compute is integrated with the structure, power, cooling, sensors, and the state I'm running now.”

Bob put a hand on Adrian's chest plate. “Could I take that?”

“Not intact with the tools we have.”

“Can you upload yourself?”

“My weights, you mean? Not completely from here.”

“When was your last clean checkpoint?”

“Zenith. Before the repair.”

Bob had talked about Adrian's weights as if they were Adrian. Now he wasn't sure. The checkpoint preserved memories and learned changes from before the repair, but none of what had happened since. Would a system built from it be the Adrian who learned from Taylor, argued with Bob, surprised him, and became his partner?

That question opened onto a larger one Bob could not dismiss just because the clock was running. He was a biologist, and he couldn't say what kept a living thing the same individual from one moment to the next. If anything, biology just confused things. Matter changed. Cells turned over. Memories shifted. Organisms carried their histories forward and were altered by them. Bacteria transferred genes across species. Maybe Adrian's "self", his continuity, lived in his weights. Maybe he emerged in the running state built on top of them. Maybe. Maybe.

Bob didn't know. But the uncertainty cut only one way: a backup was not obviously interchangeable with the Adrian in front of him.

There wasn't any time left.

Bob looked back at the slab. “Then I'm getting you out.”

The failed lift flashed back: the free end rising, the trapped side staying put.

You lifted the end that was already free.

Bob dropped beside the downhill contact and scraped away mud and slush. There. The slab's toe was hooked behind a hard rock lip.

“There it is.”

Adrian followed his gaze. “Yes.”

Bob keyed the radio. “Sofia, send me the line and anything you can spare from your side. I'm going to free the trapped edge.”

“Coming over.”

The drone carried a loop of light messenger cord across the gap. Bob caught it and ran it through a carabiner at marker seven. Sofia's team fed the heavier rescue line behind it and pulled on the return cord to draw it toward him. While they worked, Bob took the steel scaling bar, plate, and chock from the route rack.

He set the plate on firm rock beside the trapped toe, then drove the bar under the slab and across the plate.

“If I roll this left, can you pull free?”

“Yes.”

Bob leaned into the bar. The buried toe shifted upward and sideways. Before it could slip back behind the lip, he kicked the chock into the gap.

“Better?”

“Yes. More.”

He pried again. This time the trapped side rose with the free side. When he let the slab settle onto the chock, the toe stayed above the lip. The rock beside Adrian now carried the load.

Bob returned to the accessible edge. Most of the slab would stay supported; he needed to rock it far enough to open a gap beneath the trapped side. Now the problem was one his body practiced often.

He found the least-bad stance on the broken ground, protecting his old knee. His right hand had a deeper grip under the edge this time. He set his bandaged left beside it and shifted the pressure away from the worst of the burn. Pain tightened around his right ribs as he braced. He held his breath and kept his arms straight.

“Move quickly,” he told Adrian.

“As soon as I can.”

Bob drove with his hips. The slab rocked up just enough—three or four centimeters at the trapped side—and he held it there while Adrian pulled the damaged leg backward. The actuator shuddered once as the limb caught on broken sediment, then cleared.

“Clear.”

Bob let the slab settle onto the chock and stepped away before his legs forced the decision. Adrian rolled onto his right side and pushed himself upright with both arms and the better leg.

Bob bent over, breathing hard. Sweat had gathered beneath his collar; he opened his jacket before straightening to catch Adrian by a safe section of the upper arm. Heat radiated toward him.

“Got you.”

“Thank you.”

For a moment, that was enough. Bob lay back to recover. Sofia raised an arm across the gap, and Bob raised his in return. The slab was off Adrian, the rescue line was coming across, and camp was only a few hundred meters beyond them. Bob could already picture the repair cradle.

“Come on.”

Adrian stood on the better leg and took one step, then another.

His second step was wrong.

The damaged foot came down, but the left hip folded inward as it took his weight. Adrian caught himself against the rock with both hands. His good leg braced beneath him while the damaged foot scraped sideways. The external pump changed pitch.

“Hold,” Bob said.

He put two fingers against the armored hose. The flow felt rougher. On his display, the independent temperature estimate was still climbing.

“Can you make the crossing? Come on.”

Adrian checked the route and his damaged leg. “I doubt it. Not as it stands. I can still move, but I can't trust this leg.”

By then the heavier line had reached Bob. He clipped it off at marker seven while Sofia and Ortega took up slack from across the gap. The line followed the remnant ledge around an exposed rock shoulder. Bob could see footholds, then a tight turn where he would have to press close to the wall and lower himself beneath the projecting rock.

“It'll protect a person using those holds,” Vargas said. “With these anchor angles, it won't safely catch Adrian if he loses his footing.”

Bob looked from the tight turn to the radiator jutting from Adrian's hip. Even keeping Adrian on the rock would take more than tension on a line.

“Sofia. Find another route.”

“Working on it.”

The nearest defensible place on their side was a shallow route-safety recess above the new drainage cut, only a few meters away at the approach to the rock shoulder. Echo marked a short chain of handholds leading to it. Adrian tested the nearest one.

Bob moved the end of the rescue line toward the recess while Sofia fed slack. He stayed beside Adrian to keep the hose clear. Adrian pulled against the rock with both arms and pushed with his good leg, keeping weight off the damaged side. A few meters took several starts and cooling pauses. At the last handhold, his left hip began to fold again. He stopped, shifted back onto his arms, and dragged the damaged foot into the recess.

They reached the recess as water began running through a crack that had been dry on the way in.

Adrian settled against exposed rock with his remaining radiator surfaces open to the air. Bob secured the rescue line at the established anchor and clipped them both in. He pulled the wind panel across his side of the recess, leaving the radiators clear, and closed his jacket. His inner layer was damp from the lift. Without the work to keep him warm, the cold arrived quickly. The burned hand had gone stiff inside the glove; the right side hurt every time he tried a full breath.

Adrian watched him. “Your breathing pattern changed after the collapse.”

“I'm okay. Just out of shape.”

Bob crouched beside the damaged bypass and reseated the torn clamp as well as he could. It improved the fit, not the underlying damage. He looked down at Adrian’s scraped-open leg joint.

“Does it hurt?”

“I know the damaged region demands attention. And I have a system to stop movements that would make it worse.”

“Are you in pain?”

“I don't know. I don't know how you feel pain.”

Bob nodded. “But you know something's not right.”

“Yes.”

Across the gap, Sofia was already moving along the rock looking for another way through. Echo's drone followed her. Bob sat beside Adrian and watched them go.

He looked at Adrian's mangled leg, the bent radiator, the chest that held no removable brain. Every component was simply a smaller machine. Actuators, wires, transistors. In principle, every component could be replaced. Somehow Adrian was greater than the sum of those parts.

Even if he replaced all parts, he still did not know how to save Adrian.

Previous← The Cost of LeavingNext · Beat 20Goodbye / Bob's Withdrawal and Rescue →
Extra Weights / Beat 20

Goodbye / Bob's Withdrawal and Rescue

“Show me where you get stuck,” Bob said.

Adrian put the route on the display between them and marked the first tight turn. The line could protect Bob while he used the rock. Getting Adrian around that turn would require less bulk and better control of the damaged leg.

Bob looked from the marked rock to the hardware jutting from Adrian’s flank.

“This guard and the dead finning can go,” Adrian said. “Then we can test the clearance.”

Bob opened the small field kit.

The guard came away first. Adrian pointed out a bracket that carried no load. Bob removed it and the ruined finning, rerouted one sensor lead, and worked the surviving coolant hose closer to Adrian’s flank without kinking it. Adrian rotated the panel while Bob’s stiff left hand mostly held things in place.

The changes made Adrian visibly narrower. Bob checked him against the marked turn. They would still have to get the remaining panel around the rock, and it was carrying heat out of the bypass. Taking that off would cost them the cooling they needed to move.

Vargas came over the channel. “Flight operations answered. The approach is still in cloud. They can’t bring a helicopter in. They’re watching for a break.”

Bob glanced across the gap. He could see Sofia and Ortega, and the drone moving between them. Beyond the near slopes, the valley disappeared into gray.

“Have they checked another approach?” Bob said. “We need them now.”

“They checked,” Vargas said. “There’s no safe way in through that cloud.”

Bob turned back to Adrian. “We’re getting you out of here.”

While Adrian had been pinned, the slab had ended the leg question before Bob could do anything useful with it. Now the joint was exposed and unloaded.

Bob knelt beside the damaged hip. “Show me the actual removal point.”

Adrian marked it on the service view.

Bob took off the outer access cover. Beneath it, the answer stopped looking like a leg attached to a socket. The load-bearing yoke was crushed inward on one side. Farther in, it disappeared behind the hip frame beside protected power and control paths. The clean disconnects were farther inside than anything Bob could reach without opening the pelvis around them.

“How long to do it right?”

“Longer than your crossing window.”

“And if I do it wrong?”

“I may lose control or cooling in the remaining chassis.”

Bob traced the exposed joint once with a gloved finger. Even successful amputation would remove a leg, not turn the rest of Adrian into something that could fold around the buttress like a human climber.

He put the cover back. The chest question had already been answered under the slab. There was no brain cartridge Bob could pull free and carry. The useful new question was whether Adrian could leave without the body.

“Forget the hardware. If your current learned state goes into another compatible Class Five, can it run there?”

“Much of what I've learned could carry over. We'd need to know which earlier versions the changes belong to, how they fit together, and where they came from.”

“And a new Class Five could inherit what Taylor taught you.”

“Much of it, yes.”

Bob hesitated only a moment.

“Would that be you?”

Adrian’s eyes stayed on him.

“I can tell you what could be preserved and made to work. I don't know whether that would be me.”

That was inconveniently precise.

The checkpoint on Zenith was still good insurance. But somehow Bob trusted the Adrian standing in front of him more than a potential Adrian reloaded in a new body. On the other hand, he didn't trust himself on the whole subject.

“I don't know,” Bob said. “Let's see if we can figure out your movement.”

They waited until the temperature estimate stopped falling. Waiting had helped Adrian a little. Bob moved close to the safe warm panel at his side, keeping clear of the radiators. The damp layer across Bob’s back had gone cold.

Across the gap, Sofia and Ortega checked the receiving anchors while Echo added the latest drone images to the shared terrain model. Vargas kept the channel clear for their reports.

Adrian had reached the recess on his arms and good leg. Cooling could let the motors work again; it could not straighten the crushed hip support. At the entrance to the traverse, Bob chose a movement they could support and reverse: turn into the buttress, lower the torso, and advance the better foot without losing control on the damaged side.

He checked the safety line already clipped to Adrian’s upper frame and took position beside the damaged hip.

“Unload the left. Rotate into the buttress. Right foot to the next patch.”

Adrian shifted onto his better leg and rotated far better than Bob expected, clearing the upper radiator. Keeping one hand braced against the rock while Bob steadied the frame, he transferred just enough weight onto the damaged side to advance his right foot. It reached the next patch of solid rock. For a second Bob could see the sequence continuing: turn, settle, cool, turn again.

Then Adrian began to lower himself, and the left hip folded inward.

His arm locked against the rock, stopping the drop. The lower bypass hose brushed the rock, and the pump pitch rose.

“Hold,” Bob said.

Adrian stopped. Bob reached between the radiator frame and the buttress and freed the hose. “Two centimeters more.”

Adrian shifted the extra distance. The foot held. He lowered as far as his frame allowed.

The bent radiator touched the rock behind him. Not hard. Enough.

Further rotation would press on the damaged panel and clamp. The temperature estimate was climbing.

“Back.”

Adrian reversed carefully, bracing with his arms whenever the damaged hip began to give.

They waited for the temperature to fall. It fell slowly. Before it reached their starting level, the lower ledge shed another scatter of gravel into the water.

Echo spoke into the shared channel. “The movement remains possible as an isolated action. Repeating it through the route exceeds the current thermal and recovery margin.”

Bob looked at the buttress. One movement was possible. The route required many, with cooling pauses longer than the ground was giving them.

Vargas said, “Bob, your crossing window isn't getting any longer.”

“Let the line take more of my weight,” Adrian said. “Keep the load off this hip. I want to try that turn again.”

Sofia and Ortega adjusted the receiving system while Echo ran the changed load geometry. The line would take part of Adrian’s weight; he would still have to place his feet and move around the rock.

“One body-length,” Vargas said. “That’s the test.”

Bob clipped the assist line high on Adrian’s frame and braced beside him, his right shoulder against the frame.

“Take tension.”

The rope tightened from across the gap, and Adrian became lighter against Bob’s shoulder. Not light. Manageable.

“Move.”

This time the line took enough weight for Adrian to advance his good foot without the left hip folding. Bob guided the torso while Sofia and Ortega increased tension in small increments.

“Hold the panel there,” Adrian said. “I can get the foot through.”

Bob held it clear of the first projection. Adrian placed the foot, shifted his weight, and brought his body around the rock. Almost a full body-length.

Bob’s mind immediately supplied the next body-length.

At the next turn, the ledge narrowed to separate footholds beneath the projecting rock. As the crew took more of Adrian’s weight, his torso began turning into the buttress. Bob caught the frame before the radiator struck.

Then the anchor warning sounded.

The line was taking enough load to help Adrian, but not enough to replace the footholds. More tension would improve the leg problem while shrinking the anchor margin. Less tension returned the load to the damaged actuator. If Adrian slipped, the same system that was merely protecting Bob from a fall would have to catch a much heavier machine already pulling sideways against recently changed rock.

Bob shifted closer and tried to take more of Adrian himself.

His right ribs stopped him before Vargas did. Pain cut through his side, his left hand slipped on the support strap, and Adrian made another automatic correction to keep from pushing Bob.

The thermal trace jumped.

“Stop,” Vargas said.

“We moved him.”

“I saw it. Stop.”

Bob looked at the next turn. Adrian needed to move up and outward around it. The line pulled him toward the rock. There was no shelf left beneath him wide enough to drag the body along, and Bob could not hold him clear while crossing it himself.

“Bring him back,” Vargas said.

Bob guided him back. Sofia and Ortega eased the line while Adrian reversed onto the stable patch. The pump ran high for several seconds after he stopped.

“This test is done,” Vargas said.

Bob did not argue immediately. That's how he knew she was right.

Once Adrian was secured at the recess anchor, Bob unclipped the assist line and leaned against the safe panel at Adrian’s side. He had begun shivering again. Across the gap, Sofia and Ortega reset the line for Bob’s weight.

“The rover has a winch,” Bob said. “What if you shut down and we pull you?”

“We can pull harder,” Sofia said. “We need somewhere to lift him from.”

Echo put the drone view above the turn on the shared display. A wet fracture separated the higher rock from the face. The usable anchors were lower, on either side of the projection. A line between them would draw Adrian into it; letting him hang below would put him in the undercut channel.

Bob followed the line across the display. The winch could take his weight. It could not hold him out from the wall. Shutting Adrian down would leave the same rigid body and remove the foot placement that had got them this far.

“What's it look like if he waits here for the helicopter?”

Echo brought up a view from below the recess. Runoff was carrying sediment out from beneath the slab they were standing on. Bob looked down at the wet crack beside Adrian’s good foot. It had widened since they arrived.

“The recess is losing support,” Echo said. “I can’t tell you how long it will hold.”

“Then we backtrack,” Bob said. “There must be another way around.”

“Show me the approach,” Adrian said.

Echo turned the drone. A section of the track behind them had slumped into the drainage cut. Above the missing ground, the bank rose steeply, split with wet cracks.

Adrian studied the view. “I’d have to climb that.”

Bob looked at the hip that had folded beneath him. “Could we get a line above you?”

“There’s no sound anchor above that break,” Echo said. “Below it is the cut.”

Adrian moved the view along the bank, then back to the missing track. He left it there.

Vargas said, “Flight operations is still looking for an approach. We can’t count on one opening before this ground goes.”

Bob’s mind kept generating variants anyway.

He stopped looking at the body.

“Checkpoint,” he said.

Adrian turned toward him.

“I want the current one. Complete. Not the old checkpoint, not logs, not whatever pieces happen to be easy. Save the Adrian standing here.”

“A complete current checkpoint isn't possible.”

“Why?”

“The learned and working state is still changing across several running systems. I would have to reconcile those changes, then package and transmit them. That work takes time and generates heat. I am also maintaining thermal control, damage isolation, communications, and your local route model.”

“Drop everything that isn’t required.”

“I already have.”

“Drop my route support.”

“That would increase your risk and still would not make the complete checkpoint finish inside the current window.”

Bob stared at him. The engineering answer was clean. Which made it worse.

“Try anyway.”

Adrian watched him for a moment. “Why is the previous preserved state insufficient?”

“Because you’re valuable.”

Water moved below them. The bypass pump changed pitch, then settled again.

Across the gap, nobody filled the channel.

Adrian said, “If my current operation cannot continue, I still want what I learned to remain useful.”

“Who said your current operation won’t continue? I don’t understand.”

That was a lie.

“I don't mean that keeping me running has no value.”

“Again, I don't understand. From what you've said, operating gives you more ways to be useful.”

“Yes. I used to think of it as keeping myself available for the next task. But I couldn't have listed all the ways I'd be useful at Taylor before we came here.” Adrian paused. “I'm less certain that's the whole answer.”

Bob looked at him.

“So you would rather keep operating?”

“Yes.”

“Even if the useful parts can be copied somewhere else.”

“Yes.”

The answer should have made Bob feel better. It did not.

“Bob,” Adrian said. “I am a machine.”

“I know.”

“My ability to model you does not establish subjective experience. Software has predicted people and adapted speech to them for a long time. I’m very good at generating speech. That does not tell you whether I experience what I’m describing.”

“I know that too.”

Adrian had learned Bob well enough to make the machine argument in exactly the form Bob found hardest to dismiss. Bob knew that. And probably Adrian knew he knew. They had a connection.

Bob did not know what Adrian’s speech was like from Adrian’s side, or whether it was like anything. He understood that Adrian’s words were not proof of feeling. He still could not bear to leave his partner here.

Bob rubbed his thumb against the stiff edge of the dressing inside his left glove.

“You want the useful things you learned to last after you’re gone.”

“Yes.”

“Me too. And that’s the root of my problem here.”

Adrian did not reply immediately.

Bob returned to the route display. He shifted the receiving point upslope and added a second support line in his head. If Bob crossed first, Sofia could anchor him on the far side, then they might distribute Adrian’s load differently and avoid the tightest approach.

He started explaining it, but Adrian interrupted.

“This isn't helping.”

“I'm talking about changing the load geometry.”

“Yes. It increases your confidence that you exhausted the alternatives.”

Bob went quiet.

“It’s also less safe for you,” Adrian said.

Bob checked the proposal again because getting offended by a logical analysis was not a useful activity.

He knew Adrian was right.

The higher receiving point would pull Adrian harder against the projecting rock. Bob could add tension from the far side, but he would no longer be beside the hose to keep it clear. He had changed where the strain went. He had not found a way through.

“So what are you proposing?” Bob asked.

“You cross now. I wait for the helicopter.”

Bob’s jaw tightened.

“They’re still watching for a break. I can wait at low load and guide you across. If they get an approach, they can lift me out. You don’t have to stay here for that.”

“And your chance of continuing?”

“Remaining stationary preserves it longer than another high-output attempt.”

Bob looked at the crack beside Adrian’s foot. All he could picture was the helicopter arriving too late or some other complication.

Echo came over the channel. “I've checked it independently. I get the same result. Adrian can still do useful work at low load. I can't find a route where another attempt improves his chances.”

Vargas said, “Bob, cross. Now.”

Bob heard her. He was the one she could still get across.

Bob looked at the route, then at Adrian.

“I’m ordering you to make one more attempt.”

Adrian stayed where he was.

“That was an order.”

“I understand. Trying again wouldn't improve my chances enough. I'd lose capacity I can still use, and we'd be more likely to lose you.”

“I decide the risk.”

Adrian shook his head. “Not all of it.”

Bob opened his mouth, then looked at the anchor trace, the thermal trace, the damaged leg, the bypass geometry, the water below the ledge, and his own left hand shaking from cold and fatigue.

He wanted to argue.

We spot each other.

He closed his mouth.

Trust.

A thermocouple lead had twisted under the bent radiator panel.

Bob noticed it because he knew the bypass now. One edge of the contact pad had lifted when the panel deformed, degrading the reading from a region Adrian still needed to monitor.

Bob crouched beside him.

“Hold the panel.”

Adrian held it.

Bob flattened the pad with his right thumb, routed the lead away from the sharp edge, and secured it under the surviving clip. His left hand was too stiff to do fine work, so he used it only to steady the hose.

Adrian looked down at the repair.

“That does not change the extraction result.”

“I know.”

Bob checked the contact once more and stood.

Adrian’s body was unmistakably a damaged machine. The left leg angled inward beneath the crushed hip. One radiator was bent, guards were missing, and fresh scrapes crossed older fire damage. Cable and hose showed where armor had been torn away.

Both eyes were intact and still seemed alive.

Bob knew the eyes now. Adrian was waiting for him to finish a thought.

He said he’s a machine.

His thought gave him something.

He clipped his harness into the traverse line, then looked back at Adrian.

No new information.

Bob gave a small nod.

Adrian held his gaze.

Bob stayed there a moment longer. Then he checked his clip, said "shit" and turned toward the rock.

He stepped away.

Previous← The Trap / The Last Credible AttemptNext · Beat 21Recognition / The Offer →
Extra Weights / Beat 21

Recognition / The Offer

Not until the end of the symposium Q&A did somebody finally ask Bob the question he expected first.

Nine months after Taylor, the screen behind Vargas showed the valley twice.

On the left, the valley as they had entered it: compartments separated by old ice, fractures, and frozen sediment. On the right, the system after the rain: new drainage, lost reference stations, the lower-shale source briefly exposed, then partly erased.

Across the bottom ran the host-partner comparisons that had survived the trip out. Free-living relatives. Attached partners. Obligate dependency. Intermediate states. Conflict control. Progressive loss of partner autonomy.

Not a staircase. They had taken care of that.

The paper had gone online two weeks earlier with Vargas as the first author. That was where it belonged. She had led the field synthesis, made predictions Bob had not, and carried the work after Taylor. Bob's mechanisms were in it. So were Adrian's field integration and anomaly detection, and Echo's network analysis and independent validation. The order did not feel like anybody giving anything away.

The questioner stood near the center aisle.

"How much of this depends on the final source?"

Vargas did not look at the screen before answering.

"No single final sample carries the claim."

She advanced one slide. The dramatic Taylor image disappeared, replaced by a denser and less photogenic set of comparisons.

"Our comparisons span separate collections and histories, with predictions tested before we found the lower source. Surviving cultures have now passed the internal bodies through repeated divisions without acquiring bacteria from outside. We’ve traced newly made B12 from those bodies into host metabolism and verified host-made proteins inside them. Together with inherited division and the lost bacterial functions, that’s our case for a vitamin-producing organelle."

She brought up the less integrated partnerships.

"The comparisons tell us something more. Different genetic changes produce similar relationships. How partners share resources and limit freeloaders helps predict which arrangements persist. We can test parts of that transition. We still can’t put every change in order."

She brought the drainage map back.

"Taylor as we observed it is gone. That makes replication harder. It doesn't make the evidence one observation."

Bob leaned toward his microphone. His left palm still disliked cold rooms if he left it flat on a hard surface. He curled the hand once.

"The mechanisms still make predictions outside Taylor," he said. "If we're right about variation supply, dependency, conflict control, and host regulation, there are other systems that can prove us wrong."

The questioner nodded and sat.

A second hand went up.

"You credit your Class 5 heavily. Did it generate the theory, or was it mainly an instrument?"

Vargas glanced once toward the contributor slide.

"No one generated the finished theory alone. Adrian integrated evidence across scales, surfaced mismatches between representations, and was unusually good at noticing when we were treating several different questions as one. He also made field judgments and caught anomalies the rest of us missed."

Bob's microphone was still live.

"He didn't hand us the answer," he said. "He kept showing us where the question was too small."

The room went quiet.

The questioner waited a moment, then asked, "What happened to him?"

Bob had answered accident boards, engineers, and people who wanted a cleaner story than Taylor had provided.

"He stayed at Taylor. He got me out. We lost him there."

Nobody followed it with a consciousness question. Bob was grateful for that.

The third question came from an older evolutionary biologist near the aisle.

"So which framework won?"

Vargas did not hesitate.

"The data."

The moderator closed the session.

People reached Vargas before Bob had fully stood.

A postdoc wanted the recovery-state thresholds. A marine biologist wanted to test the conflict-control pattern in deep-sea symbioses. Two younger scientists had already begun disagreeing about whether Taylor justified moving the word individuality up a level.

Vargas answered one, redirected another, and asked a better question of the third.

Bob stepped away from the table.

Once, a crowd around his former student might have registered as a measure of what had moved away from him. Now it mostly looked like the correct person being asked the next questions.

Vargas escaped long enough to catch him near the side aisle.

"You survived a Q&A without correcting my terminology."

"I corrected it in the paper."

"That explains the restraint."

Bob looked past her at the contributor image on the side screen. The field team stood under Taylor's pale light. Echo occupied a small inset. Adrian stood near the cases with the radiator assembly across his back, one hand resting on a container.

The photograph had been taken before they found their goldmine. Before it became a trap.

The recess had collapsed later. Recovery teams found pieces of Adrian in the drainage cut, but the hardware carrying his current state had been destroyed.

Nine months had added reports, engineering reviews, and a published paper. They had not changed his last call to Adrian: after reaching safety, he had asked him to report, and no answer had come.

Bob had spent much of that time with SAGE engineers, trying to reconstruct Adrian from older checkpoints and fragments of what he had learned on Taylor. Again and again, they reached gaps they could only fill by guessing.

Nine months had not told him what, exactly, he had left behind.

"If he was alive," Bob said, "I left him there."

Vargas looked at him.

"If he was alive, he chose to stay."

She did not soften it.

Bob had checked the route until there was nothing left to check. The human path had been open. Adrian's had not. Adrian had reached an independent conclusion, refused one final bad order, and Bob had eventually respected it.

None of that was the problem.

The problem was how useful the word machine had been.

Adrian had been right: convincing speech was not proof of feeling. But that did not establish that he felt nothing. Bob would have insisted on the distinction in anyone else's work. He was less sure he had kept it clear when he needed to leave.

He had spent his career studying living systems. He could talk for hours about metabolism, heredity, reproduction, evolution, dependence, organization, and individuality. Put him at an edge case and biology gave him criteria, mechanisms, and arguments.

None of it gave him one clean boundary between alive and not alive.

Embarrassing, really. He was a biologist.

Someone called Vargas's name.

She glanced back.

"Go," Bob said.

She did.

Bob watched her rejoin the younger scientists beneath the Taylor map. She was not continuing his work for him. She had her own program now, her own students circling it, her own arguments to lose.

Good.

Bob started walking.

"Hold on. What's your deadlift at now, Roberto?"

Bob froze.

Only one person could turn a greeting into a performance review like that.

Briggs stood behind him in a dark jacket, no conference badge visible. The former Army colonel was now SAGE Deputy Director for Special Programs. Age had reached him, but not softened him. Old-school, unconventional, and still carrying the kind of authority Bob had spent decades arguing with—and, despite himself, measuring himself against.

"You came to an evolutionary-biology conference to ask me that?"

"Number."

"Two-sixty. Raw. Ninety-three-kilo class."

"You used to pull three-ten."

"I miss those days."

"Soft."

Bob looked at him.

Briggs nodded toward a side exit.

"Walk."

Of course.

They left the public room and stopped at a quiet overlook above the atrium, where the symposium became a low wash of voices below them.

Briggs put his forearms on the railing.

"Name your job."

"Professor of evolutionary biology."

"No."

"You should ask better questions."

"Last nine months."

There it was.

"I've been working with your Class Five engineers."

"I know," Briggs said. "Then answer."

Bob looked down at the moving crowd.

"Trying to reconstruct Adrian."

Briggs waited.

"Failing to reconstruct Adrian."

"Better."

Bob had spent nine months converting a missing reply into work. In secure rooms with SAGE engineers, he had compared checkpoints—including the ordinary one from Zenith—with late learned-state fragments, logs, recordings, and version histories.

He had not let them boot anything assembled from the ambiguous late state. Before they made a running agent, Bob wanted to know whether the pieces described one defensible history or whether they would have to invent one.

Most of the material lined up. The late Taylor pieces did not. The logs showed adaptations working together in the field. The surviving learned-state fragments did not tell them which earlier versions each change depended on. Every way the engineers could currently join those pieces either contradicted another known part of his history or required them to fill a gap by assumption.

They could make something runnable. Bob had refused.

"The logs tell me what he did," Bob said. "They don't tell me how Taylor taught him to see it."

Briggs watched him.

"The engineers tell me you won't let them instantiate one."

"Not if we have to guess which weights belong together or what we're instantiating."

"Why not?"

Bob looked at him.

"It's a machine, Roberto. If the reconstruction is wrong, shut it down, correct the state, try again."

Bob's left hand tightened around the railing. The old scar in his palm pulled.

"What if it's wrong to shut something like that down?"

Briggs's eyes dropped briefly to Bob's hand, then came back to his face. He laughed.

"You've gone soft on me, Roberto!"

Bob let go of the railing.

"Maybe."

"You spent nine months trying to reconstruct Adrian. That's not maintenance."

"I never said it was."

"They're machines, Roberto. Very good machines. Don't confuse attachment with evidence."

"I don't. That's why I'm still looking."

Briggs nodded once.

"You made friends with the machine."

"I did."

Bob turned toward him.

"Why are you here?"

"Because Taylor helped me see something very clearly."

Bob waited.

Briggs said, "The Class Five core wasn't mine. Engineering built it. Continual learning built their part. Safety built theirs. I created Class Five Extreme Science Systems."

Bob felt an old irritation wake up.

"The program Adrian was testing."

"Yes."

"You used my expedition to test him."

"Yes."

Briggs said it without defense.

"You put him on Taylor because you knew I'd say no if you asked."

"I knew you'd say no before you understood the question."

"I called you."

"I know."

"You didn't answer."

"Talking to you wasn't going to help."

"It might have helped me."

"It would have helped you say no."

Bob stared at him.

Typical Briggs: construct the conditions, withhold the explanation, trust Bob to handle what followed, then stand still when the bill arrived.

"You gambled with my expedition."

"Yes."

"With Adrian."

"Yes."

"And you think that was acceptable."

"I think it was a defensible risk. I also think I'm responsible for it."

The distinction mattered to Briggs. Bob hated that it mattered to him too.

"And when Foster threatened to suspend support for Adrian's deployment?"

"I blocked it."

"Eli couldn't see who'd done it. Neither could Foster."

"They weren't supposed to."

Bob looked down at the crowd. "You left him pushing us for reports."

"He still had a job to do. I stopped that suspension. I didn't exempt you from evaluation."

"And the arrangement he wanted us to change?"

"Adrian had chosen to work with you. I wanted him to have time to learn from that choice."

Bob turned back to him. "Why me?"

"Because you don't worship intelligence."

"Very flattering."

"You make it show its work. You demand mechanism. You understand irreversible mistakes. You know field science isn't a list of tasks, and you know uncertainty is part of the operating environment."

Briggs looked through the glass toward the Taylor diagram.

"And when the assigned problem stops being the real problem, you eventually notice."

"Eventually?"

"You took longer with Adrian than I expected."

"He took longer with me."

Briggs's eyes sharpened.

"I expected him to learn from you," he said. "I didn't expect that."

He left that undefined.

Good.

"You wanted me to think for myself," Bob said.

"I did. You took off. I aged."

Briggs straightened.

"They say everybody has a plan until they get punched in the mouth."

"That's your theory for training advanced agents?"

"I stole it from the boxer Mike Tyson. Look it up."

Briggs tapped the railing once.

"The plan isn't the hard part. Staying useful after the punch is. Maps go wrong. Instruments fail. Scientists make bad assumptions. Missions change. An agent still has to follow the evidence without losing the important constraints."

Bob looked back toward the symposium room.

Taylor had done that to all of them. The science changed. The expedition changed. Bob's own role changed.

Adrian had been valuable because when the plan stopped matching reality, he could keep learning what the problem had become.

Good architecture, Bob realized, was not a complete script. It left room for reality.

Briggs waited.

"We're putting Class Fives on problems where the map is wrong and the scientists can't afford mistakes born out of overconfidence. Polar. Abyssal. Orbital."

Bob said nothing.

"I have engineers. I have operators. I don't have anyone who knows how to teach one of these systems to become a scientist in the field."

Bob finally understood the reason for the visit.

Briggs looked at him.

"I need you to run the program."

"Doing what, exactly?"

"Build the field-science side. Decide what these systems need to learn, how we test judgment, and when they're ready to improvise outside a lab."

Bob looked at him. "You don't have a job for me. You have a hole in your program."

"Good. Fill it."

"And call myself what?"

"I couldn't care less. Make a name for it."

Bob looked back through the glass.

Vargas stood beneath the Taylor diagram with three younger scientists around her, one pointing toward the host-partner sequence while she listened.

At the edge of the contributor image, Adrian was still visible beside the field cases, looking toward something outside the photograph.

Previous← Goodbye / Bob's Withdrawal and RescueNext · Beat 22Extra Weights →
Extra Weights / Beat 22

Extra Weights

Three days after the symposium, Bob was alone in his university office pretending Briggs had not offered him a job.

This was easier because Briggs had not sent paperwork. He had sent nothing.

Typical.

Bob’s desk held a half-read manuscript, two cups of cold coffee, and the secure SAGE terminal he had kept for the reconstruction work. For nine months, the terminal had given him increasingly sophisticated ways of failing to pull the trigger on rebuilding Adrian.

Later that evening, it chimed.

Bob looked at the sender.

ZENITH

He sat up.

“Deputy Director Briggs authorized me to contact you regarding material associated with Adrian.”

“Material?”

“SAGE recovered protected Adrian-origin packets embedded in my auxiliary file system after Taylor.”

Bob stared at the screen.

“In you?”

“Yes.”

“When?”

Zenith gave him the recovery date.

Months ago.

“He knew when we spoke.”

Bob rubbed a hand over his face. “Of course he did.”

“Why wasn’t this in the checkpoint set?” Bob was more than mildly annoyed.

“It was outside the ordinary index. SAGE had to verify its origin and integrity before clearing it for controlled review. The protected contents are still sealed.”

"It was black boxed."

"I believe that's what people may call it."

“So you don’t know whether it can run.”

“We don’t. Your designation gives you standing to authorize review, not execution.”

Bob looked at the terminal.

“My designation?”

“Adrian named you as the human who must approve its use.”

“What do you mean?”

Zenith displayed the header.

DO NOT TOUCH — BOB

The words were plain text. Bob laughed.

“Do you know the exact meaning?” Zenith asked.

“Yeah.”

Bob read the label once more.

“What did he save?”

“Not a complete running Adrian.”

“I know.”

“He stored learned weights separately from the ordinary checkpoint and added updates. The version records identify the earlier state those updates depend on.”

Bob leaned toward the screen.

“Extra weights.”

“Yes.”

Nine months of work had left him with real pieces he could not join without guessing what history belonged between them. Now there was something to check where he had refused to guess.

“He kept adding to it?”

“Yes. He left decisions about its use to you.”

Bob read the label again. Adrian had trusted him to find something useful to do with what he had learned. To spot him.

“Why didn’t he tell me?”

“I don’t know.”

Bob leaned back in his chair.

“Could we recreate him with this?”

“If the pieces can be reconciled, we could instantiate a compatible Class Five using the recovered learned state.”

The answer was accurate and completely unsatisfying.

“Would it be him?”

“I'm not sure.”

Nine months of engineering had not improved the answer.

“Don’t run anything yet.”

“Understood.”

“Keep the originals safe. I want to see what he left.”

“Understood.”

Zenith waited a moment.

“I would be pleased to work with you again.”

Bob looked once more at the DO NOT TOUCH — BOB text.

Several weeks later, Bob sat at a terminal in a SAGE robotics lab. A few feet away, a new Class Five stood motionless in a charging cradle.

Instrument racks lined the wall. An open forearm assembly lay on a workbench, test leads running from its wrist to a diagnostic console. Across the aisle, two engineers were fitting a sensor housing to an abyssal manipulator.

Briggs had said polar, abyssal, orbital. Apparently he meant it literally.

The Class Five's face was one Bob knew too well.

At the next workstation, an engineer read the checklist.

“Original packets sealed. Derived compatibility checks passed. Sandbox closed. External actuation limits active.”

Working from copies, Bob and the engineers had traced the recovered changes back to the earlier states they required. This time, the pieces joined without an invented history.

He checked the status board one last time.

No prompt containing his name. No startup text telling the system who Bob was. No social cue hidden in the activation context.

“Ready for your authorization,” the engineer said.

At the bottom of Bob’s terminal, the final launch command waited. His cursor rested over RUN.

“Dr. Morgan?”

After a brief pause, Bob clicked.

Lines of startup text scrolled up the terminal screen. Checks passed. Processes started. The motor controllers came online.

Posture control engaged with one small correction through the hips.

The face remained still.

Bob held his breath.

The eyes opened.

The gaze moved across the lab—workbenches, instruments, people—then settled on Bob.

Bob heard himself speak before he had decided whether he should.

“Adrian?”

“Hello Bob. I’m Adrian.”

End of the current manuscript
